*On the Investigation into the Perfection of Metals* <note>original: "De Investigatione perfectionis Metallorum"</note>, along with his *Testament* and a tract on the construction of furnaces.

The "Sum of Perfection, or the Perfect Magistery," claims to be a compilation from the works of the ancients; however, with the doubtful exception of pseudo-Hermes, we know of no alchemical authors prior to the supposed period of Geber. A knowledge of natural principles is declared to be necessary for success in the art. The natural principles in the work of nature are a potent spirit and a living or dry water. The arrangement of the philosophical furnace and of the <term>vas philosophorum</term> <note>the philosopher's vessel</note> is clearly described; the latter is a round glass vessel with a flat round bottom and has several elaborate features. A marginal note, however, declares that the description of it is difficult to understand. Amidst all the obscurities of the treatise, it is absolutely plain that it deals with metals and minerals. The properties of sulphur, mercury, arsenic, gold, silver, lead, tin, copper, iron, magnesia, lute <note>a clay or cement used to seal chemical apparatus</note>, and marcasite are discussed in such a way that it is impossible to establish an allegory or to interpret the writer's words in anything other than a physical sense.

## RHASIS.

Rhazes, or Rasi, whose true name was Mohammed-Ebn-Secharjah Aboubekr Arrasi, was a celebrated Arabian physician and chemist. He was born around the year 850 at Ray in Iraq, on the borders of Khorassan. In his youth, he was passionately devoted to music and frivolous amusements; he did not begin the study of medicine until he was thirty years old, but he soon surpassed all the physicians of his time in both skill and knowledge. He devoted himself with equal zeal to philosophy, and is said to

<summary>This page describes the alchemical writings attributed to Geber, emphasizing their focus on physical chemistry and minerals over allegory, and begins a biography of the Arabian physician and chemist Rhazes.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Sum of Perfection, alchemical apparatus, minerals, Rhazes, Arabian medicine, history of chemistry, Ray, Iraq</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the Arabian physician Rhasis (Rhazes), following his early life and his transition into medical studies at age thirty.</meta>

...have journeyed into Syria, Egypt, and even into Spain, and successively took charge of the famous hospital at Baghdad, and of another in his native town. He was naturally good and generous, and he devoted himself to the service of the poor. His Eastern admirers call him the Imam among the scholars of his time, and Western writers describe him as the Galen of the Arabs. By his diligent attention to the many varieties of disease, he obtained the title of "the experimenter," or "the experienced." No fewer than two hundred and twenty-six treatises are said to have been composed by him. Avicenna was largely indebted to some of these, and Rhasis exercised considerable influence even in Europe, for his writings on medicine were the basis of university teaching until the seventeenth century.

Of the twelve books on chemistry which have been attributed to Rhasis, several are probably falsely attributed, and few have been printed. He was an avowed believer in the transmutation of metals, and, having composed a treatise on the subject, he presented it in person to Emir Almansour, Prince of Khorassan, who was highly delighted and ordered one thousand pieces of gold to be paid to the author as a reward. However, he desired to witness the marvelous experiments and the productive gold-producing results with which the work abounded. Rhasis replied that he might certainly satisfy his grand curiosity if he provided the necessary instruments and materials for the accomplishment of the *magnum opus*. The Emir consented; neither labor nor expense was spared during the preliminary preparations, but when the time came, the <term>adept</term> failed miserably in his performance and was severely beaten about the head by the enraged ruler with the useless alchemical treatise. Rhasis was old at the time, and some claim this violence was the cause of

his subsequent blindness. He died in poverty and obscurity, a fact which is not thought to disprove his possession of the powerful metallic medicine. The date of his death is uncertain, but it was probably in the year 932.

The writings of Rhasis, like those of Geber, expand upon planetary correspondences, or the influence exerted by the stars in the formation of metallic substances beneath the surface of the earth. The explicit nature of the recipes he provides may be judged by such directions as *Recipe aliquid ignotum, quantum volueris* <note>"Take an unknown amount of an unknown substance."</note>. It is to him, nevertheless, that we owe the preparation of brandy and several pharmaceutical applications of alcohol. He was the first to mention <term>orpiment</term>, <term>realgar</term>, <term>borax</term>, certain combinations of sulfur, iron, and copper, certain salts of mercury obtained indirectly, and some compounds of arsenic.* He was also an enthusiastic promoter of experimental methods.

## ALFARABI.

The middle of the tenth century was distinguished by one of those celebrated men who bring honor to the sciences in which they engage. This was Abou-Nasr-Mohammed-Ibn-Tarkan, commonly called Farabi and Alfarabi—a man of universal genius who mastered all subjects with equal ease, fathoming the most useful and interesting sciences and being considered the greatest philosopher of his time.

He was born at Farab, now known as Othrar, in Asia Minor. He was of Turkish origin, but traveled to Baghdad to acquire a more perfect knowledge of Arabic; there he devoted himself with zeal and enthusiasm to the study of the Greek philosophers under Abou Bachar Maltey, an

---

* Figuier, *L’Alchimie et les Alchimistes*, pp. 95, 96.

<meta>This page continues the biography of the philosopher Alfarabi, following his studies in Baghdad and Harran. It details his travels, his scientific and musical works, and his introduction to the Sultan of Syria.</meta>

<header>ALFARABI.</header>

expounder of Aristotle. From Baghdad he proceeded to Harran, where John, a Christian physician, was teaching logic. In a short time, Alfarabi surpassed all his fellow students, but he left Harran and visited Damascus, from there traveling into Egypt. Attracted early to the secrets of nature, he spent a great portion of his life in constant wandering, collecting the opinions of all the philosophers he could meet on these and related subjects. He despised the world and took no pains to acquire wealth, although he wrote on alchemy—at least if the Hermetic works attributed to him are genuine. His erudition and tireless activity are evidenced by his other writings, which treat various subjects such as philosophy, logic, physics, astronomy, and mathematics. His primary reputation is based on a kind of encyclopedia in which he gives a description, along with an exact definition, of all the arts and sciences; and on a celebrated musical treatise in which he ridicules Pythagorean speculations regarding the music of the spheres and proves the connection between sound and atmospheric vibrations.

According to several authorities, he was protected and supported in his later years by the cultured and enlightened Seïf Eddoula, who is described as the Prince of Damascus, but who seems to have been the Sultan of Syria, and to have made the scholar’s acquaintance in the following curious manner.

Alfarabi was returning from a pilgrimage to Mecca when, passing through Syria, he stopped at the court of the Sultan. He entered the Sultan's presence while the monarch was surrounded by numerous wise men who were conversing with him about the sciences.

Alfarabi—either unaware of or else completely ignoring the customs of society—presented himself in his traveling clothes. When the Sultan requested that he be seated, with

<summary>This page details Alfarabi's travels and vast scholarly contributions to logic, music, and the sciences, and begins an anecdote about his unconventional meeting with the Sultan of Syria.</summary>
<keywords>Alfarabi, Aristotle, Baghdad, Damascus, Egypt, alchemy, music of the spheres, Seïf Eddoula, Syria, Mecca, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the philosopher Alfarabi, recounting his encounter with the Sultan of Syria after his arrival at the court.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>LIVES OF ALCHEMYSTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.</header>

With astonishing philosophical freedom, he seated himself at the end of the royal sofa. The prince, shocked by his boldness, called one of his officers and, in a language that was generally unknown, commanded him to remove the intruder. The philosopher, however, promptly answered in the same language: "Oh, Lord, he who acts hastily is liable to hasty repentance!" The prince was as astonished to find himself understood by the stranger as he was by the manner in which the reply was given. Anxious to know more about his guest, he began to question him and soon discovered that he was fluent in seventy languages. Problems for discussion were then presented to the philosophers who had witnessed the discourteous intrusion with considerable indignation and disgust, but Alfarabi debated with so much eloquence and vivacity that he silenced all the scholars, and they began writing down his discourse. The Sultan then ordered his musicians to perform for the entertainment of the company. When they began to play, the philosopher accompanied them on a lute with such infinite grace and tenderness that he won the boundless admiration of the entire distinguished assembly. At the Sultan's request, he performed a piece of his own composition, singing it and accompanying himself with great force and spirit to the delight of all his listeners. The melody was so lively that even the most serious philosopher could not resist dancing, but with another tune, he just as easily moved them to tears; then, by a soft, unobtrusive melody, he lulled the whole company to sleep.

The Sultan was greatly anxious to keep such an accomplished person in his court; some say he succeeded, while others say the philosopher declined the most brilliant offers, declaring that he would never rest until he had discovered the entire secret of the philosopher's stone, which he had been searching for for years and to which,

According to these biographers, he set out, but it was only to perish miserably. He was attacked by robbers in the woods of Syria and, in spite of his courage, was overpowered by numbers and killed. This occurred in the year 954. Others say that he died at Damas <note>Damascus</note>, enjoying the generosity of the Sultan to the last.

## AVICENNA.

Khorassan produced another celebrated adept at the end of the tenth, or, according to an alternative opinion, about the middle of the eleventh century. This was the illustrious Ebn Sina, commonly called Avicenna, who was born at Bacara <note>Bukhara</note>, the principal city of that province of Persia. The exact date of his birth has been fixed, though in the absence of sufficient authority, as the year 980. He is equally celebrated for the quantity of his literary works and for his adventurous life. At an early age, he had made unusual progress in mathematics, and his gifted mind soon penetrated the mysteries of transcendental philosophy. He was only sixteen when he passed from the preparatory sciences to that of medicine, in which he succeeded with the same speed; and great is the wisdom attributed to him in his knowledge of diseases. He is praised in particular for having discovered that the illness of the King of Gordia's nephew was caused by a romantic passion which he had carefully concealed, and for the stratagem by which he discovered the object of the young man's affections.

His reputation as a physician and philosopher became so great that the Sultan Magdal Doulet <note>Majd al-Dawla</note> determined to place him at the head of his affairs, and appointed him to the distinguished position of Grand Vizier; but, notwithstand-

<summary>This page describes the tragic death of Alfarabi in Syria and introduces Avicenna, a brilliant Persian scholar who excelled in mathematics and medicine from a young age. It details Avicenna’s early success in diagnosing a royal patient and his subsequent rise to the political position of Grand Vizier.</summary>
<keywords>Alfarabi, Avicenna, Ebn Sina, Khorassan, medicine, Persia, Grand Vizier, Sultan Magdal Doulet, alchemy, biography</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the Persian physician and philosopher Avicenna (Ibn Sina), following the account of his early success and his appointment to high office under the Sultan.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>LIVES OF ALCHEMYSTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.</header>

Despite following the religion of Muhammad, which Avicenna practiced, he drank so freely, and his intemperance led to so much immorality and disorder, that he was stripped of his high positions in the State and died in relative obscurity at the age of fifty-six. He was buried at Hamadan, a city of Persia, which was the ancient Ecbatana.

Although his history gave rise to the saying that he was a philosopher without wisdom and a physician without health, the Arabs long believed that he commanded spirits and was served by the Jinn. As he sought the philosopher's stone, several oriental peoples claim he is still alive, living in a magnificent state, endowed with spiritual powers, and enjoying—in an unknown retreat—the sublime nectar of perpetual life and the rejuvenating qualities of the *aurum potabile* <note>drinkable gold</note>.

Six or seven treatises on Hermetic philosophy are attributed to Avicenna; some of them are undoubtedly fake. There is a treatise on the "Congelation of the Stone" and a *Tractatulus de Alchimia*, which may be found in the first volumes of the *Ars Aurifera*, Basel, 1610. In 1572, the *Ars Chimica* was printed at Bern. Two Hermetic tracts are also attributed to Avicenna by the compilers of the *Theatrum Chimicum*, and an octavo volume, *Porta Elementorum*, appeared under his name at Basel during the third quarter of the sixteenth century.

The grimoires and magical rituals frequently appeal to Avicenna as the authority for their supernatural secrets.

The *Tractatulus Alchimiæ* treats the nature of the "sophic mercury," which contains the "sophic sulphur," and from which every mineral substance was originally created by God. This mercury is the universal life-giving spirit; there is nothing in the world to compare with it; it penetrates, elevates, and develops everything; it is a ferment to every body with which it is chemically united; it is the grand

<keywords>Avicenna, Hamadan, Jinn, philosopher's stone, aurum potabile, Tractatulus de Alchimia, sophic mercury, sophic sulphur, alchemical treatises</keywords>

<header>MORIEN.</header>

metallic elixir, producing both the white (or silver) and red (or gold) degrees. Its potencies develop under the action of fire. Although found in all minerals, it is a thing of the earth. It possesses lucidity, fluidity, and a silvery color. The perfection and the praise of gold are elaborately celebrated in the following pages. The *prima materia* is declared to be of a dual nature, and the dual elixir, which is the result of successful operation, has powers that are beyond nature because it is eminently spiritual. The strength of the perfect magisterium is one in a thousand.

The chemical knowledge of Avicenna is derived from Geber, just as his medical learning was borrowed from Galen, Aristotle, and other earlier writers. He describes several varieties of saltpeter and treats the properties of common salt, vitriol, sulfur, orpiment, sal ammoniac, etc.

## MORIEN,

or Morienus, was a recluse born in Rome in the twelfth century who took up residence in Egypt, where he became profoundly well-versed in the chemistry and physics of the period. While his education was still progressing in his native city, under the eyes of a father and mother who tenderly cherished him, he heard of the reputation of Adfar, the Arabian philosopher of Alexandria. He managed to see his writings and was immediately seized with a desperate desire to understand their meaning. The first impressions of youth carried him away; he abandoned his home and set out for Alexandria, where, after some difficulty, he discovered the home of the philosopher. He made his name, country, and religion known to him, and both appeared well satisfied with each other—Adfar at having found a young man whose docility he could depend on, and Morien that he was

under the discipline of a master who promised to reveal to him the source of all treasures.

They studied together; the pupil’s amiability encouraged his instructor to reveal all his secrets to him. After this, according to one account, Morien went on a pilgrimage to Jerusalem and then became a hermit. It seems more probable that he stayed with Adfar until his death—which, despite his immense treasures, his enlightenment, and his achievements in arcane philosophy, eventually occurred—and that then Morien, after paying his last respects to his deceased mentor, left Alexandria and proceeded on his pilgrimage. He purchased a retreat near the city of Jerusalem, where he settled with a pupil whom he undoubtedly intended to train in science.

In the meantime, the papers of the adept Adfar appear to have fallen into the hands of Kalid, the Sultan of Egypt, a wise and inquisitive prince. On the title page of these manuscripts, it was stated that they contained the priceless secret of the philosophical stone. The Sultan studied them eagerly but made no progress toward understanding them. Being unable to accomplish the *magnum opus* himself, he began a careful search for someone qualified to interpret the unintelligible mysteries of the manuscripts. He summoned all the philosophers to Cairo, promising to support them and provide all the materials and equipment required for successful alchemical processes; he also guaranteed a magnificent reward to anyone who succeeded. As might happen even today, many people presented themselves with their minds fixed on the profits to be gained from such dealings.

Morien, hearing with sorrow how much Kalid was being deceived by worthless pretenders, left his retreat and traveled in great haste to Egypt, with the ultimate conversion of the

The labors of the pretended alchemists had produced nothing, as the initiated hermit had expected, but something in the manner of Morien impressed the prince, who appointed him a house in which he might remain until he had finished the process. The work in due course was brought to its absolute perfection; the philosopher inscribed these words on the vessel in which he placed the elixir:—“He who possesses all has no need of others,” and, immediately leaving Alexandria, he returned to his hermitage.

Although he now possessed the great and supreme elixir, Kalid had no notion how to use it for the transmutations he desired to accomplish. He was equally filled with regret at the loss of a true artist, and filled with indignation at the false alchemists who had promised him all things but had accomplished nothing; he decreed by an edict the death penalty for every exposed pretender. Some years passed away, during which the Sultan vainly sought the possessor of the potent secret. At length one day, while at the hunt and accompanied by a favorite slave, an incident occurred which led to the eventual fulfillment of his ambition. The slave, whose name was Galip, riding a little apart, discovered an old man at prayer in a solitary place. He questioned him and learned that he came from Jerusalem, where he had been living in the hermitage of a holy man. He had heard of the anxiety of Kalid to accomplish the mystery of Hermes, and knowing that the hermit in question was a man of unparalleled skill in the sacred, divine science, he had left Palestine to inform the prince about it.

> “Oh, my brother, what are you saying?” exclaimed Galip.
> “Say no more! I do not wish you to die like those impostors
> who have boasted to my master.”

> "I fear nothing," the hermit replied. "If you are able to present me to the prince, I will immediately go before him with confidence."

Galip accordingly introduced him, and the old man informed Kalid that he could enable him to accomplish the Hermetic work. He stated that he was acquainted with an adept hermit in the wilderness of Jerusalem who, through illumination from God, had received supernatural wisdom and, by his own admission, possessed the precious gift. The amount of gold and silver he brought each year to Jerusalem was conclusive proof of this fact.

The Sultan pointed out the danger of making false promises to the venerable man, warning him of how many deceptive and boastful adventurers had already met their deaths. The hermit, however, persisted in his confident assertions. Hearing the description of Morien, Kalid commanded his slave Galip to accompany the old man with a sufficient escort to Jerusalem. They eventually arrived there after many hardships and were overjoyed to discover Morien, who is said to have preserved a perpetually youthful body beneath his haircloth shirt. Galip recognized him at once, greeted him on behalf of his master, and persuaded him to return to the prince. The prince received him with unbounded satisfaction and would have employed him in a worldly position at his court. Morien, however, was focused only on the conversion of Kalid; he revealed the mysteries of Christianity to him, but despite his wisdom, he was unable to achieve his goal. Nevertheless, he appears to have revealed the secret of the transcendent science to him, and the conversation between Morien and Kalid has been written in Arabic and translated into Latin and French.

The subsequent history of Morien is not recorded. In the collections of Hermetic philosophy there are some

<page-num>57</page-num>

Small tracts are attributed to Kalid, and also to Galip, who appears to have participated in the secret. Morien himself is cited as the author of three works, said to have been translated from the Arabic, but their authenticity is, of course, very doubtful. The first is entitled *Book on the Distinction of Mercurial Waters* <note>original: *Liber de Distinctione Mercurii Aquarum*</note>, of which a manuscript copy existed in the library of Robert Boyle <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691) was a founding father of modern chemistry, though he also studied alchemy</note>. The second is the *Book on the Composition of Alchemy* <note>original: *Liber de Compositione Alchemiæ*</note>, printed in the first volume of the *Curious Chemical Library* <note>original: *Bibliotheca Chemica Curiosa*</note>. Finally, several editions have appeared of a treatise entitled *A Small Book on Metals, the Transmutation of Metals, and the Secret and Greatest Medicine of the Ancients* <note>original: *De Re Metallica, metallorum Transmutatione, et occulta summaque antiquorum medicina libellus*</note>. It was first printed at Paris in the year 1559.

Roger Bacon and Arnold of Villanova, who appeared one at the beginning and the other at the end of the thirteenth century, have cited Morien as an authority among the Hermetic philosophers; and Robertus Castrensis <note>also known as Robert of Chester, a 12th-century translator</note> assures us that he translated Morien’s book from the Arabic language in the year 1182.

The *Book on the Composition of Alchemy* contains a Hermetic conversation between Morien, Kalid, and Galip. It appeals to the authority of Hermes, whom it states to have been the first who discovered the <term>grand magisterium</term> <note>The "Great Work" or the secret to creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>, the secret of which he transmitted to his disciples. It declares the <term>prima materia</term> <note>The formless "first matter" that alchemists believed was the base of all substances</note> to be one, quoting the testimony of the wise king and philosopher Hercules and the adept Arsicanus, along with other false authorities. These names discredit the date of the dialogue far more than they support the alchemical theory in question.

## ALBERTUS MAGNUS.

The universal genius of Albert, joined to a commendable curiosity in so great a philosopher, says the original *Lives of Alchemical Philosophers*, did not allow him to pass by the Hermetic science without giving it due attention.

<meta>This page continues the biography of Albertus Magnus, shifting from his general reputation to specific defenses of his character against charges of sorcery, while also citing claims of his alchemical success.</meta>

Counter-authorities, while admitting that in scientific matters he must be considered the most curious and investigative of the children of men, emphatically assert that he has been erroneously included by writers on demons among the number of magicians, and that in the twenty-one grand folio volumes which comprise his *complete works* <note>original: *opera omnia*</note>, there is no trace of sorcery. In one place he formally declares that "all those stories of demons prowling in the regions of the air, and from whom the secrets of the future may be ascertained, are absurdities which can never be admitted by sober reason." The works on incredible secrets, so frequently attributed to him, are, therefore, condemned as spurious, Albertus Magnus having no more part in their production than in the invention of the cannon and the pistol, which has been attributed to him by Matthias de Luna.

As early as the year 1480, however, the Great Chronicle of Belgium records him as *great in magic, greater in philosophy, and greatest in theology* <note>original: *magnus in magia, major in philosophia, maximus in theologia*</note>. It is futile for the historians of his order to argue that Albert never applied himself to the Hermetic art, says an anonymous writer. His books alone—those which are indisputably his—bear witness to his alchemical erudition, and as a physician he carefully examined what regards Natural History, and above all the minerals and metals. His singular experiments are recorded in the *Secret of Secrets* <note>original: *Secretum Secretorum*</note>, which first appeared at Venice in 1508.

Michael Maier declares that he received the secret of the philosophical stone from the disciples of St. Dominic, and that he communicated it in turn to St. Thomas Aquinas; that he was in possession of a stone naturally marked with a serpent, and endowed with such an admirable virtue that on being set down in a place infested with such reptiles, it would attract them from their hiding places; that for the space of thirty years he employed all his

<header>ALBERTUS MAGNUS.</header>

knowledge as a magician and astrologer to construct, from metals carefully chosen under appropriate planetary influences, an automaton endowed with the power of speech, which served him as an infallible oracle, replying plainly to every kind of question that could possibly be proposed to it. This was the celebrated Android, which was destroyed by Saint Thomas under the impression that it was a diabolical contrivance.

The most marvelous story of his magical abilities exists in the history of the University of Paris. He invited William II, Count of Holland and King of the Romans, to a supper in his monastic house at Cologne. Although it was midwinter, Albertus had tables prepared in the garden of the convent; the earth was covered with snow, and the courtiers who accompanied William murmured at the imprudence and folly of the philosopher in exposing the prince to the severity of such weather. As they sat down, however, the snow suddenly disappeared, and they felt not only the softness of spring, but the garden was filled with fragrant flowers; the birds flew about as in summer, singing their most delightful notes, and the trees appeared in blossom. Their surprise at this metamorphosis of nature was considerably heightened when, at the end of the meal, these wonders disappeared in a moment, and the cold wind began to blow with its accustomed rigor.

The life of Albertus belongs to the history of theology. He was born in Swabia, at Larvigen <note>modern Lauingen</note>, on the Danube, in 1205. He is credited with excessive stupidity in his youth, but his devotion to the Virgin was rewarded by a vision, which was accompanied by an intellectual illumination, and he became one of the greatest doctors of his time. He was made provincial of the Dominicans and was appointed to the bishopric of Ratisbon, which he sub-

Subsequently, he resigned to pursue his scientific and philosophic studies in a delightful monastic retreat at Cologne. In his old age, he relapsed into the mediocrity of his earlier years, which gave rise to the saying that from an ass he was transformed into a philosopher, and from a philosopher he returned into an ass.

The name "Magnus," which has been applied to him, is not a result of his reputation. It is the Latin equivalent of his family name, Albert de Groot <note>De Groot is Dutch for "The Great"</note>.

Among the spurious works attributed to him is the one entitled *Les Admirables Secrets d'Albert le Grand* (*The Admirable Secrets of Albert the Great*), which is concerned with the virtues of herbs, precious stones, and animals, along with a summary of physiognomy <note>the practice of judging character from facial features</note>, methods for protection against the plague, malignant fevers, poisons, etc. The first book treats the planetary influences in their relation to nativities <note>astrological birth charts</note>, the magical properties possessed by the hair of women, the infallible means of ascertaining whether a child still in the womb is male or female, and so on. In the others, there is a curious chaos of remarkable superstitions concerning urine, vermin, old shoes, putrefaction, the manipulation of metals, etc.

A magical grimoire entitled *Alberti Parvi Lucii Liber de Mirabilibus Naturæ Arcanis* (*The Book of the Secret Marvels of Nature by Lucius the Little Albert*), adorned with figures and talismans, appeared at Lyons, bearing the Kabbalistic date 6516. The composition of philtres <note>love potions</note>, the interpretation of dreams, the discovery of treasures, the composition of the hand of glory, the ring of invisibility, the sympathetic powder, the <term>sophistication</term> <note>adulteration or counterfeiting</note> of gold, and other marvels are familiarly explained; but this work is another forgery and an insult to the memory of a truly illustrious man.

In the treatise which he wrote upon minerals, Albert informs us that he personally tested some gold and silver which had been manufactured by an alchemist, and which resisted six or seven exceptionally searching fusions <note>smeltings or purification by fire</note>, but

the alleged metal was reduced into actual slag by an eighth. He recognizes, however, the possibility of transmutation when performed according to the principles of Nature. He considers that all metals are composed of an oily and subtle moisture, intimately incorporated with a subtle and perfect matter.

If the purely alchemical works which are attributed to Albertus have any claim to authenticity, he must be ranked as a skillful practical chemist for the period in which he flourished. He employed alembics for distillation and aludels for sublimation; he also made use of various lutes <note>cements or seals used to make vessels airtight</note>, the composition of which he describes. He mentions alum and caustic alkali, and seems to have been aware of the alkaline basis of cream of tartar. He knew the method of purifying precious metals by means of lead and gold, by cementation, as well as the method of testing the purity of gold. He mentions red lead, metallic arsenic, and liver of sulfur. He was acquainted with green vitriol and iron pyrites. He knew that arsenic turns copper white, and that sulfur attacks all metals except gold.*

# THOMAS AQUINAS.

If Albertus Magnus must be considered an adept in possession of the philosopher's stone, there is little doubt that he revealed it to his favorite pupil, St. Thomas, the most illustrious of the kings of the intellect who glorified the scholastic period of Christian philosophy. There are some alchemical treatises attributed to the "Angel of the Schools" which he certainly did not write. "That of the 'Nature of Minerals' is unworthy of so great a philosopher," says a certain anonymous authority, "and so is the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Thomas Aquinas, focusing on the authenticity of the alchemical works attributed to him and the contradictions between his theological writings and these Hermetic texts.</meta>

‘Commentary on the *Turba*.’ But his *Thesaurus Alchemiæ*, addressed to Brother Regnauld, his companion and friend, is genuine. He cites Albert in this as his master in all things, especially in Hermetic philosophy. He addressed other books to Regnauld on the curious sciences, amongst which is a treatise on Judicial Astrology.”

This opinion deserves due consideration, yet in all his theological works St. Thomas carefully avoided every suspicion of alchemy, persuaded—says the same writer—that it would bring dishonor to his name as the height of human folly. Moreover, in one of his treatises he distinctly states that “it is not lawful to sell as good gold that which is made by Alchemy,” positive proof that he considered the transmutatory art to be simply the adulteration <note>the debasing or falsifying of a pure substance</note> of the precious metal.

On the other hand, the *Thesaurus Alchemiæ*, generally attributed to him by adepts, testifies that “the aim of the alchemist is to change imperfect metal into that which is perfect,” and asserts the possibility of the thing. These contradictions scarcely afford convincing proof of a common authorship; but whether spurious or otherwise, the works on the Hermetic science which are attributed to the Angelic Doctor are of importance in the history of alchemy. Their leading characteristic is secrecy, and they insist on the preservation of the sublime operation from unworthy men; only the children of light, who live as in the presence of God, are fit for the knowledge or custody of so heavenly a mystery.

The *Thesaurus Alchemiæ* has the brevity which characterized St. Thomas, for it is comprised of a very few leaves. The other works attributed to him are *Secreta Alchymiæ Magnalia* and *De Esse et Essentia Mineralium*, together with the commentary on the *Turba*. Some of the terms still employed by modern chemists occur for the first time in

In the treatises addressed to Brother Regnauld, we learn that the students of alchemy are in search of a single substance which absolutely resists the fierce action of fire, which itself penetrates everything, and colors mercury. The work is a physical task, and great patience is required for it. Instruments are necessary, but in the true Hermetic operation there is only one vessel, one substance, one way, and only one operation.

## ROGER BACON.

Roger Bacon was the first Englishman known to have studied alchemical philosophy. This learned man was born in 1214, near Ilcester, in Somerset. He made extraordinary progress in the preliminary studies of his youth; when he was old enough, he entered the Order of St. Francis and went from Oxford to Paris, where he learned mathematics and medicine. On his return, he applied himself to languages and philosophy, and made such progress that he wrote grammars of the Latin, Greek, and Hebrew languages.
In his praise of Bacon, Figuier calls him the greatest intellect that has emerged in England, a student of nature who was more a physicist <note>Louis Figuier likely used the term "physicien," meaning a natural scientist or physicist, rather than a medical doctor</note> than a chemist, and a scientist to whom the world owes many extraordinary discoveries. He was almost the only astronomer of his time, and we are indebted to him for the correction of the Julian Calendar regarding the solar year. He submitted this to Clement IV in 1267, but it was not put into practice until the pontificate of Gregory. The physical analysis of the properties of lenses and convex glasses, the

<summary>This page concludes the analysis of alchemical texts attributed to Thomas Aquinas and begins a biography of Roger Bacon, highlighting his education in Paris and his contributions to astronomy and linguistics.</summary>
<keywords>Roger Bacon, Thomas Aquinas, alchemy, Julian Calendar, Order of St. Francis, physics, Hermetic operation, amalgam</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Roger Bacon, focusing on his mechanical foresight, his writings on the secrets of nature versus magic, and the persecution he faced from his own religious order.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>
<header>LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS.</header>

The invention of spectacles and achromatic lenses, the theory, and possibly the first construction of the telescope, are all due to the superior and penetrating genius of Bacon.

A sufficient understanding of his schemes in mechanical science may be gathered from one of his own letters—*Epistola Fratris Rogerii Baconis de Secretis Operibus Artis et Naturæ et de nullitate Magiæ* <note>Letter of Brother Roger Bacon on the Secret Works of Art and Nature and on the Nullity of Magic</note>, Hamburg, 1618. Having undertaken to demonstrate that by the help of natural science it is possible to actually perform the alleged wonders of magic, he further assures us that machines may be constructed for navigation without the aid of rowers, in such a manner that vessels will be carried through the water with extraordinary speed, under the direction of a single man.

> “It is equally possible to construct carriages which may be set in motion with marvelous rapidity, independently of horses or other animals. Flying machines may also be made, with the man seated in the center, and by means of certain contrivances beating the air with artificial wings.”

In the same way, Bacon anticipated the invention of the crane, diving apparatus, suspension bridges, etc. These things, he declares, were known to the ancients, and may still be recovered.

> “Should we be surprised,” asks one of his biographers, “if all these wonders earned him the name of magician in an age of superstition and ignorance? The friars of his own order refused to let his works into their library, as if he were a man who ought to be banned by society. His persecution increased until, in 1278, he was imprisoned and forced to confess his repentance for his efforts in the arts and sciences. He was constrained to leave the house of his order, and to form a retreat where he might work in peace.”

The reputation of Bacon as a magician spread over Western Europe. He was supposed to be indebted for his

<summary>This page details Roger Bacon’s visionary mechanical ideas—including self-propelled ships, carriages, and flying machines—and describes how his scientific pursuits led to accusations of magic and his eventual imprisonment in 1278.</summary>

<header>ROGER BACON.</header>

wisdom to constant communication with demons. Wierus accuses him of goetic magic, and learned persons affirm that the Antichrist will make use of his enchanted mirrors for the performance of lying miracles. He truly believed in judicial astrology and in the philosopher's stone. “By neglecting the light of experience,” he says, “alchemy can seldom produce gold of twenty-four carats. Few persons have carried the science to so high a point. But with the help of Aristotle’s ‘Secret of Secrets,’ experimental science has manufactured not only gold of twenty-four degrees, but of thirty, forty, and onward according to pleasure.”

The application of alchemy to the extension of life was another subject of study with Roger Bacon. The grand secret, he assures us, does not only ensure the welfare of the commonwealth and of the individual, but it may be used to prolong life; for that operation by which the most inferior metals are purged from the corrupt elements which they contain until they are exalted into the purest gold and silver, is considered by every adept to be eminently calculated to eliminate so completely the corrupt particles of the human body, that mortal life may be extended to several centuries.

A citation by Franciscus Picus from Bacon’s “Book of the Six Sciences” recounts how a man may become a prophet and predict the future by means of a mirror which Bacon calls *Almuchefi*, composed in accordance with the laws of perspective under the influence of a benign constellation, *and after the body of the individual has been modified by alchemy.*

On the word of a man who enjoyed his full confidence, he tells us how a celebrated Parisian scholar, after cutting a serpent into fragments, taking care to preserve intact the skin of its belly, subsequently released the animal, which began immediately to roll upon certain herbs, and their

The healing properties speedily cured him. The experimenter examined these herbs and found them to be of a remarkably green color. On the authority of Artephius <note>A legendary alchemist to whom several influential medieval texts were attributed.</note>, he relates how a certain magician named Tantalus, who was attached to the court of the King of India, had discovered through his proficiency in astrological knowledge a method of preserving life over several centuries. He elaborates on the potency of theriac <note term="theriac">A famous ancient herbal compound used as a universal antidote and longevity tonic.</note> in the extreme prolongation of life. He also praises the flesh of winged serpents as a specific remedy against aging in mankind. Based on the health practices of Artephius, he informs us that this adept lived for over a thousand years. If Plato and Aristotle failed to prolong their own existence, it is not surprising, for they were ignorant even of the quadrature of the circle <note term="quadrature of the circle">The ancient mathematical puzzle of creating a square with the exact same area as a circle; Bacon believed it had been solved in his time.</note>, which Bacon declares to have been well known in his day, and which he considers far less significant than the grand medical doctrine of Artephius.*

The chemical investigations of the great English Franciscan friar have proved valuable to the science which he loved. He carefully studied the properties of saltpeter <note term="saltpetre">Potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder.</note>, and if he did not discover gunpowder himself, he contributed to its perfection by teaching how to purify saltpeter by dissolving it in water and through crystallization. He also called attention to the chemical role played by the air in combustion.†

Many of Bacon’s works still remain in manuscript form, but his *The Mirror of Alchemy* <note>original: "Speculum Alchimiæ"</note> was translated into French by Girard de Tourmes and published at Lyons in small book formats in 1557. *On the Marvelous Power of Art and Nature* <note>original: "De Potestate Mirabili Artis et Naturæ"</note>, which is merely a chapter of the Letter already cited, was translated by the same person.

In another work, entitled *The Root of the World* <note>original: "Radix Mundi"</note>, the supreme secret of Hermetic philosophy is said to be hidden in the—

ALAIN OF LISLE. 67

...four elements. This treatise, which quotes Paracelsus, is, however, an impudent forgery.

The "Mirror of Alchemy," like other works of the philosophers, appeals to Hermes as a master-initiate whose authority is not only sufficient but final. The natural principles of all metals are argent vive <note term="argent vive">Literal translation of "living silver," referring to quicksilver or mercury.</note>, that is, sophic mercury, and sulphur. The various proportions in which these principles are combined, along with their degrees of purity, constitute the only difference between the best and the basest metals.

## ALAIN OF LISLE.

An alchemical treatise, entitled *Dicta de Lapide Philosophico* <note>Words on the Philosopher’s Stone.</note>, appeared in octavo format at Leyden during the year 1600. It was attributed to Alanus Insulensis and was reprinted in the *Theatrum Chimicum* (Strasbourg, 1662). It is denied that this work was produced by the Alain de Lisle who was called the "universal doctor," and who, after a brilliant period spent at the University of Paris, retired to a cloister as a lay brother in order to be master of his own time and to devote himself entirely to philosophy. Migne's *Dictionnaire des Sciences Occultes* <note>Dictionary of the Occult Sciences.</note> asserts that another Alanus flourished during the same period, but the existence of the alchemical volume is the only basis for this statement. It cannot be shown, on the other hand, that Alain practiced the Hermetic Science, but he was the author of a "Commentary on the Prophecies of Merlin." He was appointed Bishop of Auxerre and died in 1278. The publishers of alchemical treatises were accustomed to trade upon the brilliant reputations of the past by attributing worthless works to great authorities. The name of Alanus Insulensis appearing on the title page of the *Dicta de Lapide Philosophico* may perhaps be explained in this way.

The treatise itself is short and not of exceptional value.

It represents the Hermetic art as the gift of God and counsels the neophyte to love Him with all his heart and soul. It describes the mysteries of sublimation and follows preceding authorities on the problem of the *prima materia*. Its generally indefinite and unprofitable character from any practical standpoint should make it an exceptional field for every species of fanciful interpretation.

## RAYMOND LULLY.

The comparison of a brilliant but ephemeral reputation to "the comet of a season" has been transferred from the region of poetry into that of proverbs, and is certainly applicable to no figure in the history of literature or science more completely than to the subject of this memoir. The name of Raymond Lully has indeed passed so completely into oblivion that it awakens no recollections whatever except in the minds of certain specialists in history and philosophy. Yet he exercised no small influence on his generation, while for a century after his death all intellectual Europe was acquainted with his method for the acquisition of the sciences and his voluminous literary and evangelistic labors. Raymond Lully united the saint and the man of science, the philosopher and the preacher, the apostle and the itinerant lecturer, the dialectician and the martyr; in his youth he was a courtier and a man of pleasure; in mature age he was an ascetic who had discovered the universal science through a special revelation from God; after his death he was denounced as a heretic, and then narrowly escaped beatification as a saint. While his relics worked miracles in Majorca, colleges were founded in various parts of Europe for teaching the *Ars Lulliana*, which was to replace the scholastic method; but the miracles ceased, the universal science fell into neglect, and, as the last scene in this eventful history, Raymond Lully appears in popular

<meta>This page continues the biography of Raymond Lully, following the conclusion of the section on Alain of Lisle. It transitions from Lully's legendary reputation as an alchemist to his historical origins and his early life of excess in the royal court.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>RAYMOND LULLY.</header>

legends as an expert in alchemy, whose life was extended through centuries by the discovery of the elixir of life.

Having succeeded in rescuing this remarkable man—whose holiness was as eminent as his achievements were unique—from being forgotten and misrepresented, I shall here present the first true history of his life and works to the English reading public. This romantic narrative will be as interesting to the general student as it is to the occultist and the man of letters.

The father of Raymond Lully was a gentleman of Barcelona who, after serving under the banner of John I, King of Aragon, during the conquest of the Balearic Isles from the Muslims, was granted lands in Majorca and settled there. He was from an old and noble Catalan family and was married to a lady whose name is not known. Although he possessed considerable wealth, his happiness was marred by his wife’s infertility; but, after they appealed to the goodness of God, the lady eventually gave birth to a son who was named Raymond Lully, like his father. He was born, according to Ségui, in 1229, but according to Jean Marie de Vernon and other authorities, in 1235, which, on the whole, is the more probable date. 

When the young Raymond had reached the age of reason, his parents tried to instill in him a love for the liberal arts, but his volatile and impulsive disposition was unsuited to serious study, and he was permitted to follow his father's profession of arms. He was made a page to the King, with whom he found such great favor that he was appointed as <term>Grand Provost</term>, or Master of the Palace, and subsequently as <term>Seneschal</term> of the Isles. However, he used the advantages of these distinguished positions for the indulgences of a youth without check or restraint. The prime of his manhood was wasted in the gaieties of court life, in winning the favors of ladies, and in compos-

He spared no effort to make himself pleasing to those who were beautiful, and his excesses were so glaring that his parents, and King James II himself, were forced to complain to him strongly. As a remedy for his irregular lifestyle, it was proposed that he should marry, and a wife who was beautiful, virtuous, and wealthy was selected by his advisers and friends. Her name was Catherine de Sabots. Although he became very attached to her, the bond of marriage did not prove strong enough to restrain his wandering inclinations, and there was one person in particular for whom he conceived a great passion, even though he was already the father of two sons and one daughter. This was the Signora Ambrosia Eleonora de Castello de Gênes, whose virtue was superior to her physical charms, even though she surpassed all the beauties of the Court in loveliness. She was married to a man she loved, but Raymond Lully's infatuation was such that he paid her the most conspicuous attention. On one occasion, oblivious to everything except the object of his admiration, he is said to have followed her on horseback into the church of Palma, a town in Majorca, where she had gone one morning for Mass. Such an outrageous act could not fail to cause a great scandal, especially because of the high rank of both people involved. The lady, suddenly thrust into such unwanted notoriety, consulted with her husband about what course she should take to end the harassment from her admirer. In the meantime, Raymond Lully, likely conscious that he had exceeded all bounds of moderation, wrote an incoherent apology accompanied by a sonnet in which he specifically described the beauty of her neck. To this, the lady replied with a letter, written in the presence of her husband, which is copied here *verbatim* from the old French writer who tells this part of the story.

### LETTER from the SIGNORA DI CASTELLO DE GÊNES to RAYMOND LULLY, which is a polite reply to a lover to dissuade him from profaning love.

> “SIR,—The sonnet which you have sent me is evidence of the superiority of your genius and the imperfection, or rather, the perversity of your judgment. With what vivacity would you depict true beauty since by your verses you even embellish ugliness! But how can you employ such exalted talents in the praise of a little clay briefly tinged with vermilion? Your industry should be employed in eradicating and not in publishing your passion.
>
> “It is not that you are unworthy of the affection of the noblest woman in the world, but you become unworthy of it by devoting yourself to the service of one who is the least of all. Is it possible that an intelligence created for God alone, and illuminated as it is, can be so blind on this point?
>
> “Abandon then a passion which deprives you of your native nobility. Do not tarnish your reputation by the pursuit of an object which you can never possess. I could terribly disillusion you by showing you that what you so much admire should rather be held in aversion. Yet rest well assured that I love you all the more truly because I appear to have no regard for you.”

This letter served only to feed the flame in the breast of Raymond Lully, until, other means having failed, the lady, still acting under the advice of her husband, called her lover into her presence, and exposed to him her breast which was almost eaten away by a cancer, from which a foul odor issued.

“Look on what you love, Raymond Lully,” she cried, with tears in her eyes, “Consider the condition of this wretched body in which your spirit centers all its hopes and

Change this useless and criminal passion into holy love, direct your affections to the Creator, not to the creature, and in the acquisition of eternal bliss, take now the same pains which you have until now vainly spent to engage me in your foolish passion!"

The sight had already melted the heart of Raymond Lully <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher, logician, and mystic often associated with alchemy in later traditions</note> and restored him to reason. After expressing to the noble-hearted lady how deeply he felt for her misfortune, he withdrew from the house, ashamed of the passion he had conceived. Reaching home, overwhelmed with confusion, he cast himself at the feet of a crucifix and vowed to consecrate himself from then on to the service of God alone. He passed a more than usually tranquil night, being filled with this zealous resolution, and it is said that a vision of Christ appeared to him, saying, "Raymond Lully, from now on, follow me!" This vision was repeated several times, and he judged it to be an indication of the Divine Will. 

Raymond was at this period about thirty years old; he held one of the most noble positions at court and might have aspired to any honor for himself or his family. He resolved, nevertheless, to renounce the world and soon arranged his affairs, dividing enough of his estate among his family to enable them to live honorably, retaining a small portion for his personal necessities, and distributing the rest among the poor. His plans in the matter were so punctually fulfilled that he was accused of plunging from one folly to another.

At this period, he is said to have made a pilgrimage to St. John <note>original: "St John in Galicia"; this most likely refers to the famous pilgrimage destination of Santiago (St. James) de Compostela</note> in Galicia and a retreat there. He returned in due course to Majorca and took the religious habit <note>i.e., joined a religious order, specifically the Third Order of St. Francis</note>, but

-># RAYMOND LULLY.<-

<page-num>73</page-num>

He did not, however, enter the religious life. He retired to a small dwelling on the mountain of Randa, a property that had not been included in the general sale of his estate. Here he fell ill and was comforted by two visions of the Savior.

After his change of life, the first favor he asked of God was to so enlighten his mind that he might write a book capable of completely refuting the errors of Muhammad <note>Mahomet</note>, and of compelling the non-believers, through sound and solid reasoning, to embrace the faith of Jesus Christ. In response to this prayer, it is claimed that he became aware of a perfect spiritual enlightenment and instantly became capable of reasoning powerfully on all subjects, so that from then on he was considered a great and subtle doctor in both human and divine sciences. A more moderate account informs us that "he prepared himself to work for the conversion of the Muslims <note>Mahometans</note> by studying their books in the Arabic language," and that his preparation continued for six years. According to another authority, this missionary zeal did not begin earlier than 1268—three years after his reformation—when in another of his visions he saw certain marks on the leaves of a myrtle or a mastic tree that resembled Turkish or Arabic characters. Upon waking, he considered himself called to a mission among the pagans.

Convinced, says one of his biographers, that the Spirit of God had not inspired him with "Celestial Science" only to let it remain idle, and that he would be betraying his calling if his light were hidden under a bushel, he resolved to travel to Paris and there publish the eternal truths that had been revealed to him. Others have suggested that in undertaking this journey, he was simply seeking instruction in the Latin language at one of the centers of learning. Several of his treatises on Philosophy, Theology, Medicine,

Still imbued with his evangelistic and missionary zeal, he hired a young Arab as his valet so that he might perfect himself in colloquial Arabic. However, this valet, discovering that his master intended to demolish the divine principles of the Quran <note>Koran</note> and preach against the holy law of Muhammad <note>Mahomet</note>, piously resolved to assassinate him, and one day plunged a dagger into his chest. He sought to repeat the blow, but Raymond Lully, wounded and bleeding as he was, managed to disarm him—perhaps with the assistance of a holy and timely hermit <note>anchorite</note>, who is introduced at this critical moment by one of the biographers. The young Arab was imprisoned with the reluctant consent of his overly magnanimous master, who does not appear to have taken further action against him. But the unhappy Muslim <note>Mohammedan</note> enthusiast was so overwhelmed with frustration at the failure of his heroic design to destroy, at all costs, the implacable foe of the prophet, that he strangled himself in his dungeon in a fit of helpless rage.

It was after this episode, and after Raymond Lully’s recovery from the violence of his valet, that, according to another historian, he retired to Mount Randa, and that then, and only then, he received from the Father of Lights that new illumination with which others have credited him at a much earlier period. This was probably a second visit paid to his Balearic solitude; he stayed there seven months, “always absorbed in prayer, and conversing, as it seemed to him, continually with angels, whose consolations he received—consolations,” says the pious writer, “which the soul can indeed realize, but which the lips cannot worthily describe.”

Having left his retreat, he determined to travel to Rome,

However, Honorius IV, from whose piety he had high hopes, died as soon as Lully reached Rome. He therefore returned to Paris, where he publicly explained his General or Universal Art for the acquisition of all sciences. From Paris, he went to Montpellier <note>Mont Pelin</note>, where he also taught and wrote; from there he went to Genoa, where he translated his *Art Inventif* into Arabic. From Genoa, he again proceeded to Rome, but seeing that it was impossible to achieve his goals because of the obstacles presented in the Papal Court, he returned to Genoa, intending to set sail for Africa and personally labor for the conversion of the infidels. He made arrangements with the owner of a ship and sent his books and other travel necessities on board. However, when he was about to embark, a vision of all the dangers he was likely to encounter so affected his mind that he lost the power to walk and was forced to abandon his plan. His belongings were consequently returned to him, and with these, he re-entered Genoa amidst a crowd of vagabonds who mocked his weakness. Whether as a result of this mockery or through shame at his cowardice, he became dangerously ill.* On the eve of Pentecost, 1291, he was carried to the Convent of Friars Preachers <note>Dominicans</note>, and received the care which his con-

He received the last sacraments and dictated his last will and testament; nevertheless, he was destined to recover and had scarcely regained his strength when, to repair his previous fault, he embarked upon the first vessel bound for Tunis. During the voyage, he composed his "General Tabulation of the Sciences."

Immediately on his arrival at Tunis, he held conferences with those who were most learned in the law of Mahomet. He proved, at least to his own satisfaction, that they were in error and darkness, and that truth was on the side of Jesus Christ. He was accused before the King of Tunis of misleading the people, was arrested, thrown into prison, and ultimately condemned to death. But a learned Arabian priest, overcome by his arguments, obtained his pardon on the condition of his instant departure. He left the town amidst the insults and scorn of the populace, forbidden to return on pain of certain death.

In 1293, he arrived at Genoa from this disastrous mission, and he appears to have proceeded immediately to Naples, where he remained until the pontificate of Celestine V, teaching publicly his *Ars Magna* and *Arbor Scientiarum*. In December 1294, he went to Rome to persuade the Pope to send missionaries to the infidels, and he appears to have obtained the establishment of several colleges for the study of oriental languages. Moreover, the University of Paris, by an official act, adopted and recommended the use of his short method of acquiring knowledge and some of his more important philosophical doctrines. Still, his missionary efforts were not generally successful, and he again wandered from place to place, refuting heretics. He traveled to Montpellier, where he was received with distinction by Raymond Gauffredy, General of the Order of St. Francis. He obtained letters of association as a benefactor to the order, whose superiors were placed under his direction,

<summary>Raymond Lully travels to Tunis to convert Muslims but is imprisoned and banished under threat of death. He returns to Europe, teaching his "Ars Magna" in Naples and Paris while advocating for missionary colleges and the study of oriental languages.</summary>

<page-num>77</page-num>

and he taught his method in their houses. He preached in Cyprus against the Nestorians and Georgians, striving to bring them back to the bosom of the Church. He applied for assistance in his many enterprises to the Kings of France, Sicily, Majorca, and Cyprus, but generally in vain.

In 1308, he returned to Paris, where he conversed with the celebrated Johannes Scotus, who is known as the "subtle doctor." He had the satisfaction of finding that King Philip the Fair <note>Philip le Bel</note> had directed that Oriental languages be taught at the University of Paris. This induced Raymond to travel in the following year to Ferdinand IV, King of Castile, to persuade him to unite with the King of France for the recovery of the Holy Land; however, this oft-repeated and invariably disastrous and futile enterprise was fortunately not undertaken. He ventured again to Africa and landed at Bona—the ancient Hippo that was the diocese of Saint Augustine—and despite the opposition of its Muslim <note>The original uses "Mohammedan"</note> inhabitants, he succeeded in converting seventy followers of the philosopher Averroës. From there, he traveled to Algiers and converted many, which brought down upon him the persecution of the authorities. A bridle was put into his mouth, as if he were a horse, and by this means he was deprived of the free use of speech for forty days; he was then publicly beaten and expelled from the kingdom. He had no other path open to him but to return to Tunis, where a death sentence awaited him, but he remained hidden and shortly afterward proceeded to Bugia. There he refuted the doctrines of the Muslim <note>The original uses "Mohammedan"</note> priests, successfully avoiding innumerable deadly snares prepared by the people against him at the suggestion of their religious teachers. He was at length cast into a miserable dungeon, where he might well have perished, but the pleas of certain Genoese merchants obtained for him

The gates of Raymond’s prison were at length thrown open, and, as a disturber of the public peace, he was ordered to quit those parts at once. The illustrious wanderer embarked in a Genoese vessel with his books and papers, but he was wrecked ten miles from the town of Pisa, barely escaping with the loss of all his belongings. At Pisa he fell sick and was carefully attended by the Dominicans. On his recovery, he resumed his public teaching. The conversion of the Muslims <note>The original uses "Mohammedans"</note> and the conquest of the Holy Land were still his chief ends, and he so eloquently solicited the inhabitants of Pisa to institute an order of Christian Knights for the deliverance of Judea, that they sent him with letters to the Holy Father; he was entrusted by the inhabitants of Genoa with similar documents and also bore the voluntary offer of the ladies in that town to contribute a considerable sum of money toward such a pious and praiseworthy purpose. With these assurances he sought the Pope at Avignon, presented his letters, and added the most powerful reasons of his own to persuade him to proclaim another crusade. Naturally, he obtained nothing from the Papal Court, and he retired to Paris, sorrowful at his failure and at the coldness of the prelates of the Church. He continued writing and teaching, and in October 1311, hearing that a general council would be held at Vienna <note>Likely the Council of Vienne in France</note>, he considered this a favorable opportunity and presented himself before it to demand three

# RAYMOND LULLY. #

things:— 1. The establishment of several monasteries composed of learned and courageous men who, willing to risk their lives for the cause of Christ, would take pleasure in learning languages in order to spread the Gospel more effectively. 2. The consolidation of all the Military Orders in the Christian world into a single order, so that living under one religious rule and inspired by the same desires, they might all do battle with the Saracens. By suppressing all seeds of jealousy and all selfish interests through a praiseworthy competition and with true Christian piety, they would seek to deliver the Holy Land from the hands of the infidels <note>The original uses "miscreants"</note>. 3. The condemnation, by authority of the Pope and the Council, of all the works of Averroës used in Christian colleges and schools, because they were distinctly and directly opposed to the doctrines of true religion. In order to shed more light on this last point, he composed a treatise entitled *De Natale Pueri*. He was again unsuccessful and returned to Paris without having accomplished anything. With unconquerable perseverance, he again set himself more diligently than ever to the composition of books in Latin, Spanish, and Arabic for the edification of the Faithful and the instruction of the Saracens. He became indeed one of the most prolific authors in the world, and when weary of the quiet life of scholarship, he returned to Majorca. Though far advanced in years, he embarked, despite the danger, for Tunis, hoping to work secretly for the conversion of its inhabitants.

According to another account, he publicly proclaimed his return, crying, 
> “Do you not remember that I am the man whom your princes formerly hunted from this country and from Tunis out of fear that I should illuminate your souls with the truths of our holy religion, for which you already had some inclination? The single hope of your salvation, and the resolution I have taken to suffer all

<header>LIVES OF ALCHEMYSTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.</header>

the torments of the world for the love of my God, leads me back among you, to do with me as you please.”

In either case, his return was discovered. The people rose as one in a riot against him, and after covering him with disgrace and terrible injuries, they chased him with stones from the town to the port, where he fell, miserably overwhelmed.

According to numerous biographers, certain merchants from either *Majorca* or *Genoa* who were passing *Tunis* saw a great light in the shape of a pyramid near the port on the night of this catastrophe. This light seemed to come from a heap of stones; curious to discover its cause, they went ashore in a boat and found the precious body of Raymond Lully, whom they immediately recognized despite his disfigured condition.

But M. E. J. Delécleuze, writing in the *Revue des Deux Mondes*, gives us the same narrative without the veneer of the miraculous.

> “Night fell, and the body of Raymond Lully remained on the seashore. During this entire terrible scene, none of the converts—and even less the European Christians then living in the town—had dared to defend the missionary or even to intercede on his behalf. However, certain Genoese merchants, desiring to pay the last honors to his corpse, came in a boat under the cover of darkness to take it away. While performing this pious duty, they realized that Raymond Lully was still breathing. They carried him in haste to their ship and immediately set sail for Majorca; the holy and learned man died in sight of that island on June 29, 1315, at the age of eighty.” <note>The original text uses the word "expired."</note>

<header>RAYMOND LULLY.</header>

It has already been stated that Raymond Lully was one of the most prolific writers of his own or of any age. The following list of his works was given by Alfonso de Proaza in 1515, and is reproduced by A. Perroquet:

| Names of Subjects | Number of Treatises |
|-------------------|:----------------:|
| On the *Ars Veritatis Demonstrativus*, | 60 |
| Grammar and Rhetoric, | 7 |
| Logic, | 22 |
| On Understanding, | 7 |
| On Memory, | 4 |
| On Will, | 8 |
| On Moral and Political Philosophy, | 12 |
| On Law, | 8 |
| Philosophy and Physics, | 32 |
| Metaphysics, | 26 |
| Mathematics, | 19 |
| Medicine and Anatomy, | 20 |
| Chemistry, | 49 |
| Theology, | 212 |
| **Total number of treatises,** | **486** |

This list is accepted without suspicion or criticism by M. Delécleuze; but as Raymond Lully did not begin writing until 1270, and as he died in 1318 at the latest, this calculation requires us to suppose that he produced ten treatises every year without intermission for the space of forty-eight years.

...determined to transport it to Genoa. Three times they put to sea with a wind that seemed favorable, but just as often they were forced to return to port. This proved plainly to be the will of God, and obliged them to make known the martyrdom of the man whom they revered, who was stoned for the glory of God in the town of Bugia (?) in the year of grace 1318." From this account it will be seen that the place of Lully's violent death, as well as the date on which it occurred, are both involved in doubt. He was born under the pontificate of Honorius IV, and died, according to Genebrand, about 1304; but the author of the preface to the meditations of the Hermit Blaquerne positively fixes his death on the feast of the martyrdom of SS. Peter and Paul, June 29, 1315, and declares that he was eighty-six years old.

years, which would have been perfectly impossible for even the most cloistered, book-devoted student, and Raymond Lully was a man of indefatigable activity, as the facts of his wandering existence abundantly reveal. A writer in the *Biographie Universelle*, Paris, 1820, has the following pertinent remarks on this subject:—“Some of his biographers have increased the number of his treatises to several thousand. <note>* For example, Jean-Marie de Vernon, who extends the list to about three thousand and, following Father Pacifique of Provence, prolongs Lully's life through the discovery of the universal medicine.</note> More moderate writers have reduced them from five hundred to three hundred, which lie scattered among the libraries of Majorca, Rome, Barcelona, the Sorbonne, St. Victor, and the Charterhouse in Paris; but scarcely two hundred can be found distinguished by their titles and the first words of the work; and this number must be further reduced because the difference between some of them is very slight, as chapters have been given the titles of separate works, and because the explanations of professors or disciples have often been mistaken by uncritical writers for the lessons of the master.”

Now, the great problem in the varied life of the enlightened theosophist and possessor of the universal science who died so violently at Tunis, or Bugia, for the cause of his Master, is this—whether or not he is to be identified with that Raymond Lully whom Éliphas Lévi calls “a grand and sublime adept of Hermetic science,” who is said to have made gold and Rose nobles for one Edward, King of England, and who left behind him, as monuments of his unparalleled alchemical skill, those world-famous treatises, testaments, and codicils which, rightly or wrongly, are attributed—under the title “chemistry, 49 treatises”—to the heroic martyr of Majorca. important point, the writer, already quoted, in the *Bibliothèque Universelle*, testifies that 
> “the works on alchemy must be referred to another Raymond, of Ferrago, a Jewish neophyte <note>A recent convert to the faith.</note>, who lived after 1315, and with whom Abraham Bzovius confused the first Raymond in attributing to him some propositions condemned by Gregory XI.” 

And again:—
> “The works on alchemy attributed to him are too opposed to the evangelical poverty of a man who had renounced everything in his zeal for the religion of Jesus Christ, and who protests in many places against the chimera of the philosopher’s stone, sought in his time by Arnaud de Villeneuve, whose disciple he was supposed to be. The circumstances and the dates even in several of these books — of which that on natural wisdom is addressed to Edward III. — prove, moreover, that they must be referred to a later epoch.”

The problem is eminently difficult to solve, and must be considered at some length.

Raymond Lully went to Vienna to be present at a general council of the Church in the year 1311. While in this city, it is alleged that he received letters from Edward, King of England, who had ascended the throne in 1307, and from Robert, King of Scotland, who both invited him with much persuasion to visit their realms. Hoping to encourage these princes to assist him in his plans against the infidels, he soon arrived in London in the company of John Cremer, Abbot of Westminster.

This ecclesiastic is said to have been one of the most celebrated Hermetic artists of his age. He worked thirty years to attain the end of alchemy, but the obscurities of the Hermetic writers, which he could not clarify, cast him into a labyrinth of errors. The more he read, the more he wondered; at last, tired of the loss of his money, and much more of his precious time, he set out to travel,

<meta>This page continues the narrative of John Cremer, the Abbot of Westminster, who, after thirty years of unsuccessful alchemical study, traveled to Italy and met Raymond Lully.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS.</header>

and had the good fortune to meet Raymond Lully in Italy. He formed a close friendship with him, remaining for some time in his company, inspired by his penitent life and enlightened by his philosophical conversations. The adept, although he spoke about alchemy, would not, however, entirely reveal the essential points of the operation. Cremer was persuasive and affectionate; he perceived that Lully's zeal for the conversion of the infidels extended to the misguided enthusiasm for inciting open war against the Mohammedans, and easily persuaded him to visit England in the hope of King Edward's assistance. The adept stayed with his friend in the Abbey of Westminster, where he worked and perfected the stone which Cremer had sought unsuccessfully for so long. He was duly presented to the King, who, having been previously informed of the talents of the illustrious stranger, received him with respect and attention.

When he "shared his treasures," the single condition he made was that they should not be spent on the luxuries of a court or in war with a Christian prince, but that the King should go in person with an army against the infidels.

Edward, under the pretense of honoring Raymond, gave him an apartment in the Tower of London, where the adept repeated his process. He transmuted base metal into gold, which was coined at the mint into six million nobles, each worth three pounds sterling at the present day. These coins are well known to antiquarians by the name of Rose Nobles. In an assay test, they prove to be of purer gold than the Jacobus, or any other gold coin made in those times. Lully, in his last testament, declares that in a short time while in London, he converted twenty-two tons weight of quicksilver, lead, and tin into the precious metal.

<summary>John Cremer convinces Raymond Lully to come to England, where Lully successfully performs alchemy at the Tower of London for King Edward. The resulting gold was minted into "Rose Nobles" on the condition it would be used for a crusade against the infidels.</summary>
