<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the alchemist Raymond Lully, specifically focusing on his legendary time in England and the historical inconsistencies surrounding the "Rose Nobles" story.</meta>

### RAYMOND LULLY.

His lodging in the Tower proved to be only an honorable prison. When Raymond had satisfied the desires of the King, the latter disregarded the goal which the adept <note>original: "adept"; a term for a person skilled in the secrets of alchemy</note> was so eager to see carried out. To regain his own liberty, Lully was forced to escape secretly, and he quickly departed from England.

Cremer, whose intentions were sincere, was no less grieved than Raymond at this outcome, but he was a subject to his sovereign and could only groan in silence. He declares his extreme distress in his testament, and his monastery offered up daily prayers to God for the success of Raymond's cause. The Abbot lived long after this and saw part of the reign of King Edward III. The course of operations which he proposes in his testament, with apparent sincerity, is no less veiled in mystery than those found in the most obscure authors. *

Now, in the first place, this story is not consistent with itself. If Raymond Lully were in Vienna in 1311, how did John Cremer manage to meet him in Italy at or about the same time? In the second place, the whole story concerning the manufacture of Rose Nobles <note>A famous English gold coin; legend claimed Lully created the gold for them through alchemy</note> is a series of blunders. The King who ascended the throne of England in 1307 was Edward II, but the Rose Nobles first appear in the history of numismatics <note>The study or collection of coins and currency</note> during the reign of Edward IV, in the year 1465.

"In the King's fifth year, by another indenture <term>a formal legal contract or agreement</term> with Lord Hastings, the gold coins were again altered, and it was ordered that only forty-five nobles, instead of fifty as in the last two reigns, should be made from a pound of gold. This brought back the weight of the noble to one hundred and fifty grains <note>A small unit of measurement for precious metals</note>, as it had been from 1351 to 1412, but its value was raised to 10 shillings. At the same time, new coins impressed with angels <note>The "Angel" was a gold coin introduced in 1465, depicting the archangel Michael</note> were ordered to be made, sixty—

seven and a half to be struck from one pound of gold, and each to be of the value of 6s. 8d.—that is to say, the new angel which weighed eighty grains was to be of the same value as the noble had been which weighed one hundred and eight grains. *The new nobles, to distinguish them from the old ones, were called Rose Nobles, from the rose which is stamped on both sides of them, or ryals, or royals—a name borrowed from the French, who had given it to a coin which bore the figure of the King in his royal robes, which the English ryals did not. Notwithstanding its inappropriateness, however, the name of royal was given to these 10s. pieces, not only by the people, but also in several statutes of the realm.*" *

In the third place, the testament attributed to John Cremer, Abbot of Westminster, to which we are indebted for the primary account of Lully's visit to England, is altogether spurious. No person bearing that name ever filled the position of Abbot at any period in the history of the Abbey.

The only coinage of nobles which has been attributed to alchemy was that made by Edward III in 1344. The gold used in this coinage is supposed to have been manufactured in the Tower; the adept <note>A person skilled in the secrets of alchemy</note> in question was not Raymond Lully, but the English Ripley <note>George Ripley, a famous 15th-century English alchemist</note>.

Whether the "saint of Majorca" <note>Raymond Lully</note> was proficient in the Hermetic art <note>Alchemy</note> or not, it is quite certain that he did not visit the British Isles. It is also certain that in the *Ars Magna Sciendi*, part 9, in the chapter on Elements, he states that one species of metal cannot be changed into another, and that the gold of alchemy has only the appearance of that metal; that is, it is simply an imitation.

As all the treatises attributed to Raymond Lully cannot possibly be his, and as his wandering and turbulent life could

<meta>This page continues the critique of Raymond Lully’s alleged alchemical career, arguing that his busy life as a missionary and philosopher left no time for extensive experiments, which suggests his attributed alchemical writings are likely forgeries or the work of another individual.</meta>

->RAYMOND LULLY.<-

would have afforded him few opportunities for the long course of experiments that are generally involved in the search for the *magnum opus* <note>the Great Work of alchemy</note>, it is reasonable to suppose that his alchemical writings are spurious, or that two authors bearing the same name have been ignorantly confused. Regarding "the Jewish neophyte" referred to by the *Biographie Universelle*, no particulars of his life are forthcoming. The whole question is necessarily involved in uncertainty, but it is a point of no small importance to have established for the first time the mythical nature of the Cremer Testament. This work was first published by Michael Maier in his *Tripus Aureus* around the year 1614. The two treatises that accompany it appear to be genuine relics of Hermetic antiquity.

The "Clavicula, or Little Key" of Raymond Lully is generally considered to contain the chief secrets of alchemical mastery; it clarifies the other treatises of its author and undertakes to explain the entire art without any fiction. The transmutation of metals depends upon their previous reduction into volatile sophic <term>argent vive</term> <note>living silver or mercury</note>, and the only metals worth reducing for the attainment of this <term>prima materia</term> <note>prime matter</note> are silver and gold. This <term>argent vive</term> is said to be drier, hotter, and more refined than the common substance, but its extraction is shrouded in mystery and symbolism, and the recipes are impossible to follow because the materials are so evasively and deceptively described. At the same time, it is clear that the operations are physical, and that the materials and objects are also physical; these points are sufficient for our purpose and may be easily verified by research.

Moreover, the alchemist who calls himself Raymond Lully was acquainted with nitric acid and its uses as a solvent of metals. He could create <term>aqua regia</term> <note>royal water; a mixture of acids used to dissolve gold</note> by

Spirit of wine was well known to him, says Gruelin; he strengthened it with dry carbonate of potash and prepared vegetable tinctures by its means. He mentions alum from Rocca, marcasite, and white and red mercurial precipitate. He knew the volatile alkali and its coagulation by means of alcohol. He was acquainted with cupellated silver and first obtained rosemary oil by distilling the plant with water.*

## ARNOLD DE VILLANOVA.

The date and the birthplace of this celebrated adept are both uncertain. Catalonia, Milan, and Montpellier have each been named for the location, and 1245 is, on the whole, the probable period.

Arnold studied medicine at Paris for twenty years, after which he traveled through Italy for ten more, visiting the different universities. He subsequently entered Spain, but hearing that his friend Peter d’Apono was in the hands of the Inquisition, he prudently withdrew and lived under the patronage of Frederick, King of Naples and Sicily, writing his tracts on medicine and his “Commentary on the School of Salerno.” He is said to have perished in a storm during the year 1314, but a circular letter written by Pope Clement V in 1311 urges those living under his authority to discover, if possible, and send to him the “Treatise on Medicine” written by Arnold, his physician, who promised it to the Holy Father but died before he could present it. In this case, the date of his death may be more accurately fixed at 1310.

Arnold was, according to the custom of the period,

<meta>This page continues the biography of Arnold de Villanova, specifically addressing the accusations of magic against him and listing his recognized alchemical writings.</meta>

charged with magical practices. François Pegna declares that all his knowledge of alchemy was derived from the demon. Mariana accuses him of attempting to create a human being by using certain drugs placed in a pumpkin. But he is defended against these accusations by Delrio, and the orthodox *Dictionnaire des Sciences Occultes* considers that Clement V would not have chosen an initiate of the magical arts as his physician. In 1317, the Inquisition of Tarragona condemned his books to be burned, but this was due to the heretical opinions they contained. He wrote criticisms of the monastic life and religious services, and maintained that works of divine faith and charity were more pleasing to God than the Sacrifice of the Mass.

His skill in Hermetic philosophy has been generally recognized. His contemporary, the celebrated legal expert John Andre, says of him:
> “During this time appeared Arnold de Villeneuve, a great theologian, a skillful physician, and a wise alchemist who made gold, which he submitted to all tests.”

Arnold also has a reputation for writing with more clarity and insight than other philosophers. His alchemical works were published in 1509 in one folio volume. His *Libellus de Somniorum Interpretatione et Somnia Danielis* is extremely rare in its original quarto edition. Several alchemical and magical works are loosely attributed to him. Among these must be included the book called *De Physicis Ligaturis*, believed to be translated from Arabic; *De Sigillis duodecim Signorum*, which concerns the signs of the zodiac; and the book of the “Three Impostors,” which the *Dictionnaire des Sciences Occultes* describes as “stupid and infamous.”

The *Thesaurus Thesaurorum* and the *Rosarium Philosophorum*, the *Speculum Alchemiæ* and the *Perfectum Magisterium* are the most notable of all his alchemical treatises. To these the student should add his *Scientia Scientiæ* and

brief *Testamentum*. There are various editions, but the tracts can be found in collected form in the *Bibliotheca Chemicæ Curiosa* by J. J. Mangetus.

Arnold asserts that <term>argent vive</term> is the medicine of all the metals, that common sulfur is the cause of all their imperfections, that the <term>stone of the philosophers</term> is one, and that it is to be extracted from that in which it exists. It exists in all bodies, including common <term>argent vive</term>. The first physical work is the dissolution of the stone in its own mercury to reduce it to its <term>prima materia</term>. All the operations of the <term>magnum opus</term> are described in succession, including the composition of the white and the red <term>elixirs</term>, and the multiplication of the metallic medicine.

The <term>marcasite</term> frequently mentioned by Arnold is thought to be identical to <term>bismuth</term>. He was familiar with the preparation of oil of turpentine and oil of rosemary, and he performed distillations in a glazed earthen vessel with a glass top and helm.

## JEAN DE MEUNG.

Poet, alchemist, and astrologer, a man of some fortune and descended from an ancient family, Jean de Meung was one of the leading figures at the Court of King Philippe le Bel. According to the latest authorities, he was born around the middle of the thirteenth century. His continuation of the *Roman de la Rose*, which Guillaume de Lorris had begun sometime before the year 1260, was undertaken not in his nineteenth year, as is generally stated, but around or slightly before the age of thirty, and at the request of the French King.

The Romance of the Rose, "that epic of ancient France," as Éliphas Lévi calls it, has been generally considered by alchemists to be a poetic and allegorical presentation of the

<summary>This page concludes the review of Arnold de Villanova's alchemical writings and chemical discoveries before introducing Jean de Meung, the 13th-century poet and alchemist who continued the "Roman de la Rose."</summary>

<keywords>Arnold de Villanova, Jean de Meung, Roman de la Rose, argent vive, philosophers' stone, magnum opus, alchemy, Philippe le Bel, bismuth, distillation</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jean de Meung’s alchemical influence and the chemical principles found within the *Romance of the Rose*, followed by a legendary account of his death and burial.</meta>

->JEAN DE MEUNG.<-

secrets of the <term>*magnum opus*</term>. It professes, at any rate, the principles of <term>Hermetic Philosophy</term>, and <term>Jean de Meung</term> was also the author of “Nature’s Remonstrances to the Alchemist” and “The Alchemist’s Answer to Nature.” <term>Hermetic</term> commentaries have been written upon the romance-poem, and tradition has ascribed to the author the accomplishment of great <term>transmutations</term>. The sermon of <term>Genius</term>, chaplain and confessor to <term>Dame Nature</term>, in the Romance, is an exhibition of the principles of <term>chemistry</term>, as well as a satire on the bombastic and unintelligible preaching which was in vogue at that period. From verse 16,914 to verse 16,997 there is much chemical information.

The year 1216 is the probable period of the poet’s death. The story told of his testament has only a foundation in legend, but it is worth repeating as evidence of the general belief in his skill as an <term>alchemist</term>.

He chose by his will, says the story, to be buried in the Church of the <term>Jacobins</term>, and, as an acknowledgment, left them a coffer that appeared, at least by its weight, to be filled with precious things, probably with the best gold which could be manufactured by the skill of the <term>Hermetists</term>. He ordered, however, that this coffer should not be opened until after his funeral, when, moved by the piety of the deceased, the monks assembled in great numbers to be present at its opening and to offer up thanks to God. They found to their great disappointment that the coffer was filled with large pieces of slate beautifully engraved with figures of geometry and arithmetic. The indignation of the fathers was excited by this posthumous imposture, and they proposed to eject the body of <term>Jean de Meung</term> from their consecrated precincts; but the <term>Parliament</term> being informed of this inhumanity, obliged the <term>Jacobins</term>, by a decree, to leave the deceased undisturbed in the honorable sepulchre of their conventual cloisters.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical literature, moving from allegorical complaints about failed alchemists to the works and experiments of the 14th-century Italian monk Ferarius.</meta>

In "Nature's Remonstrance to the Alchemist," the alchemist is described as a foolish and deceptive *<term>souffleur</term>* <note>original: "souffleur"; literally a "bellows-blower," a derogatory term for an amateur alchemist who focused only on mechanical labor rather than philosophical understanding</note> who uses nothing but mechanical arts. The complainant—Nature herself—bitterly scolds the fanatical student who spreads the foul smells of sulfur over her beautiful domain. He tortures these materials in his furnaces in vain, for he will certainly achieve nothing by such a method. In his "Reply," the alchemist appears as a repentant man, convinced of his mistakes. He blames these errors on the crude allegories, symbolic sentences, and misleading instructions found in the writings of the master alchemists.

### THE MONK FERARIUS.

Around the beginning of the fourteenth century, this Italian artist gave the world two treatises—*On the Philosophers' Stone* <note>original: *De Lapide Philosophorum*</note> and *The Treasury of Philosophy* <note>original: *Thesaurus Philosophiæ*</note>, which are printed in *The Chemical Theater* <note>original: *Theatrum Chimicum*</note>.

The "wonderful spectacle" of the <term>palingenesis</term> <note>The supposed chemical process of resurrecting a plant or animal from its ashes.</note> of plants is described by this Jesuit. "Immediately after exposing the vial filled with the essence of the rose to the sun's rays, a perfect world of miracles is revealed within the narrow space of the vessel. The plant which lay buried in its ashes wakes up, rises, and unfolds. In the space of half an hour, the vegetable phoenix is brought back to life from its own dust. The rose emerges from its tomb and takes on a new life. It is the floral symbol of the resurrection through which mortals, lying in darkness and in the shadow of death, will pass into beautiful immortality."

The treatise on the philosophical stone very correctly observes that in alchemy, the first thing to be determined is what is truly meant by the *<term>many-named quicksilver of the wise</term>* <note>original: *myrionimous argentum vivum sapientum*</note>, a point on which the author gracefully

<summary>This page describes an allegorical critique of amateur alchemists and introduces the work of the 14th-century Italian monk Ferarius, focusing on his description of "palingenesis," or the chemical resurrection of plants.</summary>
<keywords>Nature's Remonstrance to the Alchemist, souffleur, sulfur, Monk Ferarius, palingenesis, quicksilver of the wise, chemical theater, alchemy history</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the works of the monk Ferarius, specifically the obscurity of his "Thesaurus Philosophiæ," before moving to a biographical account of Pope John XXII and his debated relationship with alchemy.</meta>

declines information. Both works are extremely obscure and frustrating. The *Treasury of Philosophy* <note>original: *Thesaurus Philosophiæ*</note> testifies that the clear speech of the philosophers is entirely deceptive, and that it is only in their incomprehensible depths that we must seek the light of Hermes.

Alchemy is the science of the four elements, which are found in all created substances, but are not of the common kind. The entire practice of the art is simply the conversion of these elements into one another. The seed and matter of every metal is mercury, as it is refined and otherwise prepared within the earth, and each of them can be reduced into this <term>prima materia</term>, with the help of which they are also, each and every one, capable of being increased and multiplied, even to infinity.

### POPE JOHN XXII.

This pope is claimed as an adept by the alchemists—a claim which is denied, but not disproved, by his traditional biographers. That he believed in the power of magic is shown by the accusation he directed against Géraud, Bishop of Cahors, whom he accused of plotting to poison him, along with the entire college of cardinals, and specifically of having devised sorceries and diabolical enchantments against all of them. He was a contemporary of Raymond Lully and Arnold de Villanova, and is said to have been the pupil and friend of the latter. Nevertheless, the trouble caused at that time by the frauds of fake alchemists led him to issue a papal bull condemning those who traded in this science as charlatans who promised what they were unable to perform. Hermetic writers assert that this bull was not directed against true adepts, and his dedication to his laboratory at Avignon seems to be a fairly well-established fact.

<keywords>Alchemy, Pope John XXII, prima materia, four elements, Géraud Bishop of Cahors, Avignon, Raymond Lully, Arnold de Villanova, Thesaurus Philosophiæ</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Pope John XXII, moving from the rumors of his laboratory at Avignon to historical records of his writings, his ecclesiastical career, and his legendary wealth.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
->LIVES OF ALCHEMYSTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.<-

Franciscus Pagi, in his *Breviarum de Gestis Romanorum Pontificum*, includes the following passage:—*Joannes scripsit quoque latino sermone artem metallorum transmutorium; quod opus prodiit Gallici incerto translatore Lugduni, anno 1557 in 8vo.* <note>John also wrote in the Latin language on the art of transmuting metals; which work was published in French by an unknown translator at Lyons, in the year 1557, in octavo.</note> It is acknowledged that he was a writer on medicine. His *Thesaurus Pauperum* <note>Treasury of the Poor</note>, a collection of recipes, was printed at Lyons in 1525, and he was the author of a treatise on diseases of the eye, and of another on the formation of the fetus. He was born at Cahors, according to the general opinion, of poor but reputable parents; he showed at an early period his skill in law and the sciences. The circumstances of his life are exceedingly obscure until his consecration as Bishop of Fréjus in 1300. Subsequently, he was promoted to the see of Avignon, and Clement V created him cardinal-bishop of Porto. He was raised to the papacy at Lyons, and reigned at Avignon until his death in 1334. He left behind him in his coffers the sum of eighteen million florins in gold and seven millions in jewels, besides valuable consecrated vessels. Alchemists attribute these vast treasures to his skill in their science, and assert in addition that he manufactured two hundred ingots, apparently on a single occasion. By a calculation of one of his biographers, this quantity of the precious metal was equivalent to £660,000 British sterling. A treatise entitled “The Elixir of the Philosophers, or the Transmutatory Art of Metals,” is attributed to him. It was translated from Latin into French, and published in duodecimo at Lyons in 1557. It is written *ad clerum* <note>for the clergy</note>, and for this reason is probably the more misleading. It represents the constituents of the perfect medicine to be vinegar, salt, urine, and sal ammoniac, with the addition of an undescribed substance called <term>sulphur vive</term>.

<meta>This page transitions from the wealth and works of Pope John XXII to the biography of the famous French alchemist Nicholas Flamel, focusing on his reputation, mysterious wealth, and early life.</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>
->NICHOLAS FLAMEL.<-

The name of this alchemical adept has been profoundly venerated not only in the memory of the Hermetists but in the hearts of the French people, among whom he is the central figure of many marvelous legends and traditions. 

> “While in all ages and nations the majority of hierophants have derived little but deception, ruin, and despair as the result of their devotion to alchemy, Nicholas Flamel enjoyed permanent good fortune and serenity. Far from spending his resources in the practice of the *magnum opus*, he added with remarkable suddenness a vast treasure to a moderate fortune. These he used for charitable endowments and in pious foundations that long survived him and long sanctified his memory. He built churches and chapels which were adorned with statues of himself, accompanied by symbolic characters and mysterious crosses, which subsequent adepts long strove to decipher, so that they might discover his secret history and the kabbalistic description of the process by which he was led to the realization of the Grand Magisterium.”

Whether Flamel was born in Paris or Pontoise is no more uncertain than the precise date of his birth. This occurred some time during the reign of Philippe le Bel, the plunderer of the great order of the Temple, and, on the whole, the most probable year is 1330. His parents were poor and left him little more than the humble house in Paris which he continued to own until his death, and which he eventually bequeathed to the Church. It stood in Notary Street, at the corner of Marivaux Street, opposite the Marivaux door of the Church of Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie.

Authorities disagree as to the amount of education that

<meta>This page continues the biography of Nicholas Flamel, detailing his profession as a scrivener, his marriage to Pernelle, and the legendary vision of the book of Abraham the Jew.</meta>

Flamel obtained an education in his youth, but it was sufficient to qualify him for the business of a <term>scrivener</term>, which, in spite of his wealth and his recognized wisdom, he continued to follow throughout his life. He was proficient in painting and poetry, and had a taste for architecture and the mathematical sciences; yet he applied himself steadily to business, and contracted a prudent marriage, his choice falling on a widow named Pernelle, who, though handsome, was over forty years old, but who brought a considerable dowry to her second husband.

In his capacity as a copyist before the age of printing, books of all classes fell into the hands of Flamel, and among them were many of those illuminated alchemical treatises which are reckoned among the rarest treasures of medieval manuscripts. Acquainted with the Latin language, he gradually accumulated an external knowledge of the aims and theories of the adepts. His interest and curiosity were awakened, and he began studying them in his leisure moments. Now tradition informs us that, whether his dedication was great, his desire intense, or whether he was uniquely fitted to be included by divine election among the illuminated Sons of the Doctrine, or for whatever other reason, the mystical <term>Bath-Kôl</term> <note>A Hebrew term referring to a "divine voice" or "daughter of a voice."</note> appeared to him in the form of an angel, bearing a remarkable book bound in well-crafted copper, with leaves of thin bark, engraved very carefully with a pen of iron. An inscription in characters of gold contained a dedication addressed to the Jewish nation by Abraham the Jew—prince, priest, astrologer, and philosopher.

> "Flamel," cried the radiant apparition, "behold this book of which you understand nothing; to many others besides yourself it would remain forever unintelligible, but one day you shall discern in its pages what none but yourself will see!"

# NICHOLAS FLAMEL

At these words, Flamel eagerly stretched out his hands to take possession of the priceless gift, but the book and the angel disappeared in a gold-bearing <note>original: "auriferous"</note> tide of light. The scribe <note>original: "scrivener"</note> awoke to be enchanted from then on by the divine dream of <term>alchemy</term> <note>The medieval forerunner of chemistry, focused on transforming base metals into gold and finding a universal elixir.</note>. However, so much time passed without any fulfillment of the angelic promise that the passion of his imagination cooled. The great hope gradually dwindled away, and he was settling once more into the ordinary existence of a hardworking scribe when, on a certain day of election in the year 1357, an event occurred which proved the truthfulness of his visionary promise-maker and raised his ambition and aspirations to an intense heat. This event, along with the consequences it brought about, is narrated in the last testament of Nicholas Flamel, which begins in the following impressive manner, but leaves out all reference to the legendary vision:

> "The Lord God of my life, who raises the humble in spirit out of the lowest dust and makes the hearts of those who hope in Him rejoice, be eternally praised.
>
> "Who, by His own grace, reveals the springs of His bounty to believing souls and places the crowns of all earthly happiness <note>original: "felicities"</note> and glories beneath their feet.
> 
> "In Him let us always put our confidence, in His fear let us place our happiness, and in His mercy the hope and glory of restoration from our fallen state.
> 
> "And in our prayers <note>original: "supplications"</note> to Him, let us show a faith that is sincere <note>original: "unfeigned"</note> and firm, an assurance that shall not be shaken forever.
> 
> "And You, O Lord God Almighty, as You, out of Your infinite and most desirable goodness, have condescended to open the earth and unlock Your treasures to me, Your poor and unworthy servant, and have given into my possession the sources and springs of all the treasures and riches of this world."

<meta>This page continues Nicholas Flamel's autobiographical account, describing how he earned his living as a scribe in Paris and his discovery of the mysterious alchemical book that would define his life's work.</meta>

# NICHOLAS FLAMEL

I earned my living by the art of writing, drafting legal documents <note>original: "engrossing inventories"</note>, making up accounts, keeping books, and similar work.

“During this time, a gilded book, very old and large, fell into my hands by chance. It cost me only two *florins* <note>A gold coin used in various European countries; two florins was a modest but significant sum for a scribe at the time.</note>.

“It was not made of paper or parchment like other books, but of remarkable bark <note>original: "admirable rinds"</note> (as it seemed to me) from young trees. Its cover was made of *brass*; it was well bound and engraved all over with strange letters, which I took to be Greek characters or something similar.

“I only know that I could not read them; they were neither Latin nor French letters, both of which I understand somewhat.

“Regarding the content written inside, it was engraved (as I suppose) with an iron stylus <note>original: "pencil or graver"</note> upon the bark leaves. It was done remarkably well, in beautiful and neat Latin letters, and elaborately colored.

“It contained twenty-one leaves <note>original: "thrice seven leaves"</note>, for they were numbered at the top of each page <note>original: "folio"</note>. Every seventh leaf had no writing on it; instead, several images or figures were painted there.

“On the first seventh leaf was depicted: 1. A Virgin. 2. Serpents swallowing her up. On the second seventh leaf, a serpent crucified; and on the last seventh leaf, a desert or wilderness, in the middle of which were many beautiful fountains, from which a number of serpents emerged here and there.

“On the first leaf was written in gold capital letters: 'Abraham the Jew, Priest, Prince, Levite, Astrologer, and Philosopher, to the nation of the Jews dispersed throughout France by the wrath of God, wishes health.'

“After these words, the page was filled with many curses and threats, including the word <term>MARANATHA</term> <note>original: "Maranatha"—an Aramaic phrase meaning "Our Lord has come" or "O Lord, come!"; in this context, it is used as a solemn warning or curse against those who would profane a sacred text.</note>, which was frequently repeated against anyone who might look inside to reveal its secrets, unless he was either a Priest or a Scribe.”

The book's unusual construction and symbolic illustrations of serpents and a crucified snake suggest it contains deep alchemical secrets.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Nicholas Flamel's account of discovering a mysterious alchemical book. Having previously described the book's physical appearance—made of bark and bound in brass—he now details its contents and the spiritual and practical instructions it contained for the Jewish people.</meta>

->LIVES OF ALCHEMISTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.<-

"The person who sold me this book was as ignorant of its worth as I was when I bought it. I judge it might have been stolen from some of the Jewish nation, or else found in some place where they used to live in ancient times.

"On the second page <note>original: "leaf"</note> of the book, the author consoled his nation and gave them godly advice to turn from their wickedness and evil ways. Above all, he urged them to flee from idolatry and to wait in patience for the coming of the Messiah, who, by conquering all the kings and powerful rulers <note>original: "potentates"</note> of the earth, would reign in glory with his people for eternity. Without doubt, this was a very godly, wise, and understanding man.

"On the third page, and in all the writings that followed, he taught them, in plain words, the <term>transmutation of metals</term> <note>The alchemical process of changing "base" metals like lead into "noble" metals like gold.</note>. He did this so that he might help and assist his scattered people to pay their taxes <note>original: "tribute"</note> to the Roman Emperors, along with some other things not necessary to repeat here.

"He painted the vessels <note>The glass containers, like flasks or retorts, used in alchemical experiments.</note> along the side or margin of the pages and revealed all the colors as they should arise or appear, along with all the rest of the work.

"But regarding the <term>first matter</term> <note>original: "*prima materia*"; the base substance required to begin the alchemical process.</note>, or the primary agent, he did not say so much as a single word. He only told them that on the fourth and fifth pages he had entirely painted or illustrated it, depicting it with great skill and craftsmanship.

"Now, although it was exceptionally well and clearly <note>original: "materially or intelligibly"</note> illustrated and painted, no man could have ever been able to understand it without being well-skilled in their <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>original: "Cabala"; a system of Jewish mystical and esoteric interpretation.</note>, which is a series of ancient traditions, and also without having studied their books extensively.

"The fourth and fifth pages were without any writing, but were full of beautiful figures, bright and shining, or, as it were, illuminated, and very exquisitely depicted."

> “First, there was a young man painted with wings at his ankles, holding in his hand a <term>caducean rod</term> <note>A staff entwined with two snakes, the symbol of the Greek god Hermes or the Roman god Mercury.</note>, entwined <note>original: "writhen about"</note> with two serpents, with which he struck a helmet covering his head.

> “In my humble opinion <note>original: "mean apprehension"</note>, this seemed to be one of the pagan gods, namely <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents a fundamental substance or "spirit" used in the transformation of metals.</note>. Against him came running and flying with open wings a great old man with an hourglass fixed upon his head and a scythe in his hands, like Death. With this scythe, he intended—as if in anger <note>original: "indignation"</note>—to cut off the feet of Mercury.
>
> “On the other side of the fourth page <note>original: "leaf"</note>, he painted a beautiful flower on the top of a very high mountain, which was being shaken violently by the north wind. Its stem <note>original: "footstalk"</note> was blue, its flowers white and red, and its leaves shone like fine gold. All around it, the dragons and griffins of the north made their nests and homes.
>
> “On the fifth page was a beautiful rosebush in bloom <note>original: "fair rose-tree, flowered"</note> in the middle of a garden, growing up against a hollow oak. At the base of the tree, a fountain of pure white water bubbled forth, which flowed rapidly <note>original: "ran headlong"</note> down into the depths below.
> 
> “Yet this water passed through the hands of a great many people who dug in the earth searching for it; but because of their blindness, none of them recognized <note>original: "knew"</note> it, except for a very few who noticed <note>original: "considered"</note> its weight.
>
> “On the last side of the page was depicted a king with a <term>scimitar</term> <note>original: "faulchion"; a broad, curved sword.</note>, who ordered his soldiers to slaughter many infants before him, while the mothers stood by, weeping at the feet of the murderers.
>
> “The blood of these infants was gathered up by other soldiers and put into a large vessel, in which <term>Sol</term> <note>original: "Sol"; Latin for the Sun. In alchemy, this represents Gold or the masculine principle.</note> and <term>Luna</term> <note>original: "Luna"; Latin for the Moon. In alchemy, this represents Silver or the feminine principle.</note> came to bathe themselves.

...in their churchyard these symbolic <term>hieroglyphic</term> <note>In this context, "hieroglyphic" refers to alchemical symbols or allegorical pictures rather than Egyptian writing.</note> figures of this knowledge. Thus, you have a description of what was contained in the first five leaves.

> “As for what was in all the rest of the written pages, which were written in clear and understandable Latin <note>original: "good and intelligible Latin"</note>, I must keep them secret, lest God, being offended with me, should send His plague and judgments upon me. It would be a wickedness much greater than that of the man who wished that all people in the world had but one head so that he might cut it off with a single blow. <note>Flamel is likely referring to the Roman Emperor Caligula, who famously expressed this wish.</note>
>
> “Having thus obtained this exquisite and precious book, I did nothing else day and night but study it. I understood very well all the processes <note>original: "operations"</note> it described, but I was completely ignorant of the **primary matter** <term>prima materia</term> <note>The foundational, formless substance that alchemists believed was necessary to begin the creation of the Philosophers' Stone.</note> with which I should begin; this made me very sad and discontented.
>
> “My wife, whose name was **Perrenelle**, whom I loved as much as myself and whom I had only recently married, was deeply concerned for me. With many comforting words, she earnestly desired to know how she might deliver me from this trouble.
>
> “I could no longer keep my own counsel, but told her everything, showing her the book itself. When she saw it, she became as delighted with it as I was, and with great pleasure beheld the wonderful cover, the engravings, the images, and the exquisite figures within it; however, she understood them as little as I did.
>
> “Yet it was a comfort to me to talk and share my thoughts with her, and to consider what we should do to find the interpretation and meaning of the book.
>
> “At length, I had all the images and figures of the fourth and fifth pages painted within my chamber, making them as lifelike and true to the original as I could.
>
> “These I showed to the greatest scholars and most learned men in **Paris**, who understood no more of them than I did. I told them the figures were found in a book which taught the secret of the <term>philosophers’ stone</term> <note>A legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and sometimes said to be an elixir of life.</note>.”

<meta>This page continues Nicholas Flamel's narrative of his struggle to understand the "Book of Abraham the Jew." Having shared the book's contents with his wife, Perrenelle, Flamel now describes his attempts to seek outside help and the subsequent years he spent following a false lead.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>

# NICHOLAS FLAMEL.

“However, most of them mocked both me and that most excellent secret, except for one man named Anselm, a practitioner of medicine <note>original: "physic"</note> and a serious student of this art.

“He greatly desired to see my book, which he valued more than anything else in the world, but I always refused him, only giving him a lengthy explanation of the method.

“He told me that the first figure represented Time, which devours all things, and that, according to the number of the six written leaves, a period of six years was required to perfect the stone; and then, he said, we must turn the hourglass and look at it no more.

“I told him this was not painted merely for decoration, but to show the teacher the **primary matter** <term>prima materia</term> <note>The foundational, formless substance that alchemists believed was necessary to begin the creation of the Philosophers' Stone.</note>, or 'first agent,' as was written in the book. He answered me that this 'digestion' <note>The alchemical process of heating a substance slowly over a long period.</note> for six years was, as it were, a second agent, and that the first agent was certainly painted there, which was a white and heavy water.

“This, he claimed, was without doubt **quicksilver** <term>argent vive</term> <note>original: "argent vive" — literally "living silver"; mercury.</note>, which they could not 'fix'—that is, stop its movement or take away its fluidity <note>original: "volubility"</note>—except by that long digestion in the pure blood of young infants.

“For once this **quicksilver** <note>original: "argent vive"</note> was joined with **Gold** <term>Sol</term> <note>The alchemical symbol and name for gold, associated with the Sun.</note> and **Silver** <term>Luna</term> <note>The alchemical symbol and name for silver, associated with the Moon.</note>, it was first turned with them into a plant, like the one painted there, and afterwards through decomposition <note>original: "corruption"</note> into serpents. These serpents, once perfectly dried and digested, were made into a fine powder of gold, which is the stone.

“This discourse, which was irrelevant <note>original: "strange or foreign"</note> to the true matter, was the cause of my error. It made me wander for twenty-one years in a complete maze, far from the truth. During that time, I went through a thousand different processes <note>original: "labyrinths or processes"</note>, but all in vain. However, I never worked with the blood of infants, for I considered that to be wicked and villainous.”

<summary>Nicholas Flamel describes how a physician named Anselm provided a false interpretation of the alchemical book, leading Flamel to waste twenty-one years on fruitless experiments. Anselm wrongly suggested that the "first agent" was mercury fixed by the blood of infants, a method Flamel morally rejected.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Nicholas Flamel's autobiographical account of his quest to decipher the "Book of Abraham the Jew." After failing to understand the symbolic illustrations and finding his own experiments unsuccessful, Flamel decides to seek expert help abroad.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>

“I found in my book that the philosophers called the 'mineral spirit'—which is found in metals, primarily in **Gold** <term>Sol</term>, **Silver** <term>Luna</term>, and **Mercury** <note>original: 'Sol, Luna, and Mercury'</note>—'blood.' In my own judgment, I always agreed with this interpretation. However, these explanations were, for the most part, more clever than they were accurate <note>original: 'not more subtle than true'</note>.

“Because I did not find the signs described in my book appearing at the expected times during my operations or process, I was constantly forced to start over.

“Eventually, having lost all hope of ever understanding those symbols or illustrations, I made a vow to God to seek their interpretation from some Jewish priest belonging to a synagogue in Spain.

“With the consent of my wife, Perrenelle, I took a copy of the illustrations and set off. I dressed in a pilgrim’s robe and carried a staff, just as you see me depicted outside the arch in the churchyard where I placed these Hieroglyphic Figures <note>Flamel refers to the Cemetery of the Innocents in Paris, which he famously decorated with alchemical allegories.</note>.

“On that same wall, on both sides, I have displayed the process, showing in order all the colors of the [Philosophers'] stone as they appear and then disappear.

“This is, in a sense, the very beginning of the book by Hercules titled *Iris, or the Rainbow*, which discusses the stone in these words: *The process of the work is very pleasing to nature.*

“I included these words there specifically for the benefit of great scholars and learned men, who may understand what they allude to.

“In this way, I began my journey to Spain. I traveled so efficiently that I soon arrived at Mountjoy <note>Mons Jovis, a common stop for pilgrims</note> and, shortly after, at St. James <note>Santiago de Compostela, the famous pilgrimage site</note>, where I devoutly fulfilled my vow.

“Having done this, I was in León on my return journey when I met a merchant from Boulogne. He introduced me to a physician named Master Canches <note>original: 'M. Canches'</note>, a man of Jewish heritage who had converted to Christianity and was living in León.

> “I showed him the extract or copy of my figures, by which he was, as it were, entranced <note>original: "ravished"</note> with great astonishment and joy. He asked immediately if I could tell him any news of the book from which they were drawn.

> “I answered him in Latin (the language in which he asked me the question) that I did not doubt I could obtain a sight of the book if I could meet with someone who could explain <note>original: "unfold"</note> the mysteries <term>enigmas</term>. Hearing this, and being moved with great enthusiasm and joy, he began to decipher the beginning for me. To be brief, he was very pleased to have hope of hearing news of the book, and I was just as pleased to hear him speak and interpret it. Without a doubt, he had heard much talk of the book, but it was, as he said, regarding a thing that was believed to be utterly lost. Upon this, we resolved to make our journey, and from León we passed to Oviedo, and from there to Sareson <note>likely Castrojeriz or a similar port town on the northern route</note>, where we took a ship and went to sea in order to go to France.
> 
> “Our voyage was successful and happy, and, having arrived in the kingdom of France, he most truly interpreted for me the greatest part of my figures. In these, even down to the dots and marks <term>points and pricks</term> <note>These likely refer to specific notations or symbolic details within the alchemical illustrations.</note>, he could decipher great mysteries, which were wonderful to me. Having reached Orleans, this learned man fell sick, even to the point of death. He was afflicted with extreme vomiting, which continued to plague him, having been first caused by sea-sickness. Despite this, he was in constant fear that I should leave or forsake him, which was a great trouble to him. And although I was continually by his side, he would almost always be calling for me. At the end of the seventh day of his sickness he died, which was a great grief to me. I buried him, as well as my financial condition would permit, in a church at Orleans.
>

For it was through the grace of God that I attained the perfect knowledge of all I desired.

"Well, I now possessed the *prima materia* <note>original: "first matter"—the formless, original substance required to begin the alchemical process</note>, the first principles; yet I did not yet have their initial preparation, which is a task more difficult than anything else in the world. But in the end, I attained that also, after a long period of wandering in a labyrinth of errors for about three years. During that time, I did nothing but study, search, and labor, just as you see me depicted outside this arch where I have placed my process. I also prayed continually to God and read my book attentively, pondering the words of the philosophers and then testing the various operations I thought they might have meant. At length, I found what I desired, which I recognized by its scent and odor. Once I had this, I easily accomplished the <term>magistery</term> <note>The "magistery" refers to the successful completion of the "Great Work," or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. For by knowing the preparations of the primary agents and then following the directions in my book literally, I could not have failed the work even if I had wanted to.

"Having achieved this, I come now to the stage of projection <note>The final alchemical process of casting the Philosopher's Stone upon a base metal to transmute it into silver or gold</note>. The first time I performed a projection was upon mercury; I transformed about a pound and a half of it into pure silver, which was better than silver from a mine. I proved this by testing it myself and having others assay <term>test for purity</term> it for me several times. This was done in the year of our Lord 1382, on January 17, at about noon on a Monday, in my own house, with only Perrenelle <note>Flamel's wife and collaborator</note> present.

"Again, following exactly the directions in my book, literally and word for word, I made a projection of the red stone <note>The "red stone" or "red elixir" was the highest form of the Philosopher's Stone, capable of producing gold.</note> on a similar quantity of metal, with only Perrenelle present and in the same house. in the year of our Lord 1382, on April 25, at five in the afternoon. I truly transmuted this mercury into almost as much gold—gold that was, indeed, much better than common gold, being softer and more pliable.

> “I speak this in all truth: I have performed this three times with the help of Perrenelle, who understood the process as well as I did because she assisted me in my operations. Undoubtedly, if she had chosen to do it alone, she would have brought the work to the same perfection, or even greater, than I had done. I truly had enough wealth after I had succeeded once; however, I found exceeding great pleasure and delight in seeing and contemplating the *admirable works of Nature within the vessels* <note>In alchemy, "vessels" refers to the glass flasks or containers, such as the alembic or retort, used to heat and transform substances.</note>. To show you that I had performed it three times, I had three furnaces depicted under the same arch, identical to those used for the operations of this work.

> “For a long time, I was very concerned that Perrenelle, out of extreme joy, might not hide her happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note>—which I measured by my own—and that she might let slip some words to her relatives concerning the great treasure we possessed. For an extremity of joy takes away one's reason just as much as an extremity of grief and sorrow. But the goodness of the most great God had not only blessed me by giving me a chaste and sober wife, but she was also a wise and prudent woman. She was not only capable of reason but also of acting reasonably, and she was more discreet and secretive than women usually are. Above all, she was exceedingly religious and devout. Therefore, seeing that she had no hope of having children and was now <term>well stricken in years</term> <note>An archaic phrase meaning "advanced in age."</note>, she made it her business, as I did, to think of God and to devote ourselves to works of charity and mercy.

<page-num>108</page-num>

It was at the end of the year of our Lord 1413. After the death of my faithful companion <note>Perrenelle, Flamel's wife and alchemical partner</note>, whose loss I cannot help but mourn every day of my life, she and I had already founded and endowed with income fourteen hospitals, three chapels, and seven churches in the city of Paris. We had rebuilt all of these from the ground up and enriched them with great gifts and funding, along with many repairs to their cemeteries. We also did about as much in Boulogne as we did in Paris, not to mention the charitable deeds we both performed for specific poor people, especially widows and orphans. If I were to disclose their names, the scale of the charity, and our methods of doing it, my reward would only be in this world; furthermore, it might not be pleasing to the people we helped.

> “Therefore, while building these hospitals, chapels, churches, and cemeteries in the city, I had the most true and essential marks or signs of this art <term>the art: alchemy</term> depicted under the fourth arch. These were presented under veils, types, and hieroglyphic symbols, in imitation of the things contained in the gilded book of Abraham the Jew. This demonstrates to wise men of understanding the direct and perfect way to operate, and the step-by-step process <note>original: "lineary work"</note> of the philosopher's stone. Once someone has perfected this stone, it removes from them the root of all sin and evil—which is greed <note>original: "covetousness"</note>—changing their evil into good and making them generous, polite, religious, devout, and God-fearing, no matter how wicked they were before. For from then on, they are continually overwhelmed by the goodness of God and by the grace and mercy they have obtained from the fountain of Eternal Goodness, the depth of His divine and adorable power, and the consideration of His wonderful works.”

<meta>This page continues the description of Nicholas Flamel's charitable legacies and the physical monuments he left behind in Paris, detailing the various statues and inscriptions that served as evidence of his life and work as they remained in the mid-18th century.</meta>

<header>NICHOLAS FLAMEL.</header>
<page-num>109</page-num>

Things remained this way in the year 1742. In the cemetery of the Holy Innocents stood the arch built by Flamel with the hieroglyphic figures upon it. In two niches, outside the arch and on the cemetery side, were statues of St. James and St. John. Below the statue of St. John was the figure of Flamel himself, reading a book, with a Gothic N. F. to mark his name. The progression of the colors in the order of the process <note>the alchemical process of the Great Work</note>, originally represented on the wall, was, however, faded.

In the same cemetery was a charnel house, or a place to hold the skulls and bones dug up during the digging of new graves. Upon one of the pillars of this charnel house there was a Gothic N. F., with this inscription:

->*This charnel house was made and given to the Church,*<-
->*For the love of God, in the year 1399.*<-

The second of these proofs was upon the Marivaux door of the Church of Saint Jacques-la-Boucherie, where on the left side upon entering was the figure of Flamel, kneeling at the feet of St. James, with a Gothic N. upon the pedestal. The figure of Perrenelle was represented on the opposite side, kneeling at the feet of St. John, with the pedestal bearing a Gothic P.

The third proof was in the street of Notre Dame, at the portal of Genevieve of Arden. There, Flamel’s statue could be seen in a niche, kneeling with a desk at his side, looking toward St. James. There was a Gothic N. F. below and the inscription, “This portal was built in 1402, by the alms of many.” Flamel is supposed to have hidden the fact that he was the principal donor in this way, but the figure may have been erected in his memory.

The fourth and final piece of evidence was in the street of the cemetery of St. Nicholas of the Fields, where there was the wall of an unfinished hospital with figures engraved on the stone and the initials of Flamel.

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the French alchemist Nicholas Flamel, following the death of his wife Perrenelle, and details his final years, his literary legacy, and his last will and testament.</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>

-># LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS.<-

After the death of Perrenelle, the bereaved adept is supposed to have prepared for posterity several works on the supreme science which had enriched him: *The Book of Hieroglyphic Figures* <note>original: *Le Livre des Figures Hieroglyphiques*</note>; *The Philosophical Summary* <note>original: *Le Sommaire Philosophique*</note>, written in verse after the style of the *Romance of the Rose*; *Three Treatises on Metallic Transformation* <note>original: *Trois Traités de la Transformation Metallique*</note>, also in rhymed verse; *The Desired Desire, or Treasure of Philosophy* <note>original: *Le Desir Désiré, ou Trésor de Philosophie*</note>; *The Great Clarification of the Philosopher’s Stone for the Transmutation of all Metals* <note>original: *Le Grand Eclaircissement de la Pierre Philosophale pour la Transmutation de tous Métaux*</note>; *Chemical Music* <note>original: *La Musique Chimique*</note>; and *Annotations on D. Zacharius, etc.* <note>original: *Annotationes in D. Zacharmin, &c.*</note>

Approaching the end of his life, and having no children, he chose his burial place in the parish church of St. Jacques-la-Boucherie, in front of the crucifix. To this end, he made a contract with the wardens of the church, which is mentioned in his testament. He then disposed of his property and goods to the church and to the poor, as may be seen in his will, which is kept in the archives of St. Jacques. It is dated November 22, 1416, and begins as follows:

> “To all those to whom these present letters shall come, I, Annegny du Castel, knight <note>original: *chevalier*</note>, counselor and chamberlain <note>original: *chambellan*</note> of the King, our Master, Keeper of the Provost <note>original: *Prevot*</note> of Paris, greetings: Know ye, that before Hugues de la Barre and Jean de la Noe, notary clerks of the King at the Châtelet, there appeared personally Nicholas Flamel, <term>scrivener</term> <note>a professional scribe, notary, or clerk</note>, sound in body and mind, speaking clearly, with good and true understanding,” etc.

The document fills four sheets of parchment, which are sewn together end-to-end like ancient scrolls. It contains thirty-four articles; in the twentieth, he bequeaths to his relatives the sum of forty livres <note>a French unit of currency</note>. He lived three years after making this will, dying about 1419.

Hostile criticism has endeavored to destroy the testimony which the history of Flamel affords to the reality of...

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page appears to be from an introductory section or a different edition (indicated by Roman numeral III), continuing the investigation into Nicholas Flamel's life, specifically focusing on the historical debate over his wealth and the legitimacy of the alchemical writings attributed to him.</meta>

<page-num>III</page-num>
<header>NICHOLAS FLAMEL.</header>

transmutation, and has adopted various means. It has attempted to disprove his wealth by downplaying his generosity, representing him simply as an honest member of the middle class <note>original: *bourgeois*</note>, who, thanks to his thrift and his diligence, acquired a comfortable income, which his childless state enabled him to devote to charitable works and to the construction of public buildings on a modest scale. The alchemical testaments and treatises attributed to him are all condemned as absolutely fake. The chief exponent of this view is the Abbé L. Vilain in his *Essay on a History of Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie* <note>original: *Essai sur une Histoire de Saint-Jacques-la-Boucherie*</note>, published in duodecimo at Paris, in 1758, and again in a *Critical History of Nicolas Flamel and of Pernel his Wife* <note>original: *Histoire Critique de Nicolas Flamel et de Pernel sa Femme*</note>, Paris, 1782, etc.

It must be admitted at once that all the alchemical compositions which have passed under the name of Flamel are more or less suspicious, and some are undoubtedly forgeries. The work on metallic transmutation, which is the earliest traceable treatise, was unheard of until one hundred and forty-three years after the death of its credited author. It was published in the year 1561 by Jacques Goharry. *The Great Clarification* <note>original: *Le Grand Eclaircissement*</note> first saw the light in 1628, when the editor, who apparently had a great number of Flamel manuscripts, promised the additional publication of *The Perfect Joy of Me, Nicolas Flamel, and of Pernelle, my Wife* <note>original: *La Joie Parfaite de Moi, Nicolas Flamel, et de Pernelle, ma Femme*</note>, which has not, however, appeared.

On the other hand, there are strong arguments for the genuineness of the *Treasure of Philosophy* <note>original: *Trésor de Philosophie*</note>. "There exists in the King's Library" <note>original: *Bibliothèque du Roi*</note>, says M. Auguste Vallet, "a small manuscript book, roughly bound <note>original: *grossement relié*</note>, which by all appearances belongs to the end of the fourteenth century, and which treats of alchemical operations. It begins with these words:—

" '<term>Excipit</term> the True Practice of the Noble Science of Alchemy, the desired desire, and the prize beyond price,

"It teaches the manner of accomplishing the *Great Work* <note>original: "Magnum Opus"</note> by the aid of successive operations, which are termed *washings* <note>original: "Lavures"</note> in this treatise. On the last leaf of the manuscript is the following inscription written by the same hand as the rest of the text:—' This book belongs to Nicolas Flamel, of the Parish of Saint-Jacques-de-la-Boucherie, who has written and illuminated <note>decorated with gold, silver, or brilliant colors</note> it with his own hand.' "

With regard to the extent of the scribe’s resources, the genuine testament of Pernelle, dated 1399, and the endowments of hospitals and churches which undoubtedly took place on a scale of great generosity, are sufficient evidence that he was an exceedingly wealthy man.

Other critics, including Louis Figuier, admit the fact of his riches but expand upon the profitable nature of a scribe's occupation before the invention of printing, and upon the careful frugality of the supposed <term>alchemist</term> <note>a practitioner of the medieval chemical science aiming to achieve the transmutation of base metals into gold</note>. However, despite their own theory, they are obliged to admit that Flamel did become a student of alchemy; that the hieroglyphics, figures, and emblems in the Cemetery of the Holy Innocents are evidence of this fact; that, unlike most followers of Hermes, he was not impoverished by his experiments; and that he encouraged the report that his wealth was mainly a result of his possession of the mysterious book of Abraham, by which he had been able to create the <term>philosophical stone</term> <note>a legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the elixir of life</note>.

Gabriel Naudé, who detested magic and seems to have despised alchemy—insulting the practitioners of both these sciences alike—accounts for Flamel's riches by asserting that he managed business affairs for the Jews. Upon their banishment from the kingdom of France and the confiscation of their property for the king, "he, knowing...

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of Nicholas Flamel's wealth, specifically addressing the theory that he profited from the persecution of the Jewish community in Paris.</meta>

### NICHOLAS FLAMEL

"...the sums owed by several individuals, he settled <note>original: "compromised"</note> by receiving a portion, which they paid him to prevent him from providing information that would force them to surrender the money entirely."

This explanation for the source of Flamel’s riches is a completely unfounded assertion. If we carefully examine history, there were three expulsions of the Jewish people from France between 1300 and 1420. They were banished in 1308, were allowed to return soon after, and were banished again in 1320. These persecutions occurred before Flamel was born. The Jewish community was re-established by Charles V in 1364, and they remained in peace until the riots that occurred in Paris in 1380, at the beginning of the reign of Charles VI. At that time, the public rose up against the Jews, committing great atrocities and demanding their expulsion. The uprising <note>original: "sedition"</note>, however, was suppressed, and the Jewish people were protected until 1393. In that year, following several charges brought against them, they were ordered to leave France or convert to Christianity. 

The historian Mezeray <note>François Eudes de Mézeray (1610–1683), a prominent French historian</note> says that some of them chose to abandon their religion rather than the kingdom, but others sold their property and departed <note>original: "retired"</note>. Thus, it appears that the only expulsion of the Jews that could align with Naudé’s <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653), a scholar and librarian who often sought to debunk supernatural claims</note> theory <note>original: "surmise"</note> occurred without the seizure <note>original: "confiscation"</note> of their property. Therefore, it could not have given Flamel the opportunity alleged—if, indeed, it were even reasonable to suppose that all the Parisian Jews <note>original: "Israelites"</note> entrusted their affairs to a single person when there is no evidence that such an agent was necessary. There is, therefore, no reason to suppose that Flamel was enriched by the property of the Jews, or that those who owed them money settled their debts <note>original: "compounded"</note> with Flamel to prevent him from denouncing them to the king.*

---

* According to Louis Figuier <note>a 19th-century French writer who popularized science and history</note>, there were two minor persecutions of the Jews: one in 1346, when Flamel was merely a boy, and the other in 1354, when he had barely established his business.

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Nicholas Flamel's character and wealth, moving from a refutation of historical theories regarding the seizure of Jewish property to the legend of his transmuting powder and the tragic fate of his descendant, Dubois.</meta>

# LIVES OF ALCHEMYSTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

Thus, the theories of hostile criticism fall apart under impartial examination. To whatever source we may choose to attribute the wealth of Nicholas Flamel, we have no reason to question his integrity or to deny the explanation given by the alchemists, except on the *a priori* grounds that transmutation is impossible.

The divine gift, which was such a fortunate possession for Flamel, is supposed to have been a curse to his descendants. He is reported to have given some of the transmuting powder to M. Perrier, a nephew of Perrenelle. From him, it descended to Dr. Perrier and was found among his belongings after his death by his grandson, Dubois. The prudence and moderation that accompanied the gift to the Perriers were not found in Dubois. He displayed the "sacred miracle" to unworthy people, says an anonymous writer on alchemy, and was brought before Louis XIII. In the king's presence, he made gold from base metal, and this gold increased its weight in the cupel <note>A cupel is a small, shallow vessel used in the process of refining and assaying precious metals.</note>. The consequence of this generosity was a shameful death. Dubois's vanity was in proportion to his recklessness. He imagined that he could create or increase the amount of the powder and promised to do so, but without success. It seems that he was, consequently, suspected of withholding the art from the king—a circumstance sufficient in politics to justify extreme measures, lest the possessor of the "sinews of war" <note>A common idiom referring to the financial resources necessary to wage war.</note> should defect to the enemy.

Whatever the charges against Dubois were, he was hanged; his fate should serve as proof, says the writer already quoted, that a science producing unlimited riches is the greatest misfortune for those who are unfit and unprepared to manage such a dangerous trust with discretion.

After the death of Flamel, many people assumed that there must undoubtedly be buried treasures in the house where he had lived for so many years, and in

<header>NICHOLAS FLAMEL.</header>

This opinion persisted in all its strength, at least in the mind of one individual, as late as the year 1576, when a stranger approached the Prévôt of Paris and stated that he had been entrusted by a deceased friend with certain sums for the restoration of Flamel’s house. As the building was exceedingly dilapidated, the magistrates took advantage of the opportunity, and repairs began under the direction of delegates of the works of Saint-Jacques-de-la-Boucherie. The true object of the stranger soon became evident by the determination with which he sought to expose the entire foundations of the house, which was ransacked from top to bottom in search of the treasures it was supposed to conceal. No discoveries rewarded the zeal of the investigation, which ended in the sudden disappearance of the stranger without paying for the operations he had initiated.

To complete the history of Flamel, it may be entertaining to quote an extraordinary account that is seriously narrated by Paul Lucas in his *Journey through Asia Minor*.

> "I was at Bronosa, in Natolia, and while going to take the air with a person of distinction, I came to a little mosque which was adorned with gardens and fountains for a public walk; we were quickly introduced into a cloister where we found four dervishes, who received us with all imaginable civility and invited us to partake of what they were eating. We were told, which we soon found to be true, that they were all persons of the greatest worth and learning; one of them, who said he was from Uzbek Tartary, appeared more accomplished than the rest, and I truly believe he spoke all the principal languages of the world. After we had conversed in Turkish, he asked me if I could speak Latin, Spanish, or Italian. I told him, if he pleased, to

He spoke to me in Italian, but he soon discovered by my accent that it was not my native language, and asked me frankly what country I came from. He replied he had never been there, but that he had a great desire to undertake the journey.

> "I did all in my power to encourage that resolution and to convince him that France was the nursery <note>original: "nursery of the learned"; meaning a place where scholarship is nurtured and fostered.</note> of the learned, and its king a patron of the sciences, who paid the expenses of my travels for collecting records of antiquities, drawings of monuments, correcting maps, and making a collection of ancient coins, manuscripts, etc., all of which he seemed to politely approve. > "On the 10th, the dervish whom I took for an Uzbek <note>original: "Usbec"</note> came to pay me a visit. He was a man every way extraordinary in learning; and in external appearance, he seemed to be about thirty years old, but from his conversation, I was persuaded he had lived a century.

> "He told me he was one of seven friends who traveled to perfect their studies and, every twenty years, met in a place previously appointed. I perceived that Bursa <note>original: "Bronosa"; referring to the city in modern-day Turkey.</note> was the place of their present meeting, and that four of them had arrived. Religion and natural philosophy <note>The study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science.</note> occupied our thoughts by turns; and at last, we fell upon chemistry, alchemy, and the Kabbalah <note>original: "Cabala"; an ancient tradition of mystical interpretation of the Bible.</note>. I told him all these, and especially the philosophers’ stone <note>A legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the "elixir of life" for immortality.</note>, were regarded by most men of sense as mere fictions."

### NICHOLAS FLAMEL.

“‘That,’ he replied, ‘should not surprise you; the sage hears the ignorant without being shocked, but does not for that reason lower his understanding to the same level. When I speak of a sage, I mean one who sees all things die and revive without being troubled: he has more riches in his power than the greatest king, but lives moderately, beyond the reach of circumstances.’

“At this I stopped him: ‘Despite all these fine sayings, the sage dies just like anyone else.’ ‘Alas!’ said he, ‘I see you are unfamiliar with the sublime science <note>original: "sublime science"; a term for alchemy and the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. Such a person as I describe does indeed die, for death is unavoidable, but he does not die before the very furthest limits of his mortal existence. Inherited disease and weakness shorten the life of man, but the sage, through the use of the true medicine <note>The Elixir of Life, believed to be a byproduct of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, can ward off whatever might hinder or damage the physical processes <note>original: "animal functions"; the biological activities of the body such as breathing, digestion, and movement.</note> for a thousand years.’

“Surprised at all I heard, I said, ‘And would you persuade me that everyone who possessed the <term>philosophers’ stone</term> lived for a thousand years?’ He replied solemnly: ‘Without a doubt, every one of them could; it depends entirely on themselves.’ At last, I took the liberty of mentioning the famous Flamel, who, it was said, possessed the philosophers’ stone, yet was certainly dead. He smiled at my naivety <note>original: "simplicity"</note> and asked with a look of amusement: ‘Do you really believe this? No, no, my friend, Flamel is still living; neither he nor his wife is dead. It has not been more than three years since I left them both in the Indies <note>Historically, this often referred to India or the East generally.</note>; he is one of my best friends.’ 

Then he told me the history of Flamel, as he had heard it from Flamel himself—the same story I had read in Flamel’s own book—up until the point when Charles VI, who was then on the throne, sent Monsieur Cramoisi, a magistrate and his Master of Requests <note>A high-ranking judicial officer in the French royal court.</note>, to ask Flamel about the source of his wealth. land to await his arrival, he spread a report of his wife’s death, had her funeral celebrated, and in a few years ordered his own coffin to be buried. Since that time they have both lived a philosophical life, sometimes in one country, sometimes in another. This is the true history, and not that which is believed in Paris, where there are very few who ever had even the slightest glimpse of true wisdom.' "

According to the "Treasure of Philosophy," alchemy as a science consists of the knowledge of the four elements of philosophers, which are not to be identified with the common so-called elements, and which are convertible one into another. The true *prima materia* <note>First matter; the primitive, formless base of all matter.</note> is mercury, prepared and solidified in the depths of the earth through the influence of the heat of sulphur. This is the seed and semen of all metals, which, like other created things, are capable of growth and multiplication that may be continued even to infinity. The first step in transmutation is the reduction of the metals worked upon into their original mercurial matter, and this reduction is the subject of the entire treatise.

It does not appear that the alchemical works attributed to Nicholas Flamel have added anything to our knowledge of chemistry. On the other hand, it is perfectly clear from his history that the physical goal of Alchemy was the end which he kept in view, and also that which he is supposed to have achieved.

## PETER BONO.

This adept, born in Lombardy, was a resident of Pola, a seaport of Istria, where he claims that he created the much desired transmuting metal of the sages in the year 1330.

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Peter Bono’s alchemical contributions before introducing the life and works of Johannes de Rupecissa.</meta>

He wrote and published a complete treatise on the art under the title *Margarita Pretiosa*. Lacinius, a monk of Calabria, has printed a faithful abridgment of it, which appeared at Venice in 1546. An *Introductio in Artem Divinam Alchimiæ*, 1602, and *De Secreto Omnium Secretorum*, Venice, 1546, are attributed to this adept.

The first of these works is an exceedingly comprehensive, conscientious treatise on the history, the theory, and the practice of alchemy, written in the style of the scholastics, and naturally containing much irrelevant matter, but despite all this, it is very useful and even interesting. The difficulties of the art are manfully faced, the trickery, deceptions, and contradictions of its professors are reproved, and the author attempts to show that alchemy is in reality a short art and a simple practice, though full of truth and nobility. His other opinions are also of a revolutionary character.

## JOHANNES DE RUPECISSA.

This writer is considered one of the most remarkable of the Hermetic philosophers. He abounds with prophetic passages and denounces the fate of nations, but in his alchemical explanation of physical things, he is obscure even for an adept. Nothing is known of his life <note>He is said to have been a French monk of the order of St. Francis.</note> beyond the nobility of his origin and his imprisonment in 1357 by Pope Innocent VI, whom he had criticized. The illustrious Montfaucon was one of his descendants, and he presents himself as an initiate of the secret chemistry in the following works: "The Book of Light," "The Five Essences," *Cælum Philosophorum*, and his most celebrated treatise *De Confectione Lapidis*. There he declares that the matter of the philosophers' stone is a viscous water which is to be

### BASIL VALENTINE.

One of the most illustrious of the adept philosophers is undoubtedly Basil Valentine (Basilius Valentinus), who was born at Mainz <note>original: "Mayence"</note> and became the prior of St. Peter's at Erfurt in 1414. His name was suspected to be a pseudonym used to conceal the identity of some skilled practitioner <note>original: "accomplished artist"; in alchemy, "artist" refers to one who practices the "Great Work"</note>, but the history of the city of Erfurt, published by J. M. Gudemus, assures us of the philosopher's existence and name based on public records. Gudemus shows us that in 1413 he was a resident of the monastic house already mentioned and that he distinguished himself through a profound knowledge of nature. <note>original Latin footnote: *Eadem ætate (scilicet anno 1413) Basilius Valentinus in divi Patri monasteris vixit arte medica et naturale indagatione admirabilis.* Translation: "At that same time (namely in the year 1413), Basil Valentine lived in the monastery of Saint Peter, remarkable for his medical skill and his investigation of nature."</note> Since the work of Gudemus was printed in 1675, the accuracy <note>original: "veracity"</note> of the *Dictionary of the Occult Sciences* (*Dictionnaire des Sciences Occultes*)—a work written in the interests of religion and for the purpose of discrediting <note>original: "blackening"</note> the secret sciences—may be judged by the following passage: “His life is so mixed with fables that some have disbelieved in his existence. He is portrayed as flourishing in the twelfth, thirteenth, fourteenth, and fifteenth centuries; it is even added, *without the smallest proof*, that he was a Benedictine at Erfurt.”

According to Olaus Borrichius, Valentine hid his writings inside one of the pillars of the abbey church. They remained in this hiding place for many years, but were at length
