<meta>This page continues the biography of Basil Valentine, discussing his legendary discovery of antimony and listing his primary alchemical and medical publications.</meta>

->BASIL VALENTINE.<-

...discovered by the lucky strike of a lightning bolt. He was the first person to introduce antimony into medicine, and it is said that he originally tested the effects of antimonial medicines upon the monks of his convent. These medicines acted upon them with such excessive violence "that he was led to name the mineral from which these medicines had been extracted *antimoine* — meaning 'hostile to monks' <note>original French: "antimoine"</note>." However, Thomson, who tells this story in his *History of Chemistry*, shows how unlikely it is; the works of Basil Valentine, and specifically his *The Triumphant Chariot of Antimony* <note>original Latin: "Currus Triumphalis Antimonii"</note>, were written in German <note>original: "High Dutch"</note>. In his time, the German name for antimony was *speissglas* <note>original German: "speissglas"</note> and not *antimoine*, which is French.

Basil Valentine attacks the physicians of his time with the same intensity as Paracelsus <note>Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a famous physician and alchemist known for his fiery personality and rejection of traditional medicine</note>. The most ancient systems of chemical philosophy are preserved in his experiments. He praises antimony as an excellent medicine for those who are familiar with alchemical secrets. To others, it is a poison of the most powerful kind.

No further details of the life of Basil Valentine have been passed down to future generations. Numerous works have been printed in his name, though the authenticity of several is doubtful. He wrote in German, and relatively few of his treatises have been translated into other languages. The best are as follows:

1. *On the Microcosm, the Great Mystery of the World, and the Medicine of Man* <note>original Latin: "De Microcosmo deque Magno Mundi Mysterio et Medecina Hominis"</note>, Marpurg, 1609, octavo; 
2. *Azoth, or the Golden Wisdom of the Philosophers* <note>original Latin: "Azoth, sive Aurelia Philosophorum"</note>, Frankfurt, 1613, quarto; 
3. *Practice, together with Twelve Keys and an Appendix* <note>original Latin: "Practica, unà cum duodecim Clavibus et Appendice"</note>, Frankfurt, 1611, quarto; 
4. *The Chemical Revelation* <note>original Latin: "Apocalypsis Chymica"</note>, Erfurt, 1624, octavo; 
5. *A Manifestation of Artifice* <note>original Latin: "Manifestatio Artificiorum"</note>, Erfurt, 1624, octavo; 
6. *The Triumphant Chariot of Antimony* <note>original Latin: "Currus Triumphalis Antimonii"</note>, Leipzig, 1624, octavo; 
7. *A Chemical-Philosophical Treatise on Natural and Supernatural Things, Metals, and Minerals* <note>original Latin: "Tractatus Chimico-Philosophicus de Rebus Naturalibus et Præternaturalibus metallorum et mineralium"</note>, Frankfurt, 1676, octavo; 
8. *Haliographia, on the preparation—* <note>original Latin: "Haliographia, de præpara-"; Haliographia refers to the description of salts</note>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Basil Valentine’s written works, focusing on his "Triumphal Chariot of Antimony" and his significant contributions to early physical chemistry.</meta>

# LIVES OF ALCHEMISTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

*...tione, usu, ac virtutibus omnium Salium Mineralium, Animalium, ac Vegetabilium, ex manuscriptis Basilii Valentini collecta ab Ant. Salmincio, Bologna, 1644, 8vo.* <note>The use and virtues of all Mineral, Animal, and Vegetable Salts, collected from the manuscripts of Basil Valentine by Ant. Salmincio, Bologna, 1644, octavo.</note>

Every letter and syllable of the “Triumphal Chariot of Antimony” is declared to have its special significance. “Even to the points and marks,” it bristles with divine meanings and mysteries. The metrical treatise on the first matter of the philosophers declares that this stone is composed of white and red, that it is a stone, and yet hardly a stone; a single nature operates within it. Those who desire to achieve this, Basil informs us elsewhere, must work with much prayer, confess their sins, and do good. Many are called, but few are chosen for this supreme knowledge. Both the study of the works of the philosophers and practical experimentation are recommended. There is much in the writings of Basil—in his suggestive, though impenetrable, allegories, in his curious Kabbalistic symbols, and in his earnest spirituality—to suggest a psychic interpretation of his aims and principles. This is particularly noticeable in the “Triumphal Chariot of Antimony,” and yet it is clear from this remarkable work, which is the author's masterpiece, that Basil Valentine was one of the most illustrious physical chemists of his age. He was the first to describe the extraction of <term>antimony</term> from the <term>sulfide</term> <note>originally "sulphuret"</note>, though it does not appear that he was the inventor of this process. Prior to his investigations, the properties of antimony were almost unknown. He was also acquainted with the method of obtaining <term>hydrochloric acid</term> <note>originally "chlorohydric acid"</note> from sea salt and sulfuric acid, with the method of obtaining brandy by the distillation of beer and wine and the <term>purification</term> <note>originally "rectification"</note> of the result by means of carbonate of potassium, and with many other operations which greatly aided the progress of chemistry.

<summary>This page evaluates Basil Valentine’s "Triumphal Chariot of Antimony," noting its dual nature as both a spiritual, symbolic text and a foundational work of physical chemistry. It highlights his discoveries regarding antimony, hydrochloric acid, and the distillation of alcohol.</summary>
<keywords>Basil Valentine, Triumphal Chariot of Antimony, alchemy, antimony, sulfide, hydrochloric acid, distillation, chemistry, Kabbalistic symbols, spiritual alchemy</keywords>

<header>ISAAC OF HOLLAND.</header>

# ISAAC OF HOLLAND.

Contemporary with Basil Valentine were Isaac of Holland and his son, who are supposed to have worked with success. They were the first alchemists of Holland, and their operations were highly esteemed by Paracelsus, Boyle, and Kunckel. In practical chemistry, they followed the traditions of Geber, and their alchemical experiments are the plainest and most explicit in the entire range of Hermetic literature. They worked mainly with metals, describing every detail of each process minutely. Their lives are almost unknown. “Buried in the obscurity necessary to adepts, they were occupied in the practice of the Hermetic science, and their study or laboratory was the daily scene of their industrious existence.” *

They are placed in the fifteenth century by conjecture, because they do not cite any philosophers after that period. They speak of Geber, Dastin, Morien, and Arnold, but not of more modern authorities; while, on the other hand, their references to <term>aquafortis</term> and <term>aqua-regia</term>, which were discovered in the fourteenth century, prevent us from assigning their labors to an earlier era.

The two Isaacs were particularly skilled in the manufacture of enamels and artificial gemstones. They taught that the <term>Grand Magisterium</term> could convert a million times its own weight into gold, and declared that any person taking a small portion of the <term>philosopher’s stone</term> weekly will be forever preserved in perfect health, and his life will be prolonged to the very last hour which God has assigned to him.

The *Opera Mineralia Joannis Isaaci Hollandi, sive de Lapide Philosophico* <note>Mineral Works of John Isaac of Holland, or Concerning the Philosopher's Stone</note> is a long and elaborate treatise on the

---

* “Lives of Alchemical Philosophers.” Ed. of 1815.

They are noted for their skill in creating enamels and artificial gems, and for their writings on the philosopher's stone.</summary>

## BERNARD TRÉVISAN.

Bernard, Count of the March of Trévisan <note>original: "Bernard Compte de la Marche Trévisane"</note>, is credited by the popular legends of France with the powers of a sorcerer in possession of a "devil's bird" or familiar spirit; nevertheless, he is called “the good” and enjoyed a particular reputation for benevolence.

A descendant of a distinguished family from Padua, Bernard Trévisan began to study the time-honored science of alchemy around the same time that Basil <note>likely referring to Basil Valentine</note> in Germany and the two Isaacs <note>Isaac Hollandus and his son</note> in Holland were pursuing their labors with supposed success. His father was a physician in Padua, where Bernard himself was born in the year 1406. The account of his alchemical errors must rank among the most curious anecdotes in the annals of occult chemistry.

At the age of fourteen, under the sponsorship of his grandfather and with the full consent of his family, he devoted his attention to alchemy, which from then on became the absorbing occupation of his life. Seeking initiation into the first principles of the art, he began by studying Geber and Rhasis <note>referring to Jabir ibn Hayyan and Al-Razi, influential early Islamic alchemists</note>, believing they would provide him with a method of multiplying his inheritance <note>original: "patrimony"</note> a hundredfold. The experiments which he undertook during his costly instruction by these authoritative <note>original: "oracular"</note> masters resulted in the useless waste <note>original: "futile dissipation"</note> of eight hundred—or, according to another account, three thousand—crowns. He was surrounded by pretended philosophers who, finding him wealthy and eager to penetrate these tantalizing mysteries, offered...

Disappointed, but not discouraged, he eventually dismissed these impostors and devoted his concentrated attention to the works of <term>Rupecissa</term> and <term>Archelaus Sacrobosco</term>, whom he followed literally for a time in all his practical operations. Hoping to profit from the help of a prudent companion, he associated himself with a good monk with whom he experimented together for the space of three years. They rectified <term>spirits of wine</term> more than thirty times "until they could not find glasses strong enough to hold it." These operations cost nearly three hundred crowns.

For fifteen years he continued his preliminary experiments, and by the end of that time he had acquired a thorough knowledge of all the tricks and deceptions of alchemical rogues, and was intimately acquainted with an enormous variety of substances—mineral, metallic, and otherwise—which apparently did not belong in the composition of the <term>stone philosophical</term> <note>the philosopher's stone</note>. He calculated the cost of these experiments to have been roughly six thousand crowns. He had labored in vain to congeal, dissolve, and sublime common salt, <term>sal ammoniac</term>, every variety of <term>alum</term>, and <term>copperas</term> <note>iron sulfate</note>. He even worked with <term>ordure</term> <note>excrement</note>, both from humans and beasts, through <term>distillation</term>, <term>circulation</term>, and <term>sublimation</term>. These experiments, based on the literal interpretation of the allegories of the *<term>turba philosophorum</term>* <note>The Assembly of the Philosophers, an early alchemical text</note>, again resulted in failure; at last, discouraged beyond words by the loss of his time and his fortune, he turned to prayer, hoping to discover the goal of the alchemists through the grace and favor of God. In conjunction with a magistrate of his country, he subsequently endeavored to create the <term>philosophical stone</term> using <term>sea salt</term> as the chief ingredient. He rectified it fifteen times during the space of a year and

These dissolutions, undertaken separately, were left to themselves for a year, and then combined and concentrated over hot ashes to reduce their original volume to two-thirds. The residue of this operation, placed in a narrow crucible, was exposed to the action of the solar rays, and afterwards to the air, in the hope that it would crystallize. Twenty-two phials were filled with the mixture, and five years were devoted to the whole operation, but at the end of that period no crystallization had taken place, and thus was this operation abandoned, like the rest, as a failure.

Bernard Trévisan was now forty-six years old, and at the end of his experimental resources he determined to travel in search of true alchemists. In this manner he met with a monk of Citeaux, Maître Geofroi de Lemorier, who was in possession of a previously unheard-of process. They purchased two thousand hens’ eggs, hardened them in boiling water, and removed the shells, which they <term>calcined</term> <note>reduced to powder by heat</note> in a fire. They separated the whites from the yolks, which they putrefied in horse manure. The result was distilled thirty separate times for the extraction of a white and red water. These operations were continually repeated with many variations, and vainly occupied eight years more of the toil-worn seeker’s life.

Disappointed, disheartened, but still stubbornly adhering to his search after the **Grand Secret**, Trévisan now set to work with a <term>protonotary</term> <note>a high-ranking notary or legal official</note> of Bruges, whom he describes as a great theologian, and who pretended to extract the stone from <term>sulphate of iron</term> (<term>copperas</term>) by distillation with vinegar. They began by <term>calcining</term> the sulphate for three months, when it was soaked in the

At the end of this period, the seeker was rewarded only by a <term>quartan fever</term> <note>A form of malaria where the fever returns every four days.</note> which consumed him for fourteen months and almost cost him his life.

He had hardly recovered his health when he heard from a clerk that Maître Henry, the confessor of the German Emperor Frederick III, was in possession of the philosopher's stone. He immediately set out for Germany, accompanied by some frustrated <term>sons of Hermes</term> <note>A traditional name for alchemists, who looked to the legendary Hermes Trismegistus as their founding figure.</note> like himself. They managed, **through great means and great friends** <note>original: "par grands moyens et grands amis"</note>, to be introduced to the confessor and began to work alongside him. Bernard contributed ten marks of silver, and the others contributed thirty-two, to cover the essential expenses of the process. This process consisted of combining mercury, silver, olive oil, and sulfur. The entire mixture was dissolved over a moderate fire and stirred continually. After two months, it was placed in a glass vial, which they covered with clay and then with hot ashes. Lead, dissolved in a crucible, was added after three weeks, and the product of this melting was subjected to refinement. At the end of these operations, the imperial confessor expected that the silver used in the mixture would have increased by at least a third; on the contrary, however, it was reduced to a fourth of its original amount.

Bernard Trévisan, in utter despair, determined to abandon all further experiments. His family applauded this resolution, but within two months, the <term>Circean</term> <note>Refers to the enchantress Circe from Greek mythology; here meaning an irresistible or intoxicating power.</note> power of secret chemistry had re-established its former command over the whole being of its martyr. In a fever of eagerness, he restarted his travels and visited Spain, Italy, England, Scotland, Holland, Germany, and France. Then, anxious to drink at the Eastern fountains of alchemy, he spent several years in Egypt, Persia, and

<meta>This page continues the biography of Bernard Trévisan, detailing his travels through the Middle East and Greece, his financial ruin in the pursuit of alchemy, and his eventual discovery of the philosopher's stone in his old age.</meta>

Palestine, after which he traveled into southern Greece, visiting remote convents and experimenting alongside monks who had reputations in the science. In every country, he found alchemists at work, but he could hear no account of those who were successful. The true philosophers refused to make themselves known, while impostors, seeking the gullible, appeared on all sides. Bernard spent about thirteen thousand crowns on these travels and the failed operations connected with them; he was forced to sell an estate that had yielded eight thousand German florins per year. He was now sixty-two years old, and because he had been deaf to the warnings of his family, he found himself despised and on the threshold of poverty and misery. He tried to hide his poverty and chose the Isle of Rhodes as a place to live entirely unknown. 

Now, at Rhodes, he became acquainted with *a great cleric and monk* <note>original: "un grand clerc et religieux"</note> who was devoted to philosophy and commonly reported to possess the philosopher's stone. He managed to borrow eight thousand florins and worked with this monk on the dissolution of gold, silver, and <term>corrosive sublimate</term> <note>mercuric chloride, a substance frequently used in alchemical processes</note>; he achieved so much in the space of three years that he spent the funds he had raised and was once again at the end of his resources. Thus, effectively prevented from continuing the practice, he returned to the study of the philosophers, and after eight years—at the age of seventy-three—he claimed to have discovered their secret. By comparing the <term>adepts</term> <note>those who have mastered the secrets of alchemy</note> and examining the points on which they agree and differ, he judged that the truth must lie in those principles where they were practically unanimous. He informs us that it was two years before he put his discovery to the test; it was crowned with success, and despite the weaknesses of old age, he lived for some time enjoying his long-delayed reward.

<summary>After decades of costly travel and failed experiments that left him in poverty, Bernard Trévisan finally discovered the secret of the philosopher's stone at the age of seventy-three by identifying the common principles among previous alchemical masters.</summary>

<keywords>Bernard Trevisan, Palestine, Greece, Isle of Rhodes, philosopher's stone, corrosive sublimate, alchemical adepts, transmutation, financial ruin</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page concludes the overview of Bernard Trévisan's alchemical theories and begins the biography of John Fontaine, a 15th-century alchemist and poet.</meta>

The main work of Trévisan is *La Philosophie Naturelle des Métaux*. He insists on the necessity of deep and careful meditation for all students of Hermetic philosophy. Their operations must follow nature, and nature must not follow their arbitrary processes. Mercury is said to be the water of metals, "in which, through a mutual change, it assumes their transformations in a reversible way." Gold is simply mercury solidified by the power of sulfur. The secret of dissolution is the entire mystery of the craft, and it is to be achieved not by means of fire, as some have supposed, but with the help of mercury in an obscure manner that is not truly explained by the <term>adept</term> <note>a master of alchemical secrets</note>. The work of nature is assisted by alchemy, which mixes refined gold with mercury; the gold contains within itself a well-refined sulfur, by which it matures the mercury to the "anatide proportion" of gold, refining the elements and wonderfully shortening the natural process for producing the precious metal found in mines.

## JOHN FONTAINE.

The life of this artist is hidden in the privacy of his study or laboratory, where he divided his time between attending to his furnaces and writing unusual verses. He lived in Valenciennes in the year 1413. His Hermetic poem, *Aux Amoureux de Science*, has been printed several times. The author announces that he is a master of the art and describes the various processes involved in the art of transmutation in an allegorical manner, following the style of the "Romance of the Rose" and using the same charming and beautiful language. His small work may be studied with profit by the beginners of practical alchemy, although its benefits are of a negative kind; however, its collection of elegant devices and its old-world

## THOMAS NORTON.

The scientific methods of Ripley were followed by this alchemist, who was born in the city of Bristol. He wrote anonymously, but the initial syllables in the first six lines, and the first line in the seventh chapter of his "*Ordinall of Alchemy*," compose the following couplet:—

->“Thomas Norton of Bristol,<-
->A perfect master you may him trust.”<-

At the age of twenty-eight, and in the brief space of forty days, he is recorded to have mastered “the perfection of <term>chymistry</term>,” obtaining his knowledge from a contemporary <term>adept</term>, who appears to have been Ripley himself. He describes his initiator as a person of noble mind, worthy of all praise, loving justice, detesting fraud, reserved when surrounded by a talkative company, quite unassuming, and if ever the conversation turned upon the <term>Great Art</term>, preserving complete silence. For a long time Norton sought him in vain; the <term>adept</term> proved him by various trials, but when he was satisfied of his disposition, manners, and habits, as well as of his strength of mind, his love yielded to the fidelity and perseverance of his <term>postulant</term>, and in answer to one of his letters he addressed him as follows:—

> “MY TRUSTED AND WELL-BELOVED BROTHER,—I shall no longer delay; the time has come; you shall receive this grace. Your honest desire and approved virtue, your love of truth, wisdom, and long perseverance, shall fulfill your earnest desires.
> 
> “It is necessary that, as soon as convenient, we speak

# THOMAS NORTON.

Norton lost no time in undertaking a journey to his instructor and rode upward of a hundred miles on horseback to reach the abode of the adept. During the forty days already mentioned, he received the advice and directions of his friend. He was already to a great extent prepared for initiation by a long course of natural philosophy, as well as by the study of the occult and curious sciences. The “disclosure of the bonds of nature” took place, and he became convinced of the truth and certainty of the art by the rationality of its theorems. He felt confident of success in the practice, but the adept, on account of Norton's youth, refused to instruct him in the process from the <term>white to the red powder</term>, lest the divine gift should be misused in a moment of passion. In due time, and after further proofs of his capacity and integrity, he would communicate the work of the <term>medicinal stone</term>. This, the supreme desire of the neophyte, was afterward accomplished.

The chemical operations of Norton were destined, however, to meet with two signal disappointments. He had almost perfected the <term>tincture</term> when his own servant, who was employed in the care of the furnace, believing that the prize was complete, carried it away. He again undertook the process and succeeded in making the <term>elixir</term>, but he complains that it was stolen by the wife of a merchant, said to be William Canning, Mayor of Bristol, who suddenly came into great wealth and who built the splendid and lofty steeple of St Mary Redcliffe, besides enlarging Westbury College.

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the English alchemist Thomas Norton, author of the "Ordinal of Alchemy," focusing on his reputation, family, and the nature of his alchemical teachings.</meta>

# LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS

It is doubtful whether Thomas Norton ever enjoyed the fruits of his supposed knowledge. He does not speak of his own transmutations <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals into gold or silver.</note>, and if he is called by one of his contemporaries **the most skilled alchemist of his time** <note>original: *alchemista suo tempore peritissimus*</note>, by others he is termed a **maker of trifles in a frivolous science** <note>original: *Nugarum opifex in frivola scientia*</note>. The latter critics declare that he ruined himself by his labors, and that all his friends who trusted him with their money were as much ruined as he was. According to Fuller <note>Thomas Fuller (1608–1661), a historian famous for his biographical dictionary, "Worthies of England."</note>, he lived and died very poor; nevertheless, his family appears to have been held in high repute under King Henry VIII. There were nine brothers with the name of Norton. One anonymous writer asserts that they were all knights. The tomb of Sampson Norton, master of the king’s ordnance <note>The official in charge of the royal artillery and weaponry.</note>, who was buried in Fulham Church, was adorned with Hermetic <note>Related to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy; often refers to symbolic or alchemical art.</note> paintings according to one account. However, Faulkner, in his historical account of Fulham, describes it as a rich Gothic monument, ornamented with foliage and oak leaves, and bearing an obliterated inscription.

Thomas Norton died in 1477. His grandson Samuel followed in his steps as an alchemist and was the author of several Hermetic treatises, which are not very highly esteemed.

> *The Ordinal of Alchemy* testifies that the stone <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the legendary substance capable of transmutation.</note> is one. In appearance, it is a subtle earth, brown and opaque; it withstands the fire and is considered to be of no value. There is also another and glorious stone, which is termed the **philosophical magnesia** <note>In alchemy, "magnesia" did not refer to the chemical element magnesium, but rather a symbolic "mother" substance or a specific stage of the prime matter.</note>. Alchemy is a wonderful science, a secret philosophy, a unique grace and free gift of the Almighty, which was never discovered by independent human labor, but only by revelation or the instruction of one of the adepts <note>An "adept" is someone who has mastered the secrets of alchemy.</note>.

> “It helps a man when he has need,
> It removes vain glory, hope, and also dread:

## THOMAS DALTON.

The only account of this English <term>adept</term> is preserved by Thomas Norton. He was alive in the year 1450 and is described as a religious man who enjoyed a good reputation until—upon suspicion that he possessed a large mass of transmuting powder—he was taken from his abbey in Gloucestershire by Thomas Herbert, one of the squires of King Edward. Being brought into the royal presence, he was confronted by Debois, another of the king's squires, for whom Dalton had formerly served as a chaplain. Debois alleged that Dalton, in less than twelve hours, had made him a thousand pounds of good gold, and he attested to this fact under oath. Then Dalton, looking at Debois, said, “Sir, you are forsworn.” Debois acknowledged that he had vowed never to reveal the benefit he had received, but argued that for the king's sake and the good of the commonwealth, he should not keep his oath. Dalton now addressed the king and informed him that he had received the <term>powder of projection</term> from a canon of Lichfield, on the condition that he refrain from using it until after the donor's death. Since that event, he had been in so much danger and anxiety because of its possession that he had destroyed it in secret. The king dismissed Dalton, giving him four marks for his traveling expenses; but Herbert lay in wait for him

Dalton is accused of creating gold but claims he destroyed his transmuting powder because the danger of possessing it was too great.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the account of the alchemist Thomas Dalton and begins the biography of Sir George Ripley.</meta>

# LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

brought him from Stepney and from there transported him to the castle of Gloucester, where every means was vainly tried to induce him to produce the philosophers' tincture.

After four years of imprisonment, Dalton was brought out to be beheaded in the presence of Herbert. He obeyed with resignation and joy, saying: "Blessed are You, Lord Jesus! I have been away from You too long; the science You gave me I have kept without abusing it; I have found no one suitable to be my heir; therefore, sweet Lord, I will return Your gift to You again."

Then, after a devout prayer, with a smiling face he asked the executioner to proceed. Tears gushed from Herbert's eyes when he saw him so willing to die and realized that no ingenuity could force his secret from him. He ordered his release. His imprisonment and threatened execution had been plotted without the king's knowledge to frighten him into compliance. Since these wicked schemes failed, Herbert did not dare to take his life. Dalton rose from the block with a sorrowful face and returned to his abbey, much grieved by the further extension of his earthly stay. Herbert died shortly after this atrocious act of tyranny, and Debois also met an untimely end. His father, Sir John Debois, was killed at the Battle of Tewkesbury on May 4, 1471; and two days later, as recorded in Stow's *Annals*, he himself, James Debois, was taken with several others of the Lancastrian party from a church where they had fled for sanctuary and was beheaded on the spot.<note>Stow, "*Annals of England*," p. 424, 1615 edition.</note>

This illustrious alchemical philosopher, whose works paved the royal road to the initiation, in later times, of his

<page-num>135</page-num>
->SIR GEORGE RIPLEY.<-

still more illustrious pupil, the sublime and mysterious Philalethes, entered, at an early age, among the regular canons of Bridlington, in the diocese of York. The tranquility of monastic life afforded him a favorable opportunity for the study of the great masters in transcendental chemistry, but he nonetheless found himself unable to fully comprehend them; in the resulting disappointment, he determined to travel, persuading himself that he would discover in the conversations of philosophers what he could not gather from books.

In Italy, Germany, and France, he became acquainted with various men of learning and was present at a transmutation which was performed in Rome. He afterwards proceeded to the island of Rhodes, where a document is supposed to exist testifying that he gave £100,000 to the Knights of St John of Jerusalem. He was honored with a dignity by the Pope, a fact which, upon his return to Bridlington, excited the jealousy of his brethren; in consequence of their hostility, he entered the Carmelite order at Butolph, in Lincolnshire, and, by an indulgence from Innocent VIII, had permission to live in solitude, exempt from monastic observances. In his now uninterrupted leisure, he wrote twenty-four books—some scientific and others on devout subjects. He composed “The Twelve Gates of Alchemy” in 1471, and he declares that any of his experiments recorded from 1450 to 1470 should be entirely discredited, as he wrote them based on theory and found afterwards through practice that they were untrue. From this, it may be concluded that he spent twenty years in mastering the secrets of the science. He died at Butolph in 1490.

“The Twelve Gates of Alchemy” describes the stone as a triune microcosm, for which reason Ripley has been cited as an adept of spiritual chemistry. He insists upon the

### PICUS DE MIRANDOLA.

John Picus, Earl of Mirandola, was born on February 24, 1463. He is equally celebrated for his precocity, the extent of his learning, his extraordinary memory, and his penetrating intellect. As the pupil of Jochanum, a Jew, he was early initiated into the Kabbalistic interpretation of Scripture, and at the age of twenty-four years he published nine hundred propositions in logic, mathematics, physics, divinity, and Kabbalism, collected from Greek, Latin, Jewish, and Arabian writers. In his treatise *De Auro*, he records his conviction of the success of Hermetic operations, and gives us the following accounts:

> “I now come to declare what I have seen of this miracle, without veil or ambiguity. One of my friends, who is still living, has made gold and silver over sixty times in my presence. I have seen it performed in various ways, but the expense of making the silver with a metallic water exceeded the value produced.”

In another place he tells us that “a good man who did not have enough to support his family was reduced to the last extremity of distress; with a troubled mind he went to sleep one night, and in a dream he saw a blessed angel, who, by means of riddles, instructed him in the method of making gold, and indicated to him, at the same time, the water he should use to ensure success. Upon waking, he proceeded to work with this water, and made

He convinced me by the evidence of my own eyes that the art of transmutation is no fiction.

# PARACELSUS.

Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombast von Hohenheim was born in the year 1493, at Maria Einsiedeln, in the canton of Zurich, Switzerland. He was descended from the ancient and honorable family of Bombast, which had lived for many generations at the Castle of Hohenheim near Stuttgart, Würtemberg. His father was a physician of high reputation and possessed a large collection of rare books. His mother had been the matron of a hospital, and Theophrastus, their only child, was born one year after their marriage. It is said that he was castrated in his infancy, a tradition which may have been invented to account for his beardless and feminine appearance, and for his reported hatred of women.

Paracelsus received the first basics of his education from his father. As he advanced in his studies and abilities, he was instructed in alchemy, surgery, and medicine. One of the works of Isaac Holland <note>Isaac Holland (and his son John Isaac) were influential 15th-century Dutch alchemists whose writings focused on the philosopher's stone</note> fell into his hands, and from that moment he was driven by the ambition of curing diseases with medicines superior to the medical materials <note>original: "materia" (short for *materia medica*, the standard pharmaceutical substances of the time)</note> then in use. <insert>[</insert> He performed several chemical operations according to the books of the celebrated Hollander and adopted from his writings the ancient principles that salt, mercury, and sulfur form a "trinity" in every substance. <insert>]</insert> This system he expanded and explained through his own intellectual insights. He absorbed much of his father's extensive learning and then continued his studies under the guidance of monks in the Convent of Saint Andrew of

<summary>This page introduces the early life and education of Paracelsus, born in 1493, detailing his family background and his early fascination with the alchemical theories of Isaac Holland.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, alchemy, medicine, Isaac Holland, Maria Einsiedeln, transmutation, salt-mercury-sulfur trinity, history of science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Paracelsus, detailing his education under Trithemius, his travels through Europe and the East, his acquisition of alchemical secrets in Constantinople, and his controversial career at the University of Basel.</meta>

# LIVES OF ALCHEMYSTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

Savon, later at the University of Basel, and finally devoted himself to the occult sciences with the illustrious Johann Trithemius, Abbot of Spanheim, as his teacher and director. In this way he acquired "the Kabbalah of the spiritual, astral, and material worlds." He was later placed under the care of Sigismund Hagger or Fagger, to be improved in medicine, surgery, and chemistry. At twenty years of age he started on his travels through Germany, Hungary, Italy, France, the Netherlands, Denmark, Sweden, and Russia. In Muscovy he is said to have been taken prisoner by the Tartars, who brought him before "the great Khan" <note>original: "Cham" (an archaic form of Khan)</note>. His knowledge of medicine and chemistry made him a favorite at the court of this potentate, who sent him in company with his son on an embassy to Constantinople. It was here, according to Helmont, that he was taught the supreme secret of alchemy by a generous Arabian, who gave him the universal dissolvent, the Azoth of Western adepts, the alkahest <note>original: "alcahest"</note> or sophic fire. Thus initiated, he is said to have proceeded to India. On his return to Europe he passed along the Danube into Italy, where he served as an army surgeon, performing many wonderful cures.

<insert>
At the age of thirty-two he re-entered Germany, and was soon after invited to take a professorship of physic, medicine, and surgery at the University of Basel, then illuminated by the presence of Erasmus and Oporinus. There, in his lectures, he professed "internal medicine," denounced the antiquated systems of Galen and other authorities, and began his instruction by burning the works of these masters in a brass pan with sulfur and nitre. He created innumerable enemies through his arrogance and his innovations, but the value of his mineral medicines was proved by the cures which he performed.
</insert>

<summary>Paracelsus traveled across Europe and the East to learn the secrets of alchemy and medicine, eventually returning to Basel as a controversial professor who publicly rejected traditional medical authorities. Despite the enmity caused by his radical methods and arrogance, his mineral-based cures proved highly effective.</summary>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Paracelsus, specifically focusing on his confrontational relationship with the medical establishment, his travels after leaving Basel, and the varying accounts of his death.</meta>

# PARACELSUS.

With characteristic defiance, he invited the faculty to a lecture in which he promised to teach the greatest secret in medicine. He began by uncovering a dish that contained excrement. The doctors, indignant at the insult, departed hastily, while Paracelsus shouted after them: “If you will not hear the mysteries of putrefactive fermentation, you are unworthy of the name of physicians.” Subsequently, he came into conflict with the municipal authorities and was forced to flee from Basel. He resumed his wandering life, staying at public inns and drinking to excess, yet still performing admirable cures. Oporinus testifies that even during the period of his professorship, he never seemed sober.

In 1528, Paracelsus proceeded to Colmar. In 1530 he was staying at Nuremberg, where the medical faculty denounced him as an impostor, but he stunned his opponents by curing several desperate cases of elephantiasis in just a few days. “Testimonials to this effect,” says Hartmann, his latest biographer, “may still be found in the archives of the city of Nuremberg.” He continued his travels and his intemperate lifestyle, dying on September 24, 1541.

The actual manner of his death has been described in various ways. The original “Lives of Alchemysticall Philosophers” says that it occurred on a bench by the kitchen fire of the inn at Strasburg. Dr. Hartmann, on the other hand, tells us that he “went to Maehren <note>Moravia</note>, Kaernthen <note>Carinthia</note>, Krain <note>Carniola</note>, and Hungary, and finally landed in Salzburg, to which place he was invited by the Prince Palatine, Duke Ernst of Bavaria, who was a great lover of the secret arts. In that place, Paracelsus finally obtained the fruits of his long labors and widespread fame. But he was not destined to enjoy the rest he so richly deserved for very long. . . . He died after a short illness (at the age

<summary>Paracelsus left Basel in disgrace after insulting the faculty, but he continued to perform remarkable medical cures in Nuremberg before his death in 1541. Biographers provide conflicting accounts of his final days, placing his death either in a Strasburg inn or under the patronage of Duke Ernst in Salzburg.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Basel, Nuremberg, elephantiasis, Oporinus, Hartmann, Strasburg, Salzburg, Duke Ernst of Bavaria, putrefactive fermentation</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the biographical sketch of Paracelsus and begins the account of Denis Zachaire.</meta>

->LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS<-

at the age of forty-eight years and three days, in a small room of the ‘White Horse’ Inn, near the quay, and his body was buried in the graveyard of St. Sebastian.” His death is supposed to have been hastened by a scuffle with assassins in the pay of the traditional medical establishment <note>original: "orthodox medical faculty"</note>.

The most recent commentator on Paracelsus, Dr. Franz Hartmann, has devoted a chapter to the alchemical and astrological teachings of the visionary <note>original: "seer"</note> of Hohenheim. The first art, according to Paracelsus, separates the pure from the impure and develops species out of primordial matter <term>primordial matter: the fundamental, formless substance from which all things were thought to be created</term>. It perfects what Nature has left imperfect; therefore, its principles are of universal application and are not restricted to the metallic and mineral kingdoms. Gold can be made by physical chemistry, but the process is poor and unproductive in comparison with the gold which can be produced by an exercise of the occult powers which exist in the soul of man. Actual and material gold can be manufactured "psycho-chemically." By this amazing theory, Paracelsus created a new school of alchemy which abandoned experimental research and sought within themselves the secret, subject, and end of alchemical philosophy.

### DENIS ZACHAIRE.

It appears that the true name of this persevering and indefatigable seeker after the goal and truth of alchemy has not in reality come down to us; the name placed at the head of his *Little Work on Chemistry* <note>original: *Opusculum Chimicum*</note> is simply a pseudonym. It is to this little work that we are indebted for one of the most singular histories in the annals of the Hermetic art <term>Hermetic art: the practice of alchemy, named after the legendary founder Hermes Trismegistus</term>.

Denis Zachaire was born of a noble family in an unmentioned part of Guienne <note>Guienne: a historical province in southwest France</note> during the year 1510. He was sent, as a youth, to Bordeaux under the care of a tutor to

### DENIS ZACHAIRE.

pursue the study of philosophy and *belles lettres*. His tutor, however, had a passion for alchemy and infected his pupil with the fatal fever of the sages. They quickly abandoned the common academic courses for the difficult pathways of the *magnum opus*, and Denis, in particular, devoted himself to the diligent compilation of a vast volume of Hermetic recipes, outlining a thousand processes, with a thousand different materials, for the successful manufacture of gold. From Bordeaux, he went to Toulouse, still in the company of his tutor, for the apparent study of law, but in reality for the experimental practice of alchemy. Two hundred crowns, which were provided for their support during the next two years, were quickly spent on the purchase of furnaces, instruments, and chemicals, to literally carry out the processes contained in the books of the experts <note>original: "adepts"</note>.

> “Before the end of the year,” as he himself tells us, “my two hundred crowns went up in smoke, and my tutor died of a fever he caught in summer from his close attention to the furnace, which he set up in his room and stayed near constantly in extreme heat. His death distressed me greatly, and even more so as my parents refused to provide me with money, except for what was absolutely necessary for my support. I was therefore unable to proceed in my great work.

> “To overcome these difficulties, I went home in 1535, having come of age, to release myself from guardianship; and I sold some of my property for four hundred crowns. This sum was necessary to carry out a process which was given to me in Toulouse by an Italian, who said he had seen it proven. I kept him living with me to see the completion of his process.

I remained at Toulouse all winter waiting for him, and I might have been waiting there still, as I have never heard from him since.

In the following summer, the city being visited by the plague, I went to Cahors and stayed there for six months. I did not lose sight of my work and became acquainted with an old man who was called "the philosopher"—a name given in that region to anyone of superior information. I shared my methods with him and asked for his advice. He mentioned ten or twelve processes which he thought were better than others. I returned to Toulouse when the plague ended and renewed my labors accordingly. The only consequence was that my money was all spent, except for one hundred and seventy crowns. 

To continue my operations with more certainty, I made the acquaintance of an <term>abbé</term> <note>original: abbé; a title for a French Catholic clergyman</note> who lived in the neighborhood of this city. He was seized with a passion for the same pursuit as myself, and he informed me that one of his friends, who lived with Cardinal Armanac, had sent him a process from Rome which he believed was genuine, but it would cost two hundred crowns. I agreed to provide half of this sum, and he gave the rest, so we began to work together. Our process required a large supply of spirits of wine <note>original: spirits of wine; high-proof alcohol used as a solvent</note>. I purchased a cask of excellent wine, from which I distilled the spirit and purified it many times. We took two pounds in weight of it and half a pound of gold, which we had calcined <note>original: calcined; heated until reduced to a powder</note> for a month. These were placed in a <term>pelican</term> <note>original: pelican; an alchemical flask with two curved necks that allow distilled vapors to cycle back into the main body</note> and placed in a furnace. This work lasted a year, but, so as not to remain idle, we performed some other...

"The year 1537 passed without any change appearing in the results of our labors. We might have continued this way for our entire lives, for we should have known that the "perfect metals" <note>original: perfect metals; gold and silver, which alchemists believed were resistant to change by lesser substances</note> are not altered by vegetable or animal substances. We took out our powder and performed a <term>projection</term> <note>original: projection; the final stage of the alchemical process where the "stone" or powder is cast upon molten base metal to transform it into gold</note> onto hot quicksilver, but it was in vain! Imagine then our grief, especially since the <term>abbé</term> <note>original: abbé; the head of a monastery or a title for a clergyman</note> had notified all his monks that they would have to melt down the lead cistern of their house so that he could convert it into gold as soon as our operations were finished.

"My failure could not make me give up. I again raised four hundred crowns against my property; the <term>abbé</term> did the same, and I set out for Paris, a city containing more <term>alchemists</term> than any other in the world. I resolved to stay there as long as the eight hundred crowns lasted, or until I succeeded in my object. This journey brought me the displeasure of my relatives and the criticism of my friends, who imagined I was a dedicated law student. However, I made them believe that the purpose of my stay in Paris was the purchase of a position in the law courts.

"After traveling for fifteen days, I arrived in Paris in January 1539. I remained almost unknown for a month, but as soon as I visited the furnace makers and spoke with some amateurs, I became acquainted with more than a hundred 'artists' <note>original: artists; here referring to practitioners of the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy</note> who were all at work in different ways. Some labored to extract the <term>mercury of metals</term> <note>original: mercury of metals; a theoretical "first matter" or essential spirit believed to be hidden within physical metals</note> and afterwards to 'fix' it. Others held a variety of different systems, and scarcely a day passed in which some of them did not visit me—even on Sundays and the most sacred festivals of the Church—to hear what I had done."

I did not fail to write to the <term>abbé</term> <note>original: abbé; the head of a monastery</note> in Toulouse, enclosing a copy of the stranger's process. # DENIS ZACHAIRE. #

...a very curious personage. By his command, I went to work and succeeded according to the process I knew. These promises, which meant nothing, did not lead me to harbor the hopes of a courtier, and I returned to the <term>abbé</term> <note>original: abbé; the head of a monastery</note> at Toulouse.

“On my journey, I heard of a religious man who was very skillful in natural philosophy. “I felt the truth of this sound advice, but before I carried it out, I went to see my friend at Toulouse to give him an account of the eight hundred crowns that we had pooled together, and to share with him the reward I had received from the King of Navarre. If he was not satisfied with everything I told him, he was even less pleased with the decision I had made to stop my operations. Of our eight hundred crowns, we had only eighty-six left. I left him and returned home, intending to go to Paris and remain there until I was established in my theory through reading the works of the <term>adepts</term> <note>original: adepts; those who have mastered the secrets of alchemy</note>. I reached Paris in 1546 and stayed there a year, diligently studying the *Turba Philosophorum*, the good Trévisan, the 'Remonstrance of...'

"Nature" and some other of the best books. But as I had no *first principles*, I did not know what to decide.

"At length, I left my solitude, not to see my old acquaintances—the seekers of specific tinctures and minor works—but to frequent those who proceeded in the Great Work <note>original: great process; the systematic method of achieving the philosopher's stone</note> according to the books of the genuine adepts. I was, nevertheless, disappointed in this by the confusion and disagreement of their theories, by the variety of their works, and by their different operations. Driven by a sort of inspiration, I devoted myself to the study of Raymond Lully and Arnold de Villa Nova. My reading and meditation continued for another year. I then *formed my plan* and only waited to sell the remainder of my land to enable me to go home and put my resolution into practice. I began at Christmas, 1549, and after some preparations, having procured everything that was necessary, I began my process, not without anxiety and difficulty. A friend said to me: "What are you going to do? Have you not lost enough by this delusion?" Another assured me that if I continued to purchase so much coal, I would be suspected of counterfeiting coin, of which he had already heard a rumor. Another said I ought to follow my business as a lawyer. But I was chiefly tormented by my relatives, who reproached me bitterly for my conduct and threatened to bring law enforcement officers into my house to smash my furnaces to pieces.

"I leave you to judge my trouble and grief at this opposition. I found no consolation but in my work, which progressed from day to day and to which I was very attentive. The interruption of all trade, which was caused by the plague, gave me the opportunity for great solitude, in which I could examine with undisturbed satisfaction the success of the three colors which mark the true work. I thus arrived at the perfection of the tincture,"

# DENIS ZACHAIRE. #

> and made a trial of its efficacy on common quicksilver, on Easter Monday, 1550. In less than an hour, it was converted into pure gold. You may guess how joyful I was, but I took care not to boast. I thanked God for the favor He showed me, and prayed that I should be permitted to use it only for His glory.
>
> “The next day I set out to find the abbé, according to the promise we gave each other to share our discoveries. On my way, I called at the house of the religious man who had assisted me with his good advice. I was grieved to find that both he and the abbé had been dead for about six months. I did not go back to my house, but sought another place to await the arrival of one of my relatives whom I had left at my home. I sent him a power of attorney <note>original: procuration</note> to sell everything I owned, both house and furniture, to pay my debts, and to distribute the remainder among those of my relatives who were in need. He soon after rejoined me, and we set out for Lausanne, in Switzerland, resolved to spend our days without display in some of the celebrated cities of Germany.”

In his unknown retreat* the adept recorded his adventures and experiences while in search of the philosopher's stone, *so that I might turn good and pious living men away from deceptions toward the right path of perfection in this divine work.* <note>Latin: ut divertarem bonos piosque vivos, à sophisticationibus, ad viam rectam perfectionis in hoc opere divino.</note> His little work is entitled simply *Opusculum Chemicum*; it opens with the romantic narrative which I have cited almost *at full length.* <note>original: in extenso</note> It calls *Hermes our great prophet*, <note>Latin: Hermes magnus propheta noster</note> insists that the art is the gift of God alone on the authority of all the initiates, and quotes so extensively from previous writers that it can scarcely be considered an original work on the Hermetic philosophy.

The life of Bernard Trévisan has abundantly testified to the physical nature of his object, which is amply

## BERIGARD OF PISA.

The following account of a transmutation performed by himself is recorded by the celebrated Italian philosopher, Claude Berigard, and can be found on the twenty-fifth page of his *The Pisan Circle* <note>original: "Circulus Pisanus"</note>, published at Florence in 1641.

> “I did not think that it was possible to convert mercury <note>original: "quicksilver"</note> into gold, but an acquaintance thought proper to remove my doubt. He gave me about a dram <note>original: "drachm"</note> of a powder nearly the color of a wild poppy, and having a smell like burnt <note>original: "calcined"</note> sea-salt. To avoid all deception, I purchased a crucible, charcoal, and mercury, in which I was certain that there was no gold mixed. Ten drams of mercury which I heated on the fire were, upon projection <term>The act of casting the "philosopher's stone" or medicinal powder onto a molten base metal to effect its transformation.</term>, transmuted into nearly the same weight of good gold, which stood all tests. Had I not performed this operation in the most careful manner, taking every precaution against the possibility of doubt, I should not have believed it; but I am satisfied of the fact.”

## CHARNOCK.

Thomas Charnock was born on the Isle of Thanet in the year 1524. He calls himself an unlettered scholar and a student of astronomy and philosophy. He practiced surgery, and, though he knew only the basics of Latin,

<meta>This page continues the biography of Thomas Charnock, detailing how he acquired his alchemical knowledge from two specific masters.</meta>

### THOMAS CHARNOCK.

It appears that he was famous in the neighborhood of Salisbury, where he had established himself, for his accomplishments in the liberal sciences <note>The "liberal sciences" or "liberal arts" traditionally included grammar, logic, rhetoric, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy.</note>. He had two masters in alchemy. The first was Sir James S——, a priest living <note>original: "dwelling"</note> in the cloisters near Salisbury, who informed Charnock that he did not derive his knowledge from any living expert <note>original: "adept"</note>. Instead, by meditating upon the words of the philosophers <note>In alchemical texts, "the philosophers" usually refers to the ancient and medieval authors of hermetic and chemical secrets.</note>, he had mastered the principal secrets of alchemy as he lay in his bed and had accordingly succeeded in making the silver powder.

The other master who instructed Charnock was a blind man, led by a boy, whom the student <note>original: "neophyte"</note> accidentally discovered at an inn among other travelers. Charnock recognized him by a few words of alchemical theory <note>original: "occult chemistry"</note> which he overheard in his conversation. As soon as the company had retired for the evening, Charnock questioned the speaker and requested instruction in natural philosophy. To this the expert <note>original: "adept"</note> objected, saying that he was unacquainted with his questioner. He said he would return his knowledge to God who gave it, unless he happened to meet a certain Master Charnock, the fame of whose learning and charity had reached him.

At these words, Charnock made himself known, and the old man talked with him for an hour, during which time he found Charnock expert in many mysteries of the sacred science. He promised Charnock that if he made a vow not to reveal the secret for gold, career advancement <note>original: "preferment"</note>, or through affection for powerful men, but only at death to one who was truly devoted to the search into nature, he would make him the heir of his knowledge. Accordingly, on the following Sunday they received Holy Communion <note>original: "the Eucharist"</note> together. Then, withdrawing into the middle of a large field, the boy was sent away out of hearing; there, in a few words, the blind man uttered “the mystery of mineral prudence” <term>The core secret of the "Great Work" or the transmutation of metals.</term>. Their conversations were continued for nine days.

The secrets of alchemy were disclosed, and the adept also related his own private history, informing Charnock that his name was William Bird, that he had been a prior of Bath, and had defrayed the expense of repairing the abbey church from treasure which he had acquired by means of the red and white elixirs <note>In alchemy, the "white elixir" was believed to transmute metals into silver, while the "red elixir" (or Philosopher's Stone) transmuted them into gold.</note>. At the suppression of the abbey, he concealed the priceless powder in the wall, and returning in ten days, it was gone. He found only a few rags in the place where he had left it. This misfortune almost drove him mad; he wandered about and lost his sight. He was therefore unable to repeat his process and continued to travel over the country, led by a boy. He had received his Hermetic knowledge from a servant of Ripley <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490) was a famous English alchemist and author of "The Compound of Alchemy."</note>.

At the time of this communication, Charnock was twenty-eight years old, and two years later, his first master fell sick while attending his furnace for the completion of the red stone. He sent for Charnock, made him the heir to his work, and died after giving him instructions how to proceed. Charnock began his operations on the materials left by his leader and was much perplexed by the difficulty of keeping the fire equal. He often started out of his sleep to examine the fuel; but after all his care, which continued during the space of several months, the frame of wood that covered the furnace took fire during a short period of his absence. When, smelling the burning, he ran up to his laboratory, he discovered that his work was completely destroyed. This occurred on January 1, 1555. To repair the damage, he was obliged to start again at the first part of the process, and he hired a servant to assist in taking care of the fire. In the course of two months, certain signs filled him with hopes of success, when his dependence on his servant proved the ruin of his work. He discovered that this unfaithful assistant would let the fire nearly go out, and

<meta>This page continues the biography of Thomas Charnock, detailing his failed third attempt at the alchemical Great Work due to military conscription, and introduces the Brescian alchemist Giovanni Braccesco.</meta>

then, to conceal his neglect, he would rekindle it with grease until it was so hot that it scorched the matter beyond recovery.

In his third attempt, Charnock resolved to proceed without help. His fire cost him three pounds a week, and he was forced to sell some rings and jewels to maintain it. He made good progress over the course of eight months and expected to be rewarded in a short time for all his labors; but at this critical moment, he was conscripted to serve as a soldier at the siege of Calais. Furious with disappointment, he took a hatchet, smashed his glasses, furnace, and apparatus, and threw them out of the house.

He wrote his “Breviary of Philosophy” in 1557 and the “Enigma of Alchemy” in 1572, with a memorandum dated 1574, when he was fifty years old. In these, he declares his attainment of the gold-producing powder when his hair was white. The “Breviary” claims to describe all the vessels and instruments which are required in the science; a potter, a joiner, and a glassmaker must provide their various services. The address of one of these craftsmen, specifically recommended by the author, is said to be Chiddingfold <note>A village historically famous for its glass-making industry.</note> in Sussex; he could manufacture egg-shaped glasses that opened and closed “as precisely as a hair.” The regulation of the philosophical fire <note>The controlled heat necessary for alchemical processes.</note> is described in this curious poem, but the rest of its information is purely autobiographical.

## GIOVANNI BRACCHESCO.

This alchemist of Brescia flourished in the sixteenth century. He was the author of a commentary on Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, an influential eighth-century chemist often referred to by his Latinized name, Geber.</note>, which is not thought to shed much light on the obscurities of the Arabian philosopher. The most curious of his original treatises is *Legno della Vita, in which is declared the* <note>The Italian title translates to "The Wood of Life, in which is declared the..."</note>

<summary>This page describes Thomas Charnock's frustrations in his alchemical pursuits, including the destruction of his laboratory after being conscripted into the army, and introduces the works of Giovanni Braccesco.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Charnock, Breviary of Philosophy, Siege of Calais, Chiddingfold, philosophical fire, Giovanni Braccesco, Geber, Legno della Vita</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Giovanni Braccesco, specifically examining his published works on the "Wood of Life" and his dialogues explaining the Hermetic philosophy of Raymond Lully.</meta>

*Medecina per la quale i nostri primi padri vivevano nove cento anni*, Rome, 1542, 8vo.—"The Wood of Life, wherein is revealed the medicine by means of which our Primeval Ancestors lived for Nine Hundred Years." This work, together with *La Esposizione di Geber Filosofe*, Venice, 1544, 8vo, was translated into Latin, and may be found in the collections of Gratarolo and Mangetus. They were also published separately under the title *De Alchimia dialogi duo*, Lugd., 1548, 4to. The Wood of Life is one of the innumerable names given by the alchemists to the matured and perfected stone, the composition of which is the accomplishment of the *magnum opus*. It is more generally called the Universal Balsam or Panacea, which cures all diseases and ensures to its most blessed possessor an unalterable youth. The name Wood of Life is given by the Jews to the two sticks which hold the scroll of the Law <note>The Torah.</note>. They are convinced that simple contact with these sacred rods strengthens the eyesight and restores health. They also hold that there is no better means of facilitating the childbirth of women than to cause them to behold these vitalizing sticks, which, however, they are in no way permitted to touch.*

The work of Braccesco is written in the form of a dialogue, and explains the Hermetic principles of Raymond Lully, one of the speakers, who instructs an enthusiastic disciple in the secret principles of the divine art. The disciple in question is in search of a safeguard against the numerous infirmities and weaknesses of the "radical moisture" <note>In medieval medicine, the fundamental life-sustaining fluid of the body.</note>. Such a medicine is declared by the master to be extracted from a single substance, which is the philosophical *aqua metallorum* <note>Water of metals.</note>. The dialogue is of interest, as it shows the connection in the mind of the writer between the development of metallic perfection and the physical regeneration of humanity.

\* *Dictionary of the Occult Sciences*, vol. i. p. 232.

<header>JOHN DEE.</header>

## LEONARDI FIORAVANTI.

A doctor, surgeon, and alchemist of the sixteenth century, this Italian was a prolific author who is best known by his "Summary of the Secrets of Medicine, Surgery, and Alchemy," published in octavo at Venice in 1571, and which has been reprinted several times. It contains an application of Hermetic methods and principles to the science of medicine, but the author's account of the *petra philosophorum* <note>The philosophers' stone.</note> shows the designation to be of a purely arbitrary kind, for it is a mixture of mercury, nitre, and other substances, intended to act on the stomach, and has no connection with the transmuting *lapis* <note>Stone.</note> of the alchemical sages.

## JOHN DEE.

The life of this pseudo-adept, and of Edward Kelly, his companion in alchemy, is shrouded in a cloud of necromancy and magical-Hermetic marvels, so that the fabulous and historical elements are not easily separated.

The true name of Edward Kelly is supposed to have been Talbot. He is said to have been born at Worcester in 1555, and to have followed the profession of a lawyer in London. His talents in penmanship appear to have been used in the forgery of deeds. He was prosecuted at <unclear>L</unclear>ancaster, according to a narrative by his enemies, for an offense of this nature, and was condemned to lose his ears. By some he is said to have suffered this punishment,<note>Morhof, *Epistola ad Langlelotum de Metallorum Transmutatione*.</note> by others to have evaded it, seeking safety in Wales, where he stayed at an obscure inn, and concealed his identity by adopting a new name. During this stay an old manuscript was shown him by

<summary>This page describes Leonardi Fioravanti’s application of alchemy to medicine and introduces the early, controversial life of Edward Kelly, the associate of John Dee.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardi Fioravanti, John Dee, Edward Kelly, alchemy, petra philosophorum, necromancy, Edward Talbot, forgery</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Edward Kelly's stay in Wales, detailing how he obtained a mysterious alchemical manuscript and two bottles of powder from an innkeeper.</meta>

<header>LIVES OF ALCHEMYSTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.</header>

the innkeeper, which was indecipherable to himself or his neighbors. The so-called Edward Kelly, being initiated into the mysteries of ancient writing, discovered it to be a treatise on <term>transmutation</term>, and his curiosity was highly excited. He inquired about its history and was told that it had been discovered in the tomb of a bishop who had been buried in a neighboring church, and whose tomb had been sacrilegiously torn up by some wretched heretical fanatics at that era of furious religious mania and rampant Elizabethan persecution. The object of this desecration was the discovery of concealed treasures in the resting place of the prelate, to whom immense riches were attributed by popular tradition. The impiety was, however, rewarded by nothing but the manuscript in question and two small ivory bottles, respectively containing a heavy red and white powder. These pearls beyond price were rejected by the pigs of apostasy; one of them was shattered on the spot, and its reddish, celestial contents were for the most part lost. The remnant, together with the remaining bottle and the unintelligible manuscript, were quickly disposed of to the innkeeper in exchange for a skin of wine. The unbroken bottle was transferred by the new owner as a plaything for his children, but the providence which mainly watches over the accomplishment of the sublime act preserved its contents intact. When Edward Kelly, with an assumed antiquarian indifference regarding objects which were more curious than valuable, offered a pound sterling for all the items, a bargain was promptly made. The lawyer was by no means an alchemist, but he believed himself to be in possession of a <term>Hermetic</term> treasure; he determined, at all risks, to return to London and consult with his friend Dr. Dee, who lived in a cottage at Mortlake, and who, in matters of magical deviltry and in the complexities of the <term>occult</term>, was considered a man among men.

<keywords>Edward Kelly, John Dee, transmutation, alchemical powder, Mortlake, Elizabethan persecution, Hermetic treasure, occult</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the occultist John Dee and his partnership with the medium Edward Kelly, focusing on their alchemical discoveries and their departure from England for Central Europe.</meta>

# JOHN DEE

Whether he had been accused of forgery, or whether he had lost his ears or not <note>It was a common legal punishment for counterfeiters and forgers to have their ears cropped.</note>, the discovery of Edward Kelly caused the necromantic doctor <note>referring to John Dee, who was frequently accused of practicing "necromancy" or magic involving spirits</note> to be blind to Kelly's faults or his crimes. He immediately set to work in Kelly's company in the year 1579, and in December, a massive success crowned their shared labor. The potency of Kelly's tincture <note>an alchemical substance believed to be capable of transmuting base metals into gold</note> proved to be one part to 272,230; however, they lost much gold in experiments before they understood the full extent of its power. In Dr. Dee's "Diary in Germany," he mentions the book of St. Dunstan—which is likely Kelly's manuscript—and also the powder "found during the digging in England," which suggests there is some foundation for the story just told. The place where the treasure was obtained is reported to have been the ruins of Glastonbury Abbey, founded by St. Dunstan. The last abbot of the abbey was hanged by Henry VIII for his loyalty to the Papal cause.

Kelly appears to have lived at Islington. In June 1583, a warrant <note>original: "attachment"</note> was issued against him for counterfeiting money <note>original: "coining"</note>, an offense of which Dee declared him innocent. In the following September, Dr. Dee, his wife and children, and Edward Kelly with his wife—accompanied by a certain Lord Albert Alasco of Siradia, Poland—departed from London for Kraków. As soon as they arrived in northern Germany, Dr. Dee received a letter from one of his friends in England informing him that his library at Mortlake had been seized and partially destroyed due to common rumors <note>original: "vulgar report"</note> of his illegal studies, and that his rents and property were confiscated <note>original: "sequestered"</note>. Despite possessing the <term>Gift of God</term> <note>original: "Donum Dei," a term often used in alchemy to refer to the Philosopher's Stone or the secret of transmutation</note>, everyone involved appears to have lived in considerable poverty <note>original: "penury"</note> as a result.

In 1585, we find them at Prague, which was then the capital of alchemy and the headquarters for masters of the craft and their students.

<summary>This page details the alchemical collaboration between John Dee and Edward Kelly, their discovery of a "tincture" supposedly found at Glastonbury Abbey, and their flight from England to Poland and Prague following legal and social pressures.</summary>

<keywords>John Dee, Edward Kelly, alchemy, transmutation, St. Dunstan, Glastonbury Abbey, Lord Albert Alasco, Mortlake library, Prague, Donum Dei, counterfeiting, necromancy</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Edward Kelly’s alchemical career following his departure from England with John Dee, specifically focusing on his activities in Europe and his eventual imprisonment by the Emperor.</meta>

# LIVES OF ALCHEMYSTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

Edward Kelly and his companions soon had plenty of money, and the owner of the Hermaic Benediction <note>likely referring to the alchemical powder Kelly allegedly discovered at Glastonbury</note> made no secret of his prize or his powers. He indulged in all kinds of extravagance and performed continuous projections <note>the alchemical process of transmuting base metals into gold</note> for himself and his friends, as well as for many people of high social standing who sought his acquaintance. Much of the resulting gold was distributed. Kelly's transmutations during this period are attested to by several writers, including Gassendus. The most well-documented and remarkable instance, according to Figuier, is the one that took place in the house of the imperial physician, Thaddeus de Hazek. There, by using only a single drop of a red oil, Kelly transmuted a pound of mercury into excellent gold; the extraordinary power of the agent even left a small ruby at the bottom of the crucible. Dr. Nicholas Barnaud, Hazek’s assistant and an alchemical writer whose works are as rare as they are reputable, witnessed this wonder and later manufactured the precious metal himself—the *désir désiré* <note>the "desired desire," a term for the Philosopher's Stone or its result</note>—with the assistance of Edward Kelly.

News of this spread, and the adept <note>an expert in alchemy</note> was invited by Emperor Maximilian II to the Court of Germany, where his transmutations raised him to the highest favor; he was knighted and created Marshal of Bohemia. Now completely intoxicated by his success, he presented himself as a true adept who was able to create the priceless projecting powder. This gave an advantage to the enemies his rise had created; they persuaded the Emperor to essentially imprison this living philosophical treasure and to force his alchemical secret from him. His misfortunes then began. His absolute inability to obey the imperial command and produce a large amount of the philosopher's stone was interpreted as a stubborn refusal. He was thrown into a dungeon, but after promising to comply with the demand if he had the freedom to seek assistance, he was quickly set free, whereupon he

<summary>Edward Kelly gains fame and a knighthood in Europe by performing successful alchemical transmutations, but he is eventually imprisoned by the Emperor for failing to produce a large supply of the philosopher's stone.</summary>

### JOHN DEE.

...rejoined Dr. Dee, and they again set to work together. The Book of St. Dunstan indicated how to use the powder but not how to prepare it, and their experiments—closely watched to prevent Kelly's escape—proved entirely futile. In the desperation that followed their failure, Kelly’s violent temperament broke out, and he murdered one of his guards. He was imprisoned again, while his companion, for the most part, remained undisturbed and used his opportunities, it is said, to interest Queen Elizabeth in the fate of the Emperor’s prisoner. She claimed the alchemist as her subject, but his recent crime had rendered him liable to the laws of the empire, and he was still held in his dungeon.

In 1589, Dr. Dee set out for England himself. He stopped at Bremen and was visited there by Henry Khunrath, one of the greatest experts of the age. The Landgrave of Hesse sent him a complimentary letter and was presented in return with twelve Hungarian horses. Dr. Dee arrived in England after an absence of six years; he was received by the Queen, who later visited him at his house, gave him two hundred angels <note>An English gold coin featuring the archangel Michael</note> to cover his Christmas expenses, and granted him a license to practice alchemy. Sir Thomas Jones offered him his Castle of Emlyn in Wales as a residence; he was made Chancellor of St. Paul’s, and in 1595, Warden of Manchester College. He moved there with his wife and children and was installed in February 1596. He does not appear to have achieved any transmutation after his return to England. In 1607, we find him again at Mortlake, living on the income he received from Manchester, but subject to much persecution by the Fellows of that college. He died in 1608 at the age of eighty.

The <term>Hermetic</term> abilities of Kelly were always believed in by the Emperor; he continued to detain him, hoping—

to extract his secret. Some friends of the unfortunate alchemist endeavored, in the year 1597, to effect his escape by means of a rope, but he fell from the window of his prison and died of the injuries he received.

During his confinement, he composed a treatise on the philosophical stone, and the Diary of Dr. Dee was published from a genuine Ashmolean manuscript in 1604. The son of John Dee became physician to the Czar in Moscow, and in his *Fasciculus Chemicus*, he states that, in early youth, he witnessed transmutation repeatedly for the period of seven years.

The metrical account of Sir Edward Kelly’s work in the *Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum* informs all who are broiling in the kitchen of Geber <note>A famous 8th-century Arab alchemist</note> to burn their books “and come and learn of me,” for they can no more compound the *Elixir Vitæ* <note>The Elixir of Life</note> and the precious stone than they can manufacture apples. The progenitor of magnesia, wife to the gold of the philosophers, is not a costly thing. The philosophical gold is not common but Hermetic sulphur, and magnesia is essential mercury.

The *Testamentum Johannis Dee Philosophi Summi ad Johannem Gwynn, transmissum 1568* <note>The Testament of John Dee, Greatest Philosopher, sent to John Gwynn, 1568</note>, is clearly worded as follows in its reference to the *magnum opus* <note>The Great Work of alchemy</note>:—

> “Cut that in three which Nature has made one,
> Then strengthen it, even by itself alone;
> Wherewith then cut the powdered sun in twain,
> By length of time, and heal the wound again.
> The self-same sun tries yet more, you must wound,
> Still with new knives, of the same kind and ground;
> Our true monas <note>The Hieroglyphic Monad, a symbol representing the unity of the cosmos</note> thus use by Nature’s Law,
> Both bind and loose, only with ripe and raw,
> And always thank God who alone is our Guide,
> All is enough, no more than at this tide.”
