<meta>This page provides a biographical and philosophical overview of Henry Khunrath, focusing on his education, his primary published work, and his mystical interpretation of alchemy.</meta>

### HENRY KHUNRATH.

This German alchemist, who is claimed as a high priest <term>original: "hierophant"; one who interprets sacred mysteries</term> of the spiritual <note>original: "psychic"</note> side of the Great Work <note>original: *magnum opus*</note>, and who was undoubtedly aware of the larger issues of Hermetic theories, must be classed as a follower of Paracelsus. He was a native of Saxony, born about the year 1560. He traveled throughout a large portion of Germany, and at the age of twenty-eight received the degree of medical doctor at the University of Basle. He practiced medicine at Hamburg and afterwards at Dresden, where he died in obscurity and poverty on the 9th of September 1601, aged about forty-five years. *The Amphitheater of the Only True Eternal Wisdom, Christian-Kabbalistic and Divinely Magical*, etc. <note>original: *Amphitheatrum Sapientiæ Æternæ solius veræ, Christiano Kabbalisticum divino magicum*</note>, published in large format <term>original: "folio"</term> in 1609, is the most curious and remarkable of his works, some of which still remain in manuscript.<margin>* Chausepié, *Dictionary*.</margin> It was left unfinished by its author, appearing four years after his death, with a preface and conclusion by his friend Erasmus Wohlfahrt.

The prologue directs the aspirant to the supreme temple of everlasting wisdom to know God and Jesus Christ whom He has sent, to know also himself, and the mysteries of the macrocosm <term>the universe as a whole, contrasted with the individual</term>. The whole treatise is purely mystical and magical. The seven steps leading to the portals of universal knowledge are described in an esoteric commentary on some portions of the Wisdom of Solomon. The Philosopher’s Stone <note>original: *lapis philosophorum*</note> is declared to be identical with the Spirit of God <note>original: *Ruach Elohim*</note> who hovered over the face of the waters during the first period of creation. The Spirit of God is called the "vapor of the power of God" <note>original: *vapor virtutis Dei*</note> and the internal form of all things. The perfect stone is attained through Christ, and, conversely, the possession of that treasure gives the...

<keywords>Henry Khunrath, alchemy, Paracelsus, Amphitheatrum Sapientiae Aeternae, Philosopher's Stone, Ruach Elohim, Christian Kabbalah, Hermeticism</keywords>

### MICHAEL MAIER.

This celebrated German alchemist, one of the central figures of the Rosicrucian controversy in Germany and the greatest expert <note>original: "adept"</note> of his age, was born at Rendsburg <note>modern spelling of Ruidsburg</note>, in Holstein, around the year 1568. In his youth, he applied himself closely to the study of medicine. Establishing himself at Rostock, he practiced that art with so much success that he became physician to Emperor Rudolph II, who ennobled him for his services. 

Nevertheless, some experts <note>original: "adepts"</note> succeeded in enticing him away from the practical path he had followed for so long and into the thorny complexities <term>original: "tortuosities"</term> of alchemical labyrinths. He became passionate about the Great Work <note>original: *Il se passionna pour le grand œuvre*</note> and scoured all Germany to hold conferences with those whom he imagined to be in possession of transcendent secrets. The *Universal Biography* <note>original: *Biographie Universelle*</note> declares that he sacrificed his health, his fortune, and his time to these “ruinous absurdities.” According to Buhle<margin>* See De Quincey’s “Rosicrucians and Freemasons.”</margin>, he traveled extensively; on one occasion, he paid a visit to England, where he made the acquaintance of the Kentish mystic Robert Fludd.

He emerged as an alchemical writer shortly before the publication of the Rosicrucian manifestos. In the controversy that followed their appearance—which threw mystical Germany into turmoil—he took an early and enthusiastic part, defending the mysterious society in several books and pamphlets. He is supposed to have traveled in search of genuine members of the “College of Teutonic Philosophers R.C.” <note>R.C. stands for *Roseae Crucis*, or the Rosy Cross.</note> and, failing to meet with them, is said to have

<summary>This page concludes the entry on Henry Khunrath and begins the biography of Michael Maier, a renowned German physician and alchemist who became a key defender of the Rosicrucian movement.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Maier, Rosicrucianism, alchemy, Rudolph II, Robert Fludd, Henry Khunrath, The Great Work</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Michael Maier, discussing his possible initiation into the Rosicrucian Order and his prolific alchemical writings, before introducing the life and philosophy of Jacob Böhme.</meta>

established a brotherhood of his own on the plan of the *Fama Fraternitatis*. These statements rest on inadequate authority, and there is better ground for believing that he was initiated, towards the close of his life, into the genuine order. A posthumous tract by Michael Maier, entitled "Ulysses," was published in 1624 by one of his personal friends, who added to the same volume the substance of two pamphlets which had already appeared in German, but which, by reason of their importance, were now translated into Latin for the benefit of the learned <note>original: "literati"</note> of Europe. The first was entitled *Colloquium Rhodostauroticum trium personarum, per Famam et Confessionem quodamodo revelatam de Fraternitate Roseae Crucis*. The second was an *Echo Colloquii*, by Benedict Hilarion, writing in the name of the Rosicrucian Fraternity. It appears from these pamphlets that Maier was admitted into the mystical order, but when or where is uncertain. He became the most prolific <note>original: "voluminous"</note> alchemical writer of his period, publishing continually until his death in the year 1622.

Many of his works are Hermetic elaborations of classical mythology, and are adorned with very curious plates. They are all hopelessly obscure, if his Rosicrucian apologies be excepted; the latter are not lacking in ingenuity, but they are exceedingly labored, and, of course, completely unsatisfactory. He does not appear to have been included among the experts <note>original: "adepts"</note>, and he is now almost forgotten. His chemical knowledge is buried in a multitude of symbols and insoluble enigmas, and believers in spiritual chemistry will not derive much comfort or profit from his writings.

In this way, the mystery of the *vas philosophorum* <note>the vessel of the philosophers</note> is said to have become less impenetrable than before, when it was considered a divine secret in the keeping of God and His elect.

Jacob Böhme, who may perhaps be considered the central figure of Christian mysticism, was born in the year 1575 at Old Seidenberg, a village near Görlitz, in what was then called German Prussia. His parents were poor but honest and sober peasants; they were unable to provide him with more than the usual religious schooling and the most basic elements of a common education. In his spare time, he tended cattle with other boys from the village. "He was a quiet, introspective lad," says one of his recent biographers, "whose face bore something of the dreamy expression that is common in poetic natures." Even at this early age, he was rich in inward visions. On one occasion, he withdrew into a cave in the rock called Land’s Crown and discovered a large wooden vessel full of money, from which he hastily retreated without touching it, as though it were something diabolical. He told his companions, but no such cavern could be found at the place in question, though they often visited the spot in search of the hidden treasure.

After leaving school, Jacob was apprenticed to a shoemaker. One day, while he was serving in the shop during his master's absence, an old man of remarkable and benevolent appearance entered and asked for some shoes. Fearing to finalize a sale without his employer, the lad demanded an extravagant price to discourage the stranger from buying them. The stranger, however, paid the sum requested; then, calling him by his name, he beckoned him into the street, where, taking him by the hand, with

With sparkling eyes and an earnest, angelic countenance, he said:
> “Jacob, you are as yet but small; nevertheless, the time will come when you shall be great, and the world shall marvel at you. Therefore, be pious, fear God, and reverence the Word. Read the Holy Scriptures diligently; in these you shall have comfort and instruction through the misery, poverty, and persecution which are in store for you. Be courageous and persevere; God loves you and is gracious to you.”

The stranger then disappeared or departed, leaving Jacob more serious and devoted than ever. The words of instruction and inspired warnings which he was frequently prompted to give to his fellow apprentices brought him into disputes with his master and eventually led to his dismissal. He became a journeyman shoemaker but returned to Görlitz in 1594, where he married the daughter of a tradesman, with whom he had four children.

In 1598, he imagined himself to be surrounded by divine light for several consecutive days. He beheld the power and nature of the vegetable world, gazing into the very heart of creation and learning the secrets of the physical cosmos by means of the self-interpreting “signatures” which seemed to be impressed on everything around him. A similar experience occurred again in 1600, when he entered a trance-like state by accidentally fixing his eye on a polished pewter dish. These visions did not interfere with his capacity for work or with his attention to his domestic affairs. Ten years passed, and his psychic perceptions suddenly became clearer. “What he had previously seen only chaotically, in fragments, and in isolated glimpses, he now beheld as a coherent whole and in more definite outlines.” He wrote what he experienced under a fervor of inspiration, and in this way his first book

was produced—*Aurora: The Day Spring*, or *Dawning of the Day in the East*, or *Morning Redness in the Rising of the Sun*. It was not originally intended for publication, but manuscript copies were circulated by one of his friends, and as a result, he suffered much persecution from the church authorities of Görlitz. He was forbidden from writing any more books and was commanded to focus on his trade <note>Böhme was a shoemaker by trade</note>. For five years he humbly obeyed this tyrannical command, and afterward contented himself with writing only for his close friends. 

From 1619 to 1624, he produced a number of long treatises, of which the book of the *True Principles*, *The Great Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"</note>, and *The Signature of Things* <note>original: "Signatura Rerum"</note> are perhaps the most characteristic and important. The publication of his book *Way to Christ*—which was seemingly unauthorized <note>original: "surreptitious"</note>—again brought him into conflict with the religious authorities <note>original: "orthodoxy"</note> of Görlitz and led to his temporary exile. He was invited to the electoral court at Dresden, where a conference of famous theologians examined him. They were so greatly impressed by the man that they declared themselves unqualified <note>original: "incompetent"</note> to judge him.

In 1624, he fell ill with a fever at the house of a friend in Silesia. He was carried at his own request to his native town, and there, on the 22nd of November, he died in a state of spiritual ecstasy <note>original: "semi-ecstatic condition"</note>.

While serving his apprenticeship at Görlitz, Jacob Böhme acquired some knowledge of chemistry, and he subsequently used Hermetic <term>Hermetic: relating to alchemy and the secret occult sciences</term> terminology in a metaphorical and spiritual sense. His example was followed by his disciples, including the famous Louis Claude de Saint-Martin, Dionysius Andreas Freher, and William Law. The second writer mentioned, Freher, has discussed the similarities between the processes of the alchemical work <note>original: "philosophic work"</note> and the spiritual redemption of humanity through Jesus Christ, as explained by Jacob Böhme.

A treatise on metallurgy <term>metallurgy: the study of the properties of metals and their production and purification</term> is attributed to the theosophist <term>theosophist: someone who seeks a direct, mystical knowledge of God and the universe</term>...

# JACOB BÖHME

himself, and there are several alchemical references in his numerous private letters <note>original: epistles</note>. The Holy Spirit <note>original: Holy Ghost</note> is stated to be the key to alchemy; there is no need for hard labor or searching (presumably among physical substances). “Seek only Christ, *and you will find all things*.” He describes the philosophers’ stone as dark, unvalued, and gray in color. It contains the highest tincture. Like Henry Khunrath before him, he discourages any expenditure beyond that of the time and cost of the operator’s maintenance. “It does not cost any money, except what is spent upon the time and the maintenance; otherwise, it might be prepared for four shillings. The work is easy, the act simple. A boy of sixteen years might make it, but the wisdom within it is great, and it is the greatest mystery.”

The seal of God is elsewhere declared to be set on the secret of alchemy, “to conceal the true basis <note>original: ground</note> of the same under penalty of eternal punishments, unless a man knows for certain that it shall not be misused. There is also no power to attain it; no skill or art is sufficient <note>original: availeth</note>. Unless one gives the tincture into the hands of another, he cannot prepare it, unless he is certainly in the new birth.”

The following lines, copied from a manuscript inserted in a volume of his works, are included in the original edition of the “*Lives of Alchemical Philosophers*”:

->The wisdom God-like Plato knew;<-

->The sacred fire of saint and sage<-
->Through every climate <note>original: clime</note>, in every age,<-
->In Böhme’s <note>original: Bohmen’s</note> wondrous page we view<-
->Discovered and revealed anew.<-

## J. B. VAN HELMONT.

In the year 1557, at Bois-le-Duc in Brabant, John Baptist van Helmont was born to a noble family. He studied at Louvain and became eminent in mathematics, algebra, the doctrines of Aristotle and Galen, and the medicine of Vopiscus and Plempius. At seventeen, he lectured on physics as a prælector <note>a public lecturer</note> and took his degree as a doctor of medicine in 1599. He read Hippocrates and the Greek and Arabian authors before he was twenty-two years old. He then spent ten years in the unsuccessful practice of medicine <note>original: physic</note> until he met a Paracelsian chemist who revealed <note>original: discovered</note> various chemical medicines to him. He then retired to the castle of Vilvord, near Brussels, and worked with unremitting diligence in the chemical-experimental analysis of bodies of every class. He spent his life in retirement and was almost unknown to his neighbors, whom he nevertheless treated during illness without accepting a fee. He declined an invitation and flattering offers from the Emperor and the Elector Palatine; after writing several tracts which are held in high regard <note>original: considerable estimation</note> even today, he died in his sixty-seventh year.

This author, so illustrious throughout Europe for his scientific knowledge, and no less celebrated for his noble rank than for the integrity <note>original: probity</note> of his character, testifies in three

Van Helmont lived a reclusive life at the castle of Vilvord, practicing medicine charitably and writing highly regarded scientific tracts.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, J. B. van Helmont, Brabant, Louvain, Paracelsian chemistry, medicine, Vilvord, Aristotle, Galen, Hippocrates</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of J. B. van Helmont, specifically detailing his firsthand experiences with alchemical transmutation and his claims regarding the philosophers' stone and the universal solvent known as the alcahest.</meta>

...different places that he has witnessed and where he himself performed transmutation. In his treatise, *De Vita Eterna*, he declares the following:—"I have seen and I have touched the philosophers' stone more than once; its color was like saffron powder, but heavy and shining like crushed glass. I was once given the fourth part of a grain—I define a grain as that of which it takes six hundred to make an ounce. I performed a projection with it, wrapped in paper, upon eight ounces of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> heated in a crucible, and immediately all the quicksilver, after making a little noise, stopped and congealed into a yellow mass. Having melted it in a strong fire, I found it to be within eleven grains of eight ounces of the purest gold; thus, one grain of this powder would have transmuted nineteen thousand one hundred and fifty-six grains of quicksilver into very good gold."

If Helmont had possessed the art of making the transmuting powder himself, his testimony might be open to suspicion. He says, on another occasion, that an adept, after a few days of acquaintance, presented him with half a grain of the powder of projection, with which he transmuted nine ounces of quicksilver into pure gold. He tells us further that he performed a similar operation many times in the presence of a large company, and always with success. On these grounds, he believed in the certainty and in the prodigious resources of the art, citing his acquaintance with an artist who had enough of the red stone to make gold weighing two hundred thousand pounds.

Though ignorant of the nature of this powder of projection, Helmont claimed to have knowledge of the alcahest <note>a hypothetical universal solvent</note>, and the methods of preparing medicines of transcendent efficacy by its means.

<summary>J. B. van Helmont recounts his successful experiments using the philosophers' stone to transmute mercury into gold and affirms his belief in the immense power of alchemy. He also claims knowledge of the alcahest, a universal solvent used to create highly effective medicines.</summary>

## BUTLER.

In the reign of James the First, the attention of the curious was attracted by a report of several transmutations performed in London by an artist of the above name. He was an Irish gentleman who had recently returned from his travels. It was said that he was not himself acquainted with the secret of the stone, so far as it regards its manufacture. To account for possessing it, the following story was related:—The ship in which he took passage during one of his voyages was captured by an African pirate, and on arriving in port he was sold as a slave to an Arabian, who was an alchemical philosopher. Butler, appearing to his master to be skillful and ingenious, was employed in the most difficult operations in the laboratory. Having a perfect knowledge of the importance of the process, as soon as it was finished he bargained with an Irish merchant for his ransom and made his escape, taking with him a large portion of the red powder.

The performers of public transmutations generally found it necessary to conceal their real knowledge by similar inventions. A physician, who was a countryman of Butler, however, formed a plan for discovering his secret. He presented himself as a servant in search of a position and was hired in that capacity by Butler. He found the philosopher so circumspect that he sought in vain for some circumstance to justify the public report of his treasures, until at last Butler sent him into the city to purchase a large quantity of lead and quicksilver <note>mercury</note>.

The disguised doctor now hoped to make a discovery. He executed his commission with dispatch and prepared a little hole in the wall of his master's room, through which, from the adjoining apartment, he could see what

<summary>This page recounts the legend of Butler, an Irish alchemist in London who supposedly stole a large quantity of the philosophers' stone from an Arabian master. It also describes a physician's attempt to spy on Butler to uncover the truth behind his transmutations.</summary>
<keywords>Butler, James I, London, transmutation, Arabian alchemist, red powder, philosophers' stone, quicksilver, mercury</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the story of Butler, an Irish alchemist, whose secret operations were being watched by a physician disguised as his servant.</meta>

<header>BUTLER.</header>

was going on. He soon noticed Butler taking something out of a box, which he placed on the melted lead, and then hid the box in a concealed place under the floor of his room. At this moment, the table and chair on which the doctor was standing to reach the spy-hole gave way, and he fell to the ground with a loud noise. Butler rushed out of his room to find the cause of this disturbance; seeing the spy-hole, he with difficulty restrained himself from running his servant through the body with his sword.

Finding there was no hope of obtaining anything from Butler, the doctor expected to seize his treasures by reporting to the officers of justice that he was a counterfeiter of false money. A vigilant search was made according to his directions, but nothing was found, for Butler had already removed whatever could betray him—his furnace, crucibles, and eighty marks of gold were all he appeared to possess. He was therefore released from the prison in which he had been confined during the investigation.

Butler was afterwards imprisoned in the Castle of Vilvord, in Flanders, where he is said to have performed wonderful cures by means of Hermetic medicine. A monk of Brittany, who was one of his fellow prisoners and had a desperate erysipelas <note>a bacterial skin infection</note> in his arm, was restored to health in an hour by drinking almond milk in which Butler had merely dipped the stone. The next day, at the report of this event, the celebrated Helmont, who lived in the neighborhood, went with several noblemen to the prison. There, Butler cured, in their presence, an aged woman of a migraine <note>then called a megrim</note> by dipping the stone into olive oil and then anointing her head. An abbess, whose arm was swollen and whose fingers had been stiff for eighteen years, was also cured by a few applications of the same stone to her tongue.

These cases are attested by the illustrious van Helmont in his works.

## JEAN D’ESPAGNET.

This Hermetic philosopher is known to us by two treatises—*Handbook of Restored Physics* <note>original: *Enchiridion Physicæ Restitutæ*</note> and *The Secret of Hermetic Philosophy* <note>original: *Arcanum Philosophiæ Hermeticæ*</note>. This second work, however, has also been claimed as the production of an unknown individual who called himself the Imperial Knight <note>original: *Chevalier Impérial*</note>. “The Secret of Hermetic Philosophy” comprises the practical part of the Great Work <note>original: *magnum opus*</note>, and the *Handbook* contains the physical theory on which the possibility of transmutation is founded. D’Espagnet is also the author of the preface to the *Description of the Inconstancy of Demons* <note>original: *Tableau de l’Inconstance des Démons*</note>, by Pierre Delancre.

“The Secret of Hermetic Philosophy” is better known under the title of the “Canons of Espagnet,” and, as I have shown in the Introduction, it is claimed as a treatise on mystical alchemy. The author, however, very plainly states that “the science of producing Nature’s grand Secret is a perfect knowledge of Nature universally and of art, concerning the realm of metals; the practice of which involves finding the principles of metals by analysis.” Moreover, the authors whom Espagnet recommends as guides to the student include those who, like Bernard Trevisan <note>A 15th-century Italian alchemist who spent his life and fortune seeking the philosopher's stone</note>, are known to have spent their existence in practical alchemy. The Sethon-Sendivogius treatises <note>Works attributed to Alexander Sethon and Michael Sendivogius, famous for their claims of successful transmutation</note> are also respectfully cited. At the same time, it may be freely granted that much of the matter in the canons, though treating of a physical object, may be extended to the psychic side of the Hermetic art.

---

<note>Footnote in French</note>

* This knight, highly revered by alchemists, is mentioned often in the *French Trumpet* <note>original: *Trompette Française*</note>, a small volume containing a *Prophecy of Bombast <note>A reference to Paracelsus, whose full name was Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim</note> on the Birth of Louis XIV*. We have, by the Imperial Knight, the *Mirror of Alchemists* <note>original: *Miroir des Alchimistes*</note>, with instructions for ladies to be beautiful from now on without using their poisonous stings <note>original: *dards venimeux*; likely a metaphorical reference to toxic cosmetics</note>, 1609, 16mo. *Dictionary of Occult Sciences* <note>original: *Dictionnaire des Sciences Occultes*</note>.

->ALEXANDER SETHON.<-

### ALEXANDER SETHON.

Few alchemical adepts suffered more from the unwise display of their powers than the subject of this article. He was a native of Scotland and is believed to have lived in a mansion in a village near Edinburgh, close to the seashore.* In the summer of 1601, a Dutch vessel was wrecked on the coast, and some of the crew were saved through Sethon's assistance. He welcomed them into his home, treated them with great kindness, and provided them with the means to return to Holland. 

A year later, he visited James Haussen—the ship's pilot and one of the people he had rescued—at Enkhuysen in that country. The sailor received him with joy and hosted him for several weeks. During this time, Haussen watched with amazement as his guest performed several transmutations, eventually admitting that he was an alchemical adept. Although Haussen was bound by gratitude and friendship to maintain strict secrecy, he could not help sharing the marvel he had witnessed with Vanderlinden, a physician in Enkhuysen. Vanderlinden was a man of integrity and caution, and Sethon had even given him a piece of gold that had been transformed from lead in his presence on March 13, 1602. This curiosity eventually came into the possession of the doctor’s grandson, who showed it to the famous George Morhoff. Morhoff mentioned the gold and its history to Langlet du Fresnoy in a letter <note>original: "epistle"</note> concerning the transmutation of metals.

From Enkhuysen, Sethon proceeded to Amsterdam and—

---

\* The names Seton or Seatoun have been given as the name of the village in question, but in Camden’s *Britain* <note>original: "Britannia"</note> it appears as the name of the house itself. The alchemist himself is quite <term>myrionimous</term> <note>meaning "having many names"</note>, being variously called Sethon, Sidon, Sethonius, the Scot <note>original: "Scotus"</note>, Sitonius, Sidonius, Suthoneus, Suethonius, and even Seehthonius.

Rotterdam, and then sailed for Italy. After a short stay there, he traveled into Switzerland and entered Germany. He was accompanied by Wolfgang Dienheim, an opponent of Hermetic philosophy <note>The tradition of alchemy and occult wisdom</note>, whom he convinced of his error through visible demonstration in the presence of several distinguished people in Basle.

To this opponent we are indebted for a description of Sethon, whom he described as looking very spiritual, short in stature but quite stout, with a high-colored complexion and a French-style beard. He calls him Alexander Sethonius and states that he was a native of Molier, "on an island in the ocean."

The lead required for the transmutation was brought by Jacob Zwinger from his own house; a crucible was borrowed from a goldsmith, and common sulfur was purchased on the road to the house where the operation was to be performed. During the entire experiment, Sethon touched nothing. He simply provided the small packet containing the powder of projection <note>A substance believed to trigger the transformation of base metal into gold</note>, which transformed the base metal into gold of the purest quality, equal in weight to the original lead.

The experiment was repeated on another occasion with the same brilliant success. In addition to Dienheim’s testimony, we also have that of Zwinger, a name highly respected by Germans in the history of medicine.*

Alexander Sethon left Basle and went under an assumed name to Strasbourg, from where he proceeded to Cologne. He stayed with an amateur alchemist named Anton Bordemann, who introduced him to the other <term>souffleurs</term> <note>literally "puffers," a derogatory term for amateur alchemists who focused on the physical labor of the bellows rather than the philosophy</note> of that city. He began a kind of alchemical crusade among them, recklessly exposing his knowledge to the gullible and suspi—

Leaving Cologne completely stunned by his marvelous operations, the illustrious <term>hierophant</term> <note>a high priest or person who explains sacred mysteries</note> of the <term>Hermetic</term> <note>the secret or occult tradition of alchemy</note> art traveled to Hamburg, where his further amazing <term>projections</term> <note>the process of turning base metal into gold</note> are described by George Morhoff. At Munich, the next stop in his alchemical pilgrimage, he performed no transmutations, suddenly disappearing with the daughter of one of its citizens. He appears to have legally married her, and he remained most devotedly attached to her from then on.

The fame of Sethon around this time attracted the attention of Christian II, the young Elector of Saxony. He sent for the alchemist, but Sethon—absorbed by his passion—had let the lover replace the <term>Hermetic</term> promoter. He instead sent William Hamilton, who appeared to be his servant but was in reality a confidant and friend, to convince the Elector of the truth of <term>alchemical</term> operations through eyewitness evidence. A <term>projection</term> was performed by Hamilton with perfect success in the presence of the whole court, and the gold manufactured there resisted every test.†

The Elector, previously a skeptic, was now more eager than ever to meet the adept. Sethon reluctantly consented, and at this point, he seems to have been left by Hamilton. He was received with honor and favor, and he presented a small quantity of the powder to Christian II. However, the Elector soon tried to take the philosopher’s entire secret for himself. Sethon refused to satisfy him and was deaf to both persuasion and threats; but the Elector, convinced that he was in possession of a

The unfortunate <term>adept</term> <note>one who is skilled in the secrets of alchemy</note> was subjected to every torment that greed and cruelty could devise. He was pierced with pointed iron, scorched with molten lead, burned by fire, beaten with rods, and stretched on the rack from head to foot, yet his resolve never failed him. At length, he exhausted his torturers and was left in solitary confinement.

At this time, Michael Sendivogius, a Moravian gentleman who usually lived in <term>Cracovia</term> <note>modern-day Krakow</note>, Poland, happened to be staying in Dresden. He was a skillful chemist who, like others of his time, was searching for the <term>philosopher's stone</term> <note>a legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold</note>, and he naturally took an interest in the case of Alexander Sethon. Having some influence at the court of the Elector, he obtained permission to see him. After several interviews—during which the <term>adept</term> was exceedingly reserved on all subjects connected with the divine science—he proposed to arrange his escape. The tortured alchemist gladly consented to these plans and promised to assist him in his <term>Hermetic</term> <note>relating to the ancient tradition of alchemy and magic</note> pursuits. As soon as the decision was made, Sendivogius departed for <term>Cracovia</term>, sold his house to raise money, and, returning to Dresden, established himself near the prison. He gained the favor of the warders through his generosity and indirect bribes. At length, the day came to carry out his plan; he entertained the guards better than usual, and when they were all drunk, he carried Sethon—who was unable to walk—on his back to a post-chaise, in which they escaped without being discovered. They stopped at Sethon’s house for his wife, who had a quantity of the <term>transmuting powder</term> <note>the substance used to change base metals into gold</note>, and then hurried to reach <term>Cracovia</term>. > “You see what I have endured,” he continued, “my nerves are withered <note>original: "shrunk"</note>, my limbs are dislocated; I am starved to the point of death, and my body is almost decaying; even to avoid all this, I did not reveal the secrets of alchemy <note>original: "philosophy"; in this context, the "secrets of philosophy" refers specifically to Hermetic philosophy or the secret of transmuting metals</note>.”

Sendivogius was not, however, destined to be deprived of all reward for his efforts and self-sacrifice. Alexander Sethon did not enjoy his freedom for long after his friend had obtained it for him, and upon his death, which occurred two years after his escape, he gave his rescuer the remains of his transmuting powder.

### MICHAEL SENDIVOGIUS.

Sendivogius, whose true name was Sensophax, was born in Moravia in 1566, and was therefore about thirty-eight years old at the death of his silent <note>original: "taciturn"</note> master. He is said by some of his biographers to have been the illegitimate <note>original: "natural"</note> son of a Polish nobleman named Jacob Sendimir. His life has been written at some length by his supporter, an anonymous German who, however, produced a work of fiction rather than a history; among other fantasies, he represented his hero as being sent by the Emperor Rudolf II to the East, where he received the discovery of the great secret from a Greek patriarch. In reality, Sendivogius had made no progress in <term>alchemy</term> <note>alchemy: a medieval chemical science and speculative philosophy aiming to achieve the transmutation of base metals into gold and the discovery of a universal cure for disease</note> before his acquaintance with Sethon.

Having almost exhausted his fortune to obtain the liberation of that adept, and having a taste for a lavish lifestyle <note>original: "extravagant living"</note>, he was unsatisfied with simply owning a portion of the transmuting powder, and was more eager...

From this work, the uninformed seeker believed he had discovered a method for increasing the powder, but he only succeeded in decreasing it.

Thwarted in this attempt, he was still eager at any rate to appear as an <term>adept</term>, and he acquired an immense reputation through constant <term>projections</term> <note>projection: the process of transmuting base metal into gold by casting the "powder" or Philosopher's Stone upon it</note>, which, aided by his lavish lifestyle, made him pass for a great <term>hierophant</term> <note>hierophant: a person who interprets or reveals sacred mysteries</note>. At Prague, he presented himself to the Emperor Rodolph II, and in the presence of several nobles, the king himself made gold by projection; overjoyed at the success of the operation, he appointed Sendivogius as one of his state counselors. From the court of Rodolph II, the alchemist proceeded to the court of Poland. As he passed through Moravia, a local lord who had heard of his <term>transmutations</term> at Prague and suspected he had an abundance of transmuting powder, laid an ambush for him on the road, seized him, and secretly imprisoned him. The lord threatened that he would never be liberated until he shared the secret of his treasure. Sendivogius, fearing the same fate as Sethon, cut through the iron bar of his dungeon window, and by making a rope from his clothes, he escaped almost naked from the power of the petty tyrant. He then summoned the lord to the emperor’s court, where the nobleman was condemned to pay a fine; a village on his estate was confiscated and transferred to Sendivogius, who later gave it as a dowry for his daughter’s marriage.

Sendivogius performed several <term>transmutations</term> at Varsovia <note>Varsovia: the Latin name for Warsaw</note>, but his powder was visibly diminishing. Duke Frederick of Würtemberg invited him to visit, and two <term>projections</term> took place in the presence of this nobleman. To grant him a status equal to a prince of the blood, the Duke gave him the territory of Nedlingen.

However, he was destined to suffer a severe setback at Würtemberg due to the schemes of an envious alchemist already attached to the court. This rival persuaded Sendivogius that Duke Frederick had formed plans that threatened his guest’s freedom and the safety of his transmuting treasure. ->MICHAEL SENDIVOGIUS.<-

Sendivogius appears to have degenerated into a mere charlatan, obtaining large sums on the pretense of manufacturing the <term>powder of projection</term>. On one occasion he descended so far as to silver-coat a piece of gold, and pretending that he possessed the <term>elixir</term>, he caused the silver to disappear by a chemical process, which he imposed on the ignorant as a projection of the tincture and a conversion of silver into gold.

His confidential servant, Bodowski, explains this deception as a maneuver to conceal his real character, having learned from experience the necessity of defending himself from the violence of greedy men. He sometimes faked poverty, or stayed in bed as if afflicted with gout or some other sickness. By these means he deflected the general suspicion that he possessed the <term>philosophers' stone</term>, preferring to pass for an impostor rather than for one in the enjoyment of limitless wealth. He frequently traveled in a servant's livery, concealing most of his red powder in the step of his carriage, and causing one of his servants to sit inside. He kept some of the powder in a small gold box, and with a single grain of it would convert as much mercury into gold as would sell for five hundred ducats.*

He was at his castle of Groverna, on the borders of Poland and Silesia, when he was visited by two strangers, one of whom was old while the other was young. They presented him with a letter bearing twelve seals, and addressed to Sendivogius. He declared that he was not the person whom they sought, but was at length persuaded to open the document, and learned that they were a delegation from the <term>Rosicrucian Society</term>, who wished to initiate him. He pretended not to understand them when they spoke of the stone of the philosophers, but they drew

him into conversation on several abstruse subjects; he, however, declined to the last the initiation which was offered to him.

Michael Sendivogius died at Parma in 1646 at the age of eighty-four, having been a counselor of state to four successive emperors. His only daughter had married an army captain against her father's wishes. He left her nothing but a "Treatise on the Salt of the Philosophers," which has never been printed, and, therefore, must not be confused with a spurious work which has been attributed to him under a similar title.

The Sethon-Sendivogius treatises are generally known under the collective title, "A New Light of Alchemy." They were written to counteract the many adulterated and false recipes composed through the fraud and greed of impostors. The procedure they indicate is declared to be the result of manual experience. 

> "Many men, both of high and low condition, in these last few years, have to my knowledge seen Diana unveiled. The extraction of the soul out of gold or silver, by whatever common method of alchemy, is but a mere fancy. On the contrary, he who, in a philosophical way, can, without any fraud and plausible deceit, make it so that it shall really tint the basest metal with the color of gold or silver (withstanding all necessary trials), whether with profit or without profit, has the gates of Nature opened to him for the inquiring into further and higher secrets, and with the blessing of God to obtain them."

It is thus in the writings of the alchemists that we are continually glimpsing or hearing of altitudes beyond transmutation, of regions of achievement which nothing in the pages of the adepts prove them to have actually explored, but which in possession of a comprehensive theory of

<summary>Michael Sendivogius died in 1646 after serving four emperors, leaving behind a rare treatise for his daughter and a legacy of influential alchemical writings. His works, often titled "A New Light of Alchemy," sought to distinguish true philosophical practice from the fraudulent methods of contemporary impostors.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the philosophical foundations of alchemy before transitioning into a specific historical anecdote about an alchemist named Gustenhover.</meta>

organic and inorganic development they beheld as a Promised Land.

The "New Light of Alchemy" <note>Original title: *Novum Lumen Chymicum* (1604), a highly influential work attributed to Michael Sendivogius.</note> insists on the existence of a seed <note>original: "sperm," used here in the archaic sense of a vital reproductive principle found in all matter</note> in everything, and that all Nature originated at the beginning from one single seed. It treats the generation of metals and the manner of their differentiation, the extraction of their seed, and the manufacture of the "stone" or tincture <note>The "Philosopher's Stone" or "Tincture" was the legendary substance believed to be capable of curing disease and transmuting base metals into gold.</note>.

### GUSTENHOVER.

A respectable goldsmith named Gustenhover resided at Strasburg <note>Modern-day Strasbourg, France.</note> in 1603. In a time of great peril, he gave shelter to a certain M. Hirschborgen, who is described as a good and religious man. On leaving his house after a considerable stay, this person presented his humane host with some "powder of projection" <term>a concentrated alchemical substance used to trigger the transformation of base metal into gold</term> and then, departing on his journey, was heard of no more.

Gustenhover imprudently performed transmutations before numerous people, and the fact was reported to the Emperor Rudolph II, who was himself an amateur in alchemy. He wrote to the magistrates of Strasburg, directing that the goldsmith should be immediately sent to him. The order was zealously obeyed; the man was arrested and guarded with vigilance to prevent any possibility of escape. When he discovered that the intention of his imprisonment was to send him to the Emperor at Prague, he guessed the whole of the business. He invited the magistrates to meet together, asking them to bring a crucible <note>original: "crucet"</note> and charcoal, and—without approaching it himself—asked them to melt some lead. Musket balls were used for the purpose, and when the metal was molten, he handed them a small portion of red powder. They cast this into the crucible, and the result of their <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air.</note> was a considerable quantity of pure gold.

<vocab>alchemy, Gustenhover, Strasburg, Hirschborgen, powder of projection, Rudolph II, transmutation, crucible, calcination, gold</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the alchemical theory of a universal seed and recounts the story of Gustenhover, a goldsmith who acquired a transmuting powder and was subsequently summoned by the Emperor Rudolph II.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Michael Sendivogius, Gustenhover, Rudolph II, transmutation, powder of projection, Strasbourg, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the goldsmith Gustenhover, who, after receiving a transmuting powder from a mysterious stranger, is summoned by the Emperor Rudolph II.</meta>

When he was brought into the presence of the gold-seeking Emperor, Gustenhover was forced to admit that he had not prepared the miraculous powder himself, and that he was completely ignorant of its nature and composition. The monarch regarded this merely as one of the excuses that were common in his experience with secretive adepts. The goldsmith repeated his protests in vain; all of his powder was quickly exhausted, yet he still found himself assigned the now impossible task of making gold. He sought refuge from the fury of the greedy wretch—who has been called the "German Hermes" by an alchemical blasphemy cursed by all followers of the doctrine—but he was pursued, dragged back, and imprisoned in the White Tower. There, the "imperial dragon," blindly and stubbornly convinced that the alchemist was hiding his secret, kept him for the rest of his life.

The adept who presented the goldsmith with the gold-bearing <note>Original: "auriferous"</note> gift of misery, the so-called Hirschborgen, is supposed to be the same person as Alexander Sethon, who was traveling through Germany under various disguises at that period.

### BUSARDIER.

Few details are recorded about this adept. He lived in Prague with a nobleman of the Court, and, falling ill, he realized that his immediate death was inevitable. In this crisis, he wrote a letter to his chosen friend Richtausen, in Vienna, begging him to come and stay with him during his last moments. Upon receiving this letter, Richtausen set out, traveling with all possible speed, but, on arriving in Prague, he had the disappointment of finding that the adept was already dead. He inquired diligently if he had left anything behind, and he was informed by the steward of the nobleman with whom he had stayed that only a powder

On again melting, it became exceedingly soft, and the master of the mint declared to his highness that it was more than twenty-four carats, and that he had never seen so fine a quality of the precious metal.

## ALBERT BELIN.

Amateurs of alchemy and the occult sciences have greatly prized the following minor works <note>original: "opuscula"</note> by this Benedictine, who was born at Besançon in 1610: "A Treatise on Talismans or Astral Figures, demonstrating the exclusively natural origin of their no less admirable virtues, with the manner of their composition and their practical utility;" "Justification of the Sympathetic Powder," published together at Paris in 1671, in duodecimo <note>a book size format</note>; and, in particular, "The Adventures of an Unknown Philosopher in the Search for and Discovery of the Philosopher's Stone." This is divided into four books, and the method for accomplishing the *magnum opus* is indicated with clarity in the fourth. It was published in duodecimo at Paris in 1664 and has since been reprinted. In the dedicatory letter <note>original: "epistle"</note>, Belin disclaims authorship, remarking that, in accordance with his profession, he should be occupied with the great work of divine grace rather than with the natural *magnum opus*. The adventures are the work of a young man with whom he was once well acquainted and who had recently died at that time. In the fourth book, the narrator of the story relates how, with a copy of Raymond Lully in his hand, he went by himself into a wood; there, he was interrupted in his studies by a wonderful lady in a marvelous silvery dress embroidered with flowers of gold. She proves to be Wisdom and is greeted by the student as his adorable mistress. In her infinite grace and condescension, this divine incarnation of philosophy instructs her enraptured <note>original: "ravished," meaning filled with delight</note> listener, during three separate discourses, on the nature, effects, and excellences of the rich and fruitful stone, and of its matter

<summary>This page describes the alchemical writings of Albert Belin, a 17th-century Benedictine, focusing on his work "The Adventures of an Unknown Philosopher." The narrative involves an allegorical encounter between a student of Raymond Lully and a personification of Wisdom who reveals the secrets of the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

## EIRENAEUS PHILALETHES.

In “*The Real History of the Rosicrucians*,” having no space for an adequate discussion of the question, I followed the more general opinion of Hermetic writers by identifying the author of the *Introitus Apertus* with the author of the *Lumen de Lumine*, Thomas Vaughan, and concluded that he wrote interchangeably under the pseudonyms of Eugenius Philalethes and Eirenaeus Philalethes.

Certain misleading references in great but fallible bibliographies, and one piece of inextricable confusion in the text of the *Introitus Apertus*, made this view appear to be fairly reasonable. However, in the course of a somewhat detailed notice, a writer in the *Saturday Review* has criticized me—by no means discourteously, it must be said—for inaccuracy in my account of Vaughan.

On the authority of Ashmole and Wood, he states that this individual was the brother of the Silurist poet, Henry Vaughan; that he was born at Llansaintffraid, in Brecknockshire, during the year 1621; and that he graduated at Jesus College, Oxford, took holy orders, and returned to hold the living of his native parish. Under the Commonwealth he was removed from his position as a Royalist, and then turned his attention to chemical experiments, one of which cost him his life on February 27, 1665.

Now, it is clear that these facts do not correspond with the life, as far as we know it, of the author of the *Introitus Apertus*, and the identification of the two Philalethes, a

The grounds of the confusion are these: First, the similarity of the assumed name, half of which was common to them both, while the other half appears to have been interchangeable in the minds of historians and bibliographers alike, including the compilers of the Catalogue in the Library of the British Museum, which attributes the *Introitus Apertus* indiscriminately to both Philalethes. Second is the fact that almost every edition and translation of this treatise contains the following passage in the initial paragraph of the preface:

> “Being an anonymous adept and a lover of learning, I decided to write this little treatise of physical secrets in the year 1645, in the twenty-third year of my age, to fulfill my duty to the sons of art, and to lend my hand to bring them out of the labyrinth of error, to show the adepts that I am a brother equal to them. I foresee that many will be enlightened by these my labors. They are no fables, but real experiments which I have seen, performed, and know, as any adept will understand. I have often laid aside my pen while writing because I was willing to have concealed the truth under the mask of envy; but God compelled me to write, and I could not resist Him. He alone knows the heart—to Him only be glory forever. I believe without a doubt that many will become blessed in this last age of the world with this <term>arcanum</term>. May the will of God be done! I confess myself unworthy of accomplishing such things—I adore the holy will of God, to whom all things are subjected! He created and preserves them for this purpose.”

A simple arithmetical operation will show that the

### EIRENÆUS PHILALETHES

The author was consequently born in the year 1621, the same year that Eugenius Philalethes—also known as Thomas Vaughan—first saw the light <note>was born</note>. This would remain undisputed, except for the fact that the original edition* of the *Introitus* <note>Full title: *Introitus Apertus ad Occlusum Regis Palatium* or "The Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King," a famous alchemical text</note> is claimed to read "in the thirtieth year" <note>original Latin: *trigesimo anno*</note>—interpreted as the thirty-third year—instead of "in the twentieth year" <note>original Latin: *vigesimo anno*</note>. There is no copy of this original edition in the British Museum, and my knowledge of it comes from the reprint in Langlet du Fresnoy’s *History of Hermetic Philosophy* <note>original title: *Histoire de la Philosophie Hermétique*</note>. Eirenæus, according to later printings, is honored by the followers of Hermes <note>alchemists and students of Hermeticism</note> as the adept who achieved the "grand and sublime act" <note>The successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone, the goal of alchemy</note> at the age of twenty-two.

These reasons, which are significant in themselves, may be supported by the fact that there is a great deal of similarity in the writing styles and methods of the two authors.

Eugenius Philalethes wrote *Theomagical Anthroposophy* <note>original: *Anthroposophia Theomagica*</note> and *The Hidden Magical Soul* <note>original: *Anima Magica Abscondita*</note>, both published together in 1650; *Adamic Magic* <note>original: *Magia Adamica*</note>, 1650; "The Man-Mouse" (a satire on Henry More, the Platonist); "The Second Wash, or The Moore (meaning Henry More) Scoured once more," 1651; *Light from Light* <note>original: *Lumen de Lumine*</note>, 1651; "The Fame and Confession of the Fraternity of the Rosy Cross" <note>original: *The Fame and Confession of the Fraternity R.C.*, referring to the Rosicrucians</note>, 1652; *The Hall of Light* <note>original: *Aula Lucis*</note>, 1652; and "Euphrates, or The Waters of the East," 1655. "A Brief Natural History," published in 1669, also bears his name, and in 1679 his surviving poems were published by his brother, Henry, along with some of Henry's own work, titled *Thalia Reborn* <note>original: *Thalia Rediviva*</note>.

The level of confusion existing among biographers and bibliographers on this subject can be seen in the fact that some authorities claim Thomas Vaughan died in 1656, while Chalmers’ *Biographical Dictionary* attributes all the works of Eugenius Philalethes to Henry "the Silurist" <note>Henry Vaughan, a famous poet known as "the Silurist" because he lived in the ancient territory of the Silures tribe in Wales</note>, whom Chalmers describes as a "Rosicrucian fanatic."

---

\* It was published at Amsterdam in 1667 and is supposed to have been free from the numerous printing errors of the later editions.

### LIVES OF ALCHEMYSTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

If there is much confusion concerning Thomas Vaughan, everything is chaos regarding Eirenæus Philalethes. He seems to have emigrated to America at a relatively early period. The original Amsterdam edition of his *Experiments on the Preparation of Sophic Mercury* <note>Original Latin title: *Experimenta de Præparatione Mercurii Sophici*</note>, which was issued by Daniel Elzevir in 1668, describes that work as "from the manuscript of an American philosopher, otherwise Eirenæus Philalethes, born an Englishman, a citizen of the world by residence" <note>Original Latin: *ex manuscripto philosophi Americani, alias Æyrenæi Philalethes, natu Angli, habitatione Cosmopolitæ*</note>. In this way, those who have avoided identifying him with Thomas Vaughan carefully confuse him with George Starkey, also an Anglo-American. Starkey claimed a close acquaintance with Philalethes and, having been initiated by him, may be considered his philosophical son, but not his *alter ego* <note>other self or second identity</note>. Starkey returned to London and wrote several chemical books, some of which detail the transmutations performed by Philalethes in the apothecary’s American laboratory. Starkey died of the plague in London in 1665, while Eirenæus continued publishing for many years after that date and lived for some time on intimate terms with the illustrious Robert Boyle, who, however, has given us no biographical details concerning him.

One of the most curious facts in the history of this mysterious adept is the apparently complete disappearance of many of his printed works. An authentic list extends to some forty volumes, some of which seem perfectly unknown and unheard of by bibliographers and collectors alike. Langlet du Fresnoy lists several manuscript treatises but gives no clue to their location.

We must be content to gather the few facts of his life from the books of Philalethes himself. The thirteenth chapter of the *Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King* <note>Original Latin title: *Introitus Apertus ad Occlusum Regis Palatium*</note> contains the following remarkable account of its author:—

> “All alchemical books abound with obscure riddles and

-> ### EIRENÆUS PHILALETHES. <-

...sophistical operations. I have not written in that style, having surrendered my will to God's pleasure. I do not fear that the art <note>The "art" refers to alchemy, specifically the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> will be looked down upon because I write plainly, for true wisdom will defend its own honor. I wish gold and silver were as lowly in value as common soil, so that we would not need to hide ourselves so strictly. For we are like Cain, driven from the pleasant society we once enjoyed without fear; now we are tossed back and forth as if pursued by furies <note>original: "furies" — in mythology, these are female spirits of vengeance; here, they represent constant anxiety and pursuit.</note>; nor can we feel safe in any one place for long. We weep and sigh, complaining to the Lord,

> "Behold, whoever finds me will kill me!" <note>The author is quoting Genesis 4:14, comparing his life of hiding to that of the biblical Cain.</note>

We travel through many nations like wanderers, and do not dare to take on the responsibility of a family; nor do we have any permanent home. Although we possess all things <note>As an adept, he claims the ability to create infinite wealth, yet cannot use it.</note>, we can use only a few. What, therefore, do we enjoy except the thoughts of our minds? Many who are strangers to this art imagine that if they possessed it, they would do great good. I once believed this myself, but the danger I have experienced has taught me otherwise. Anyone who faces the imminent danger of losing their life will act with more caution from then on. I found the world in a most wicked state; there is hardly a man who is not guided by some selfish and unworthy motive, regardless of how honest or upright he appears in public. An adept <term>adept: a person who has successfully mastered the secrets of alchemy and created the Philosopher's Stone</term> cannot perform works of mercy on a large scale without trusting others to keep his secret, and this puts him at risk of imprisonment and death. I recently had proof of this; for, while in a foreign country, I administered the medicine to some distressed poor people who were dying. Because they recovered miraculously, a rumor immediately spread about the elixir of life, so much so that I was forced to flee by night with great difficulty, having changed my clothes, shaved my head, put on a wig <note>original: "other hair"</note>, and changed my name.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Eirenaeus Philalethes, who is describing the extreme personal dangers and legal risks faced by an "adept"—someone who has successfully discovered the secret of alchemy.</meta>

-> ### LIVES OF ALCHEMISTICAL PHILOSOPHERS. <-

...lay in wait for me, merely on suspicion, driven by a thirst for gold. I could mention other dangers that might seem ridiculous to those who have never been in a similar situation. These people think they would manage their affairs better, but they do not realize that the intelligent people whose company is most sought after are extremely perceptive; a mere hint of suspicion is enough to spark a conspiracy. For the wickedness of men is so great that I have known a person who was strangled with a rope <note>original: "strangled with a halter"</note> based only on suspicion. Although he did not actually possess the art <note>The "art" refers to the secret of alchemy and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, it was enough that a desperate man had heard reports of it. 

This age is full of ignorant alchemists; however little they know of science, they know enough to recognize or suspect a true adept <note>An "adept" is a person who has mastered the Great Work of alchemy.</note>. Any appearance of secrecy will cause them to investigate every detail of your life. If you heal the sick or sell a large amount of gold, the news spreads throughout the entire neighborhood. The goldsmith knows when the metal is too pure, and it is illegal for anyone who is not a regular metallurgist to mix it with alloys. 

I once sold pure silver worth £600 in a foreign country. Even though I was dressed as a merchant, the goldsmith told me, "This silver was created by art." I asked him why he said that. He replied, "I know the silver that comes from Spain, England, and so on. This is purer than any of them." Upon hearing this, I left immediately. There is no better silver in the trade than Spanish silver, but if I had tried to lower the purity of my silver <note>The author means adding base metals to the pure silver to make it look like common "legal" silver.</note> and was discovered, I would have been hanged for a felony <note>Tampering with precious metals or unofficial alloying was often a capital crime.</note>. I never went back for either the silver or the payment for it. The transport of gold and silver from one country to another is governed by strict laws, and this alone is enough to condemn an adept who is found with a large quantity of it. Therefore, having been taught by these hardships, I have determined to remain hidden, and I will communicate the art to you, who dream of

...performing public good, so that we may see what you will undertake when you obtain it.

> "The Searcher of all hearts knows that I write the truth; nor is there any reason to accuse me of envy <note>original: "envy" – here meaning a begrudging or secretive attitude regarding his knowledge</note>. I write with a fearless pen <note>original: "unterrified quill"</note> in an unprecedented style, for the honor of God and the benefit of my neighbors, with contempt for the world and its riches. I do this because Elias the Artist <note>A legendary figure predicted by Paracelsus who would appear during the end times to reveal all the secrets of alchemy and nature.</note> is already born, and now glorious things are being declared concerning the City of God.
> 
> I dare to claim that I possess more riches than the entire known world is worth, but I cannot make use of them because of the traps of dishonest men <note>original: "snares of knaves"</note>. I disdain, loathe, and detest the idolizing of silver and gold, through which the pomps and vanities of the world are celebrated. Ah, filthy evil! Ah, vain nothingness! 
> 
> Do you believe that I conceal the art <note>The "art" refers to the secret of the Philosopher's Stone and the transmutation of metals.</note> out of envy? No, truly I protest to you, I grieve from the very bottom of my soul that we are driven like vagabonds from the face of the Lord throughout the earth. But what need is there for many words? The thing we have seen, taught, and made—which we have, possess, and know—that is what we declare. 
> 
> We are moved with compassion for the studious, and with indignation toward gold, silver, and precious stones—not as they are creatures of God (far be it from us, for in that respect we honor them and think them worthy of esteem), but because the people of God adore them just as the world does. Therefore, let them be ground to powder like the golden calf <note>A reference to Exodus 32, where Moses destroyed the idol worshipped by the Israelites.</note>! 
>

...the nations. I know these my writings will be to men as pure gold; and through them gold and silver will become as worthless as dirt. Believe me, the time is at the door—I see it in spirit—when we, the adepts <term>adepts</term> <note>original: "adeptists" — those who have achieved the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note>, shall return from the four corners of the earth. We shall not fear any traps that are laid against our lives, but we shall give thanks to the Lord our God. I wish to God that every clever man in the whole earth understood this science; then it would only be valued for its wisdom, and only virtue would be held in honor. I know many adepts who have vowed a most secret silence. I am of another judgment because of the hope I have in my God; therefore, I did not consult with my brothers, or with any human authority <note>original: "flesh and blood"</note>, in these my writings: God grant that it be to the glory of His name!”

We are told in the preface to *Ripley Revived* <note>A famous commentary by Philalethes on the works of the 15th-century English alchemist George Ripley.</note> which authors he was, at any rate, chiefly indebted to.

> “For my own part, I have cause to honor Bernard Trévisan <note>A 15th-century Italian nobleman and alchemist.</note>, who is very clever, especially in his letter to Thomas of Boulogne, where I sincerely confess I received the main light regarding the hidden secret. I do not remember that I ever learned anything from Raymond Lully <note>A 13th-century philosopher and alchemist from Majorca.</note>. Some who are not adepts give more instruction to a beginner than someone whom perfect knowledge makes cautious. I learned the secret of the magnet from one, the *steel* <note>original: *chalybs* — often referring to a specific preparation of antimony or iron used as a catalyst.</note> from another, the use of *Diana’s Doves* <note>An alchemical symbol for the white, purified vapors of mercury.</note> from a third, the *air* or *chameleon* <note>Refers to the changing colors or the volatile state of the matter during the process.</note> from another, the rough preparation of the solvent from another, and the number of 'eagles' <note>In alchemy, "eagles" refers to the number of times a substance has been distilled or sublimated.</note> from another; but for operations on the *true matter* and signs of the *true mercury*, I know of none like Ripley, though Flamel <note>Nicolas Flamel (c. 1330–1418), a famous French scribe and reputed alchemist.</note> is also eminent. I know what I say, having learned by experience what is truth and what is error.

->### EIRENÆUS PHILALETHES.<-

> "...and having at last, through the undeserved mercy of God, arrived at my haven of rest, I shall reach out my hand to those who follow. I have written several treatises: one in English, which is very clear but unfinished—unfortunately, it was lost. I would be sorry if it were published to the world. I also wrote two in Latin, *A Brief Guide to the Celestial Ruby* <note>original: *Brevis Manuductio ad Rubinem Cœlestem*</note> and *The Fountain of Chemical Philosophy* <note>original: *Fons Chymicæ Philosophiæ*</note>—these, for specific reasons, I have decided to suppress. Two others I wrote recently, which you may perhaps enjoy: namely, *The Art of the Metamorphosis of Metals* <note>original: *Ars Metallorum Metamorphoses*</note> and *An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King* <note>original: *Introitus Apertus ad Occlusum Regis Palatium*</note>. I wrote two poems in English which are lost; also in English, a manual <note>original: "Enchiridion"</note> of experiments, a daily journal of meditations, and many recipes explaining the entire secret, with an enigma attached. These also fell into the hands of one who, I believe, will never return them."

The offender in question was undoubtedly <term>George Starkey</term> <note>A colonial American alchemist and physician who moved to London; he is widely believed by modern scholars to be the actual author behind the Philalethes persona.</note>, who published the "Marrow of Alchemy" under the name of Eirenæus Philoponos Philalethes. This poetic account of the <term>Hermetic theory</term> <note>The philosophical and practical system of alchemy, named after Hermes Trismegistus.</note> and practice is apparently the lost verse of the <term>adept</term> <note>One who has mastered the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note>, but it also contains an account of the editor’s own initiation, which is certainly worth transcribing:

> "I must now state, from my own experience, facts of <term>transmutation</term> <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals into gold or silver.</note> of which I was an eye-witness. I was well-acquainted with an alchemical practitioner <term>artist</term> <note>In this context, "artist" refers specifically to a master of the alchemical art.</note> with whom I often spoke on the subject, and I saw in his possession the white and the red <term>elixir</term> <note>The substances (also called the Philosopher's Stone) used to perform transmutation; the white converts metal to silver, and the red to gold.</note> in very large quantities. He gave me more than two ounces of the white medicine, which was powerful enough to convert 120,000 times its weight into the purest pure silver. With this treasure, I went to work ignorantly on <term>multiplication</term> <note>The process of increasing the quantity or potency of the alchemical elixir.</note> and was caught in the trap of my own greed, for I spent or wasted all of this <term>tincture</term> <note>Another name for the elixir or "medicine."</note>. However, I performed a <term>projection</term> <note>The final stage of the alchemical process: casting the elixir onto molten base metal to transform it.</note> with part of it, which is sufficient..."

...for my present purpose, enabling me to assert the possibility of the art through eye-witness demonstration. I have many times transformed <note>original: "tinged"</note> hundreds of ounces into the finest silver. From a pound of mercury, I have produced silver within less than a scruple of its original weight; from lead, there was very little more waste. But it is wondrous to see tin—even though impurities <note>original: "dross"</note> were burned away from it, its weight actually increased in the fire. I tested the medicine <note>In alchemy, "the medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the substance used for transmutation.</note> on copper, iron, even on brass and pewter, on <term>spelter</term> <note>an old term for zinc</note>, solder, <term>tinglass</term> <note>an archaic name for bismuth</note>, mercury, and on <term>regulus of antimony</term> <note>the metallic form of antimony</note>; and I can say with truth that it conquers all metallic things and brings them all to perfection. I found there was nothing related to metals that it would not transform into pure silver.

Even perfect gold was penetrated and changed into a white glass which would <term>transmute</term> <note>transform one element into another</note> inferior metals into silver, though only in small quantities. But when this silver was tested, it was found to withstand <term>*aquafortis*</term> <note>original: *aqua fortis*; literally "strong water," or nitric acid, used to test and separate metals</note> and the cupel of antimony, and it weighed as much as gold, so that it was **white gold**. This was because the white <term>tincture</term> <note>the alchemical agent of transformation</note> had fermented with <term>red earth</term> <note>likely referring to the final red stage of the alchemical process or the "Red Stone"</note>, and both powers combining during <term>projection</term> <note>the final stage of alchemy where the "medicine" is cast upon base metal to transform it</note> produced silver-colored gold—or silver equaling gold in perfection, but lacking its color. I did not know the value of this silver until my medicine was nearly gone; I sold eighty ounces of it at the regular price for silver, even though it was as valuable as gold. I cast the medicine upon pure silver and obtained a crystalline metal like burnished steel or a mirror, but there was no increase in its power; it transformed only as much as it would have if it had not been cast upon silver.

The artist who gave me this is still living; I prize him as my own life and wish for his happiness, for he has been a steadfast friend. He is currently on his travels, visiting fellow artists and collecting antiquities as a citizen of the world. He is an Englishman from an ancient, honorable family who still live in the place where he was born. He is barely thirty-three years old and is exceptionally <note>original: "rarely"</note> learned. You can—

You will not learn more about him from me, nor can you become acquainted with him; his friendship with me was as unexpected as his affection was sincere. I had often seen through experiment that he was a master of both the white and the red <note>The white and red refers to the two stages or types of the Philosopher's Stone: the "white" used for making silver and the "red" for making gold.</note> before he would graciously trust me with even a small piece of the stone. Nor would I pressure him, trusting in his future kindness, which I soon received, along with what I have already said about the white medicine and a portion of his mercury.

> "He told me this mercury was a treasure beyond compare if God would open my eyes to its use; otherwise, I might grope in the dark. With this <term>dissolvent</term> <note>a substance used to dissolve others; in alchemy, often a universal solvent</note>, which is the hidden secret of all masters, he immensely increased the power of his red stone. I saw him put a measured piece of the red stone into that same mercury; it then <term>digested</term> <note>a process of slow, steady heating in a sealed vessel</note> and dissolved it, causing it to change color. In three days, it passed through the stages of black, white, and red.

> "I believed that if the red and the white stones could be increased in power, a single direct path would lead to either result. Based on this false reasoning, I destroyed ten parts out of twelve of my medicine. This loss was not enough to stop me; I mixed the remaining two parts with ten times their weight of <term>Luna</term> <note>original: Luna; the alchemical name for silver</note> and went to work again, hoping to correct my first mistake. I then began to reflect on the principles found in the old books, considering how my work agreed with the laws of Nature. At last, I concluded that each thing must be treated according to its own nature.
>
> "When I realized that my vain attempts were only wasting the <term>tincture</term> <note>the concentrated alchemical substance used for transmutation</note>, I stopped, resolving to save the few remaining grains for some urgent need. To preserve it, I mixed it with ten parts of silver.

### EIRENAEUS PHILALETHES.

...the evil he does is blamed on the master <note>original: "adept"</note> who was so unwise. Consider what a prize you possessed in both the *stone* and the *mercury*. Would anyone not say that a person must be mad to throw it all away without any gain?

"If you had been guided by reason, you might still have enough of what I gave you. Your method was to add only a grain of the *stone* to the purest *gold*; once melted <note>original: "in fusion"</note>, they would unite. Then you could proceed with your *mercury*, which would quickly mix with that gold and greatly shorten the process. You could easily have managed the work until it reached the *red* <note>original: "red"; refers to the Rubedo, the final stage of the alchemical Great Work</note> stage. Since you saw how I joined new *gold* to such *sulfur* and *mercury*, and you observed the weight, time, and heat required, what more could you have wanted? And since you know the art of preparing the *fiery mercury*, you could have as much in stock as anyone else.

"But you do not realize that God is displeased with you and caused you to waste the treasure I gave you. Perhaps He sees that you would break His holy laws and use it wrongly. Although He has granted you so much knowledge, I see clearly that He will keep you for several years from enjoying what you would undoubtedly misuse. Know that if you attempt this art without a <term>ferment</term> <note>a substance added to the alchemical mixture to trigger transformation, similar to how yeast works in bread</note>, you must beware of constant mistakes. You will wander from the right path despite all your care, and you might never in your lifetime obtain this treasure, which is a gift from God alone. If you follow the most direct path, it will take a year to reach perfection. But if you take the wrong turns, you will often be delayed—sometimes for a whole year—and you must restart your efforts and labor, regretting your loss and errors amidst much distraction, worry, and danger, all at a cost you can hardly afford. Listen, therefore, to my advice, and I will disclose the secret to you on one condition. Swear before the mighty God that for a certain time you will abstain from attempting or practic—

<summary>A mentor scolds Eirenaeus Philalethes for wasting alchemical materials through impatience, interpreting his failure as a sign of God's displeasure. He warns that the Great Work requires a "ferment" and at least a year of perfect labor, offering a secret insight only under a solemn oath.</summary>
