<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Eirenaeus Philalethes and his mentor, concluding with the mentor's demand for secrecy, and then transitions into an analysis of Philalethes’ works and an introduction to the scholar Pierre Jean Fabre.</meta>

practice; nor shall you at that time, even if you are at the point of death, reveal those specific points that I will disclose to you in secret.”

> “I swore, and he shared his mind with me, proving he was not deceiving me by showing me those truths <note>original: "lights"</note> which I shall honestly recount, as far as my oath allows.”

Eirenaeus Philalethes is credited with exceptional clarity <note>original: "unexampled perspicuity"</note>, and his *Open Entrance* <note>original: *Introitus Apertus*</note>, in particular, is an abridgment or summary of the entire *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: *turba philosophorum*</note>. Those who are searching for the physical secret of alchemy should begin by carefully studying his works. From there, they should move on to the authors he recommends; after which, the best Hermetic writers—from the days of <term>Geber</term> <note>an influential 8th-century chemist and alchemist, often called the father of chemistry</note> onward—should be read in their chronological order, carefully analyzed, and their similarities and differences noted.

The physical nature of the alchemical <term>secrets</term> <note>original: "arcana"</note> held by the true Philalethes are best understood through the writings and commentaries of his pupil, <term>George Starkey</term> <note>an American-born physician and alchemist (1628–1665) who moved to London and claimed to have met a true master of the art</note>. The mystery surrounding such an <term>adept</term> <note>a person who has mastered the "Great Work" of alchemy</note> excites overactive imaginations and makes his projects and supposed achievements seem legendary <note>original: "Titanic stature"</note>. Although the *Open Entrance* contains much that is mystical and open to romantic interpretation, it is ultimately a manual of practical alchemy. It further develops the physical principles explained in the verse essays preserved and published by Starkey.

This physician from Montpellier, who contributed to the progress of chemistry, lived during the...

beginning of the seventeenth century. He believed in the transmutation of metals, but is not considered a master <note>original: "adept"</note>, though he wrote seventeen treatises on this subject and on <term>Spagyric Medicine</term> <note>a branch of medicine based on alchemical principles, specifically the separation and recombination of plant or mineral components</note>. His most curious work is *The Christian Alchemist* <note>original: *Alchimista Christianus*</note> (Toulouse, 1632, octavo). In *The Pious-Chemical Hercules* <note>original: *Hercules Piochymicus*</note>, published in the same place two years later, he maintains that the labors of Hercules are allegories which contain the secrets <note>original: "arcana"</note> of <term>Hermetic philosophy</term> <note>the ancient philosophical and spiritual system focused on the relationship between the divine and the material world through alchemy</note>.

He defines the philosophical stone as the seed out of which gold and silver are generated. It is three and yet one; it may be found in all compounded substances and is formed of salt, mercury, and sulphur. These, however, are not to be confused with the common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> substances of the same names.

## HELVETIUS.

The following singularly impressive and even convincing testimony to the reported fact of metallic transmutation was published by the eminent Dutch physician, John Frederick Helvetius, at the Hague in 1667. It was dedicated to his friends, Dr. Retius of Amsterdam, Dr. Hansius of Heidelberg, and Dr. Menzelin of Brandenburg.

> “On the 27th of December 1666, in the afternoon, a stranger in plain, rural dress came to my house at the Hague. His manner of speaking was honest, serious, and authoritative; he was short in stature, with a long face, black hair, and a smooth-shaven chin. He seemed like a native of the north of Scotland, and I guessed he was about forty-four years old. After greeting me, he requested most respectfully that I pardon his unannounced intrusion, explaining that his love of the <term>chemical arts</term> <note>original: "pyrotechnic art"</note> led him to visit me. Having read some of my short treatises, particularly the one written against the <term>sympathetic powder</term> <note>original: "sympathetic powder of Sir Kenelm Digby"; a contemporary medicinal theory that a wound could be healed by applying a powder to the weapon that caused it</note> of Sir Kenelm Digby, and having observed in them my...”

Because he had some doubt about the Hermetic mystery <note>the secrets of alchemy and divine transformation</note>, it caused him to request this interview. He asked me if I still believed that there was no medicine in Nature capable of curing all diseases, unless the vital organs—such as the lungs or liver—had failed, or the time of death had come. I replied that I had never met an adept <note>original: "adept," a master who has successfully created the philosophers' stone</note> or seen such a medicine, though I had read much about it and often wished for it.

Then I asked him if he was a physician. He replied that he was a brass founder, yet from his youth he had learned many rare things in chemistry—specifically from a friend—including the method of extracting many medicinal secrets <note>original: "arcana"</note> from metals through the use of fire. After we discussed experiments with metals, he asked me, "Would you recognize the philosophers’ stone if you saw it?"

I answered that I would not; though I had read much about it in the works of Paracelsus, Helmont, Basil <note>Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemist</note>, and others, I dared not say I could recognize the substance of the philosophers. Meanwhile, he pulled a polished ivory box from his breast pocket and took out three heavy lumps of the stone, each about the size of a small walnut. They were transparent and of a pale sulfur <note>original: "brimstone"</note> color; some scales from the crucible still stuck to them from when this most noble substance was melted. I later calculated that its value was equal to twenty tons of gold. 

When I had eagerly examined and handled the stone for nearly fifteen minutes, and heard from its owner many rare secrets regarding its wonderful effects on human and metallic bodies, as well as its other amazing properties, I returned this "treasure of treasures" to him. I did so with a truly sorrowful heart, like one struggling with self-denial, yet I was also very thankful and humble, as was proper. I further asked to know why the color was yellow, and not red, ruby-colored, or purple, as the alchemical authors describe. He answered that the color did not matter, for the substance was mature and ripe enough. Then I humbly asked him to give me a small piece of the medicine as a lasting memory of him, even if it were only...

He immediately answered, "Oh no, this is not permitted, even if you would give me as many gold ducats as would fill this room—not for the value of the metal, but because of certain consequences. Indeed, if it were possible," he said, "that fire could be burned by fire, I would rather at this instant cast all this substance into the fiercest flames." He then asked if I had a more private room, as this one could be seen from the public street. I immediately led him into the best-furnished room at the back of the house, not doubting that he would bestow part of it or some great treasure upon me. He entered without wiping his shoes, although they were covered in snow and dirt. He asked me for a small piece of gold, and, pulling off his cloak, opened his vest, under which he had five pieces of gold. They were hanging from a green silk ribbon and were the size of breakfast plates. This gold so far excelled mine that there was no comparison in its flexibility and color. He allowed me to transcribe the inscriptions engraved upon them; they were pious thanksgivings to God, dated August 20, 1666, with the symbols of the Sun, Mercury, the Moon, and the signs of Leo and Libra.

> "I was in great admiration and desired to know where and how he obtained them. He answered, 'A foreigner, who stayed several days in my house, said he was a lover of this science and came to reveal it to me. He taught me various arts—first, how to make rubies, chrysolites, sapphires, etc., from ordinary stones and crystals, which were much more valuable than those from a mine; and how to make an oxide of iron in a quarter of an hour, one dose of which would infallibly cure the pestilential dysentery, or bloody flux; also how to make a metallic liquor to cure all kinds of dropsy <note>an old term for edema, or swelling caused by fluid retention</note> most certainly and in four days; as also a limpid, clear water, sweeter than honey, which in two hours by itself, in hot sand, would extract the tincture <note>the medicinal or alchemical essence</note> of garnets, corals, and glass..."

<summary>The mysterious stranger refuses to sell the philosophers' stone but shows Helvetius five large, remarkably flexible gold plates and claims to have learned how to create precious gems and powerful medicines from a traveling guest.</summary>

and such like." He said more, which I, Helvetius, did not notice, my mind being occupied with understanding how a noble juice could be extracted from minerals to transmute metals. He told me his master had him bring a glass of rainwater and put some silver leaf into it, which was dissolved within a quarter of an hour, like ice when heated. "He immediately drank half to me, and I pledged the other half to him; it did not have as much taste as sweet milk, but it seemed to me that I became very light-headed. I then asked if this were a philosophical drink, and why we drank this potion; but he replied that I ought not to be so curious." By the master’s directions, after a piece of lead pipe was melted, he took a little sulfurous powder out of his pocket and put a small amount of it from the point of a knife into the melted lead. After a great blast of the bellows, he soon poured it onto the red stones of the kitchen chimney. It proved to be the most excellent pure gold, which the stranger said brought him into such trembling amazement that he could hardly speak; but his master encouraged him, saying, "Cut for yourself the sixteenth part of this as a memorial, and give the rest away to the poor." The stranger did this, distributing these alms, as he affirmed—if my memory does not fail me—at the Church of Sparenda. "At last," he said, "the generous foreigner taught me this divine art thoroughly."

"As soon as his story was finished, I asked my visitor to show me the effect of transmutation and so confirm my faith; but he declined it for that time in such a discreet manner that I was satisfied. He promised to come again in three weeks to show me some curious arts involving fire, provided it were then lawful and not prohibited. At the end of three weeks he came and invited me out for an hour or two. During our walk, we discussed nature’s secrets, but he was very silent on the subject of the great elixir.

### JOHN FREDERICK HELVETIUS.

...gravely asserting that it was only to magnify the sweet fame and mercy of the most glorious God; that few men endeavored to serve Him, and he expressed this like a pastor or minister of a church. But I recalled his attention, entreating him to show me the <term>metallic mystery</term> <note>original: "metallic mystery"; the secret art of alchemy and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, also desiring that he would eat, drink, and stay at my house. I pressed him on this, but he was of such a fixed determination that all my efforts were frustrated. I could not help telling him that I had a laboratory ready for an experiment, and that a promised favor was a kind of debt. "Yes, true," said he, "but I promised to teach you upon my return, with this condition: if it is not forbidden."

When I perceived that all of this was in vain, I earnestly requested a small crumb of his <term>powder</term> <note>The "projection powder," a powdered form of the Philosopher's Stone used to trigger transmutation.</note>, sufficient to <term>transmute</term> <note>To change the nature of a substance; in alchemy, specifically turning "base" metals like lead into "noble" metals like gold.</note> a few grains of lead to gold. At last, out of his <term>philosophical commiseration</term> <note>original: "philosophical commiseration"; the stranger's pity or compassion as a fellow seeker of wisdom.</note>, he gave me a piece as small as a turnip seed, saying, "Receive this small portion of the greatest treasure in the world, which truly few kings or princes have ever seen or known." "But," I said, "this perhaps will not transmute even four grains of lead," whereupon he told me to give it back to him. In hopes of receiving a larger portion, I did so; but he, cutting half of it off with his fingernail, threw it into the fire and gave me the rest wrapped neatly in blue paper, saying, "It is still sufficient for you." 

I answered him, indeed with a very dejected face, "Sir, what does this mean? The other portion being too small, you now give me even less." He told me to put half an ounce of lead into the <term>crucible</term> <note>A ceramic or metal container used for melting materials at very high temperatures.</note>, for no more lead should be added than the <term>medicine</term> <note>original: "medicine"; alchemists often referred to the Stone as a "medicine" that "healed" or "purified" imperfect metals into gold.</note> can transmute. I gave him great thanks for my diminished treasure, which was truly concentrated to the highest degree, and put it carefully away into my little box, saying I intended to try it the next day and would not reveal it to anyone. "Not so, not so," said he, "for we ought to reveal all things to the <term>children of art</term> <note>original: "children of art"; a traditional term for fellow practitioners and students of alchemy.</note> which may lead..."

At this unexpected account, he immediately said, "You are more skillful at committing theft than at applying the medicine; for if you had only wrapped the stolen prey in yellow wax to protect it from the fumes of the lead, it would have sunk to the bottom and transmuted the lead to gold. But because you cast it directly into the fumes, the violence of the vapor—partly through its <term>sympathetic alliance</term> <note>The alchemical belief that substances with similar "spiritual" natures are naturally drawn to one another.</note>—carried the medicine entirely away." He promised to come the next morning at nine o'clock to show me that this tincture would transmute the lead into gold. 

Having taken his leave, I impatiently awaited his return, but the next day he did not come, nor has he ever since. He sent an excuse at half-past nine that morning and promised to come at three in the afternoon, but I never heard from him again. I soon began to doubt the whole matter. Late 그 night my wife, who was a most curious student and inquirer into the art, came to me, begging me to make an experiment with that little grain of the stone we had been given, just to be sure of the truth. "Unless this is done," she said, "I shall have no rest or sleep tonight." 

Since she was so earnest, I ordered a fire to be made, saying to myself, "I fear, I fear indeed, that this man has deceived me." My wife wrapped the small piece of matter in wax, and I cut half an ounce of lead and put it into a crucible in the fire. I wrapped the small pill in wax, which immediately made a hissing noise. In a quarter of an hour, the mass of lead was totally <term>transmuted</term> <note>The alchemical process of changing one element into another, specifically lead into gold.</note> into the best and finest gold, which amazed us exceedingly. We could not gaze enough upon this admirable and miraculous work of nature; for the melted lead, after <term>projection</term> <note>The final stage of the alchemical process where the "Philosopher's Stone" is cast into molten base metal to transform it.</note>, showed the rarest and most beautiful colors imaginable while on the fire, finally settling into a green color. When it was poured out into an <term>ingot</term> <note>A block of metal, typically cast into a rectangular shape.</note>, it had the lively, fresh color of blood. Once cold, it shone like the purest and most splendid gold. Truly, all those standing around me were exceedingly startled. While this "aurified" <note>original: "aurified"; turned into gold.</note> lead was still hot, I ran with it to the goldsmith, who was astonished at its fineness. After a short trial by the <term>assay</term> <note>original: "test"; a procedure to determine the purity or value of a metal.</note>, he said it was the most excellent gold in the world.

The next day, news of this <term>prodigy</term> <note>An amazing or unusual thing, especially one seen as a miracle or omen.</note> spread throughout the Hague, so that many illustrious and learned people paid me friendly visits because of it. Among the rest was the general Assay-master and examiner of coins for the province of Holland, Mr. Porelius, who—along with others—earnestly begged me to put some of the gold through all their customary tests. I did so to satisfy my own curiosity. We went to Mr. Brectel, a silversmith, who first mixed four parts of silver with one part of the gold. He then filed the mixture down and added <term>nitric acid</term> <note>original Latin: "aquafortis", literally "strong water." It was used by alchemists and smiths to separate gold from silver.</note> to it. This dissolved the silver and allowed the gold to settle as a powder at the bottom. After the solution was poured off and the <term>residue</term> <note>original: "calx"; the powdery substance remaining after a metal has been burned or chemically dissolved.</note> of gold was washed with water, then refined and melted, it appeared as excellent gold. Instead of a loss in weight, we found the gold had actually increased; it had transmuted a <term>scruple</term> <note>A small unit of weight, roughly 1.3 grams.</note> of the silver into gold through its "abounding tincture" <note>In alchemy, the "tincture" is the powerful, transformative essence of the Philosopher's Stone that spreads through other metals.</note>.

Doubting whether the silver was now sufficiently separated from the gold, we mixed it with seven parts of <term>antimony</term> <note>A brittle, silvery-white semi-metal often used in the process of refining gold.</note>. ### GUISEPPE FRANCESCO BORRI.

"The Rape of the Lock" and the graceful romance of "Undine" have familiarized everyone with the doctrine of elementary spirits <note>The belief in beings that inhabit the four elements: gnomes (earth), undines (water), sylphs (air), and salamanders (fire).</note>. However, the primary philosophical—or pseudo-philosophical—account of these unseen but not otherworldly intelligences is found in the small book by the Count of Gabalis, a series of conversations on the secret sciences. 

It is generally unknown that this work is little more than an uncredited translation of "The Key to the Cabinet of the Chevalier Borri, in which may be found various letters—curious, scientific, and chemical—with political instructions, matters that deserve the attention of the curious, and a variety of magnificent secrets."* Borri, who appears to have been a small-scale predecessor of Cagliostro <note>A famous 18th-century Italian occultist and adventurer.</note>, was born in Milan in 1627. In his early years, certain suspicious activities caused him to seek refuge in a church; however, later—much like Joseph Balsamo <note>The birth name of Count Cagliostro.</note>—he underwent a complete transformation and announced that he—

F. Borri, by means of which are seen various scientific, chemical, and very curious letters, with various political instructions, and other things worthy of curiosity and many very beautiful secrets.* Cologne (Geneva), 1681, 12mo.

->GUISEPPE FRANCESCO BORRI.<-

was inspired by Heaven, that he was elected by the omnipotent God to accomplish the reformation of mankind, and to establish the *Regnum Dei* <note>Latin: "Kingdom of God."</note>. There should be henceforth but a single religion, with the Pope as its head, and a vast army, with Borri as general, for the extermination of all anti-Catholics. He exhibited a miraculous sword which St. Michael had deigned to present to him, declared that he had beheld in the empyrean <note>The highest part of heaven, believed by ancients to be the realm of pure fire or light.</note> a luminous palm-branch reserved for his own celestial triumph, announced that the Holy Virgin was divine by nature, that she conceived by inspiration, that she was equal with her Son, and was present in the Eucharist with Him, that the Holy Spirit had taken flesh in her person, that the second and third persons of the Trinity are inferior to the Divine Father, that the fall of Lucifer involved that of a vast number of angels, who now inhabit the regions of the air, that it was by the intervention of these rebellious spirits that God created the world and gave life to all beasts, but that men were in possession of a Divine soul which God made in spite of Himself. Finally, with a contradiction more French than Italian, he gave out that he was himself the Holy Spirit incarnate.

Needless to say, this novel gospel, according to mystical imposture, brought him into conflict with church authority. He was arrested, and, on January 3, 1661, he was condemned as a heretic, and as guilty of various misdeeds. He managed to escape, took flight northward, and by the expectation of the <term>philosopher's stone</term> <note>A legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold; "stone philosophical" in the original.</note> contrived to cheat Christina, Queen of Sweden, out of a large sum of money. He traveled through various parts of Germany, making many supposed <term>projections</term> <note>The process in alchemy of casting a substance onto a molten metal to effect its transformation into gold or silver.</note>, visited the Low Countries, and in 1665 entered as a professional alchemist into the service of the King of Denmark. He announced that he was the master of a demon, who responded to his

magical evocations and dictated the operations required for the successful transmutation of metals. The name of this spirit was <term>Homunculus</term> <note>original: "spook"; in alchemy, a homunculus is a miniature human being created through artificial means</note>, which, according to Paracelsus, signifies a minute human being generated unnaturally—without the assistance of the female organism—from the sperm of a man or a boy.

The monarch, determined to monopolize the talents of his adept, decided that the laboratory of <term>Borri</term> <note>Giuseppe Francesco Borri (1627–1695) was an Italian alchemist, prophet, and charlatan who traveled through European courts</note> should be transferred to his own palace. The alchemist, with an eye toward his freedom, objected that the power of his imp would be destroyed by any attempt to separate him from a certain vast iron furnace, which was the sulfurous abode of the Homunculus. However, his royal patron was a man of resources, and the furnace was also transported. Five years passed, and after Frederick III died, his successor determined to conduct a closer investigation into the transmutatory secrets of Borri. The alchemist took flight at the rumor of this inquiry but was arrested on the frontiers of Hungary and imprisoned at Vienna. There, he was claimed by the <term>Papal Nuncio</term> <note>a permanent diplomatic representative of the Pope</note> as a fugitive condemned for his heresies. He was sent to Rome and imprisoned in the Castle of Sant'Angelo. There, he was permitted to continue his alchemical processes, which were pursued unsuccessfully until his death in the year 1695.

"The Key to the Cabinet of the Chevalier Borri" has never been actually translated; the adaptation by the <term>Abbé de Villars</term> <note>author of 'Le Comte de Gabalis,' a famous 1670 satirical novel about Rosicrucianism and elemental spirits</note> is, of course, famous throughout Europe. As to the chemical secrets contained in the original letters, it may be safely concluded that they are few and unimportant.

## JOHN HEYDON.

This <term>mountebank</term> <note>mountebank: a flamboyant charlatan or fraud who tricks people for money</note> royalist mystic has no claim to be included among alchemical philosophers, and is only

<meta>This page continues the critique of John Heydon and begins the historical account of the mysterious alchemist known as Lascaris.</meta>

LASCARIS.

It is noted here to advise students that everything relating to alchemy in the whole of his so-called works was impudently stolen from <term>Philalethes</term> <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes was the pseudonym of a famous 17th-century alchemist, likely George Starkey</note>. He practiced wholesale piracy on his contemporaries and on ancient authors with equal boldness. The account of his voyage to the land of the Rosicrucians is a distorted version of Bacon’s “Atlantis”; his apologues, epilogues, enigmas, etc., are also stolen goods; in short, whatever is of value in his books is matter borrowed from the highways and byways of occultism and heaped indiscriminately together. Everything emanating from his own weak intelligence is utterly contemptible; he was grossly superstitious and pitiably credulous, as may be seen by his medical recipes. He claimed a familiar acquaintance with the most arcane Rosicrucian mysteries and pretended that he had visited the temples, holy houses, castles, and invisible mountains of the Fraternity. Of all the alchemical liars and of all mystical charlatans who have flourished in England since the first days of Anglo-occultism, John Heydon is the leader.

### LASCARIS.

German writers have principally occupied themselves with the transmutations of this singular personage, who so successfully shrouded himself in mystery that his name, his age, his birthplace, and everything which concerns his private life are completely unknown.

He called himself Lascaris, but also adopted other names. He claimed an Eastern origin, and as he spoke Greek fluently, he passed for a descendant of the royal house of Lascaris. He represented himself as the <term>archimandrite</term> <note>a superior of a monastery or a group of monasteries in the Eastern Orthodox Church</note> of a convent on the Island of Mytilene and bore letters from the Greek patriarch of Constantinople. His mission in the West was the solicitation of alms for the

ransom of Christian prisoners in the East. He appeared for the first time in Germany at the beginning of the eighteenth century. He seemed to be about forty or fifty years old, with an attractive appearance, an agreeable manner, and was a fluent conversationalist. Finding himself unwell in Berlin, he sent for a certain apothecary, who for some reason was unable to attend; instead, the apothecary was represented on several occasions by a pupil at the stranger’s bedside. Lascaris struck up a conversation with this youth, and a kind of friendship developed between them. The apothecary’s pupil had studied Basil Valentine and had attempted experiments based on the principles of this adept <note>Basil Valentine was a legendary 15th-century alchemist whose writings were highly influential</note>. Lascaris recovered, and at the moment of departing from Berlin, he took the youth aside and presented him with a quantity of the transmuting powder. He commanded the youth to remain silent about where he had obtained it and forbade him from using it until some time after his departure. He assured him that when the skeptics of Berlin witnessed its amazing effects, no one would be able to accuse the alchemists of madness.

The name of this young man was John Frederick Bötticher. Intoxicated by the possession of such an unexpected treasure, he determined to devote himself entirely to alchemy. The apothecary, his master, tried in vain to dissuade him from a pursuit which he considered fanciful, but Bötticher astonished both him and his friends by changing silver into gold in their presence.

The experiment was repeated with mercury for the benefit of one of Bötticher’s friends. The story spread, and the apothecary’s pupil became the celebrity of Berlin, especially as he spread the report that he was able to create the <term>philosophical tincture</term> <note>A substance, often synonymous with the Philosopher's Stone, used to transmute base metals into gold</note> himself.

He was summoned before the King, Frederick William I, who wished to witness his performances, but he fled to an uncle at Wittenberg. He was claimed from the authorities

<meta>This page continues the account of Johann Friedrich Bötticher, an apothecary's apprentice turned alchemist, who claimed to possess the secret of the philosopher's stone under the guidance of the mysterious Lascaris.</meta>

# LASCARIS.

Bötticher lived in that town as a Prussian subject, but he was now a valuable prize. The Elector of Saxony <note>The Elector was the ruler of Saxony and one of the high-ranking princes who elected the Holy Roman Emperor.</note> made a competing claim for custody of him, and Bötticher decided to go to him. He was warmly welcomed, and after his transmutations <note>The supposed alchemical process of changing base metals into gold.</note> were witnessed, the title of baron was granted to him. He took up residence at Dresden, living in a style of great magnificence and wastefulness until every bit of his powder was used up. At that point, his extravagance left him deep in debt. 

His servants, whom he was unable to pay, spread the rumor that he intended to flee. The shortsighted <note>original: "purblind"</note> Elector refused to see that this sudden lack of money was proof that Bötticher was unable to create or increase the philosophers' stone <note>A legendary substance believed to turn base metals into gold and grant long life.</note>. Instead, the Elector surrounded Bötticher’s house with guards and held him essentially as a prisoner.

At this point, Lascaris, who was still traveling through Germany, took pity on the misfortunes of his young student <note>original: "neophyte"</note>. He tried to rescue him from his embarrassing situation with the help of a young doctor named Pasch, who was a personal friend of the now-noble apothecary’s apprentice. Their schemes resulted in the imprisonment of Pasch at the fortress of Sonneinstein, while Bötticher was closely confined in another castle at Kœnigstein.

Two and a half years passed. At the end of that time, Pasch succeeded in escaping, though he was badly injured <note>original: "at the expense of his limbs"</note> in the attempt. He died a few months later, bitterly complaining about the betrayal of the master alchemist <note>original: "adept"</note> Lascaris, who had completely abandoned him in his danger.

Bötticher remained in confinement with every opportunity to manufacture the philosopher's stone. However, he failed to do so. Nevertheless, between his training as an apothecary and his experiments in prison, he had become skilled in several branches of chemistry. He discovered the process for producing red porcelain, and afterwards—

<meta>This page continues the biography of Johann Friedrich Bötticher, who, after being imprisoned by the Elector of Saxony, manages to regain favor not through alchemy, but through a different "Great Work"—the invention of porcelain.</meta>

# THE LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

...that of white porcelain, which was very superior in quality to the substances already known by that name. These inventions proved as valuable to the tyrannical Elector as the accomplishment of the Great Work <note>original: "magnum opus," the ultimate goal of alchemy to create the philosophers' stone</note>. Bötticher was restored to his favor and again enjoyed his title of baron, but once he had his liberty, he surrendered himself to an immoderately luxurious life and died in 1719 at the age of thirty-seven.

Bötticher was by no means the only apothecary’s apprentice <note>original: "boy"</note> who was enriched with the powder of Lascaris and sent out to preach the gospel of alchemy with practical demonstrations. Godwin, Hermann, Braun, and Martin of Fitzlar are mentioned among these half-initiated laborers, who shone until their stock-in-trade was exhausted and then disappeared one after another.

In the meantime, Lascaris himself was not idle. On February 16, 1609, he is believed to have changed mercury into gold and gold into silver. This was a double transmutation <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals into precious ones</note> considered by alchemical experts <note>original: "connoisseurs"</note> to be evidence of an unparalleled mastery <note>original: "adeptship"</note>. Liebkneck, a counselor of Wertherbourg, was a witness to this transmutation.

In the same year, a goldsmith of Leipzig <note>original: "Leipsic"</note> was visited by a mysterious stranger, who is unanimously identified as Lascaris. The stranger showed him an ingot <note>original: "lingot"</note> which he declared was manufactured by art, and which proved in assaying <note>The process of testing a metal to determine its purity</note> to be gold of twenty-two carats. It was purified by the goldsmith with antimony, and part of it was presented to him by the unknown man as a memorial of the alleged transmutation.

Shortly after, a lieutenant-colonel in the Polish army named Schmolz de Dierbach, who had inherited a belief in alchemical science from his father, was conversing on the subject at a café when he was approached by a stranger. This stranger presented him with some powder of projection <note>A substance alchemists believed could trigger the transformation of base metal into gold</note>. It was red in color, and a microscopic...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the mysterious alchemist Lascaris, moving from his successful transmutations witnessed by others to his association with various figures in Germany, and introducing the impostor Domenico Manuel who claimed to be his disciple.</meta>

## LASCARIS.

examination revealed its crystalline nature. It increased the weight of the metals which it was supposed to transmute to an extent which chemical authorities declare to be physically impossible. The recipient made use of it generously, distributing to his friends and acquaintances the gold it produced in projection <note>The final stage of the alchemical process where the "stone" or "powder" is thrown upon the molten base metal to effect transmutation</note>. The unknown donor is identified in the imagination of German historians with the mysterious Lascaris, who is supposed, in the same anonymous and unaccountable manner, to have enriched the Baron de Creux with a box of the precious powder, and to have gratified the amateur Hermetic ambition of the Landgrave of Hesse-Darmstadt through the commonplace medium of the post. In a word, every anonymous adept who appeared at this period in or about Germany is supposed to be Lascaris.

The last of his debtors or victims was the son of a Neapolitan mason, Domenico Manuel, who claims to have been mysteriously initiated into the transmutatory art in the year 1695. He was put in possession of a small quantity of both the white and red tinctures. Being insufficient to really enrich himself, he determined to trade upon the wonders they produced, and obtained large sums from wealthy amateurs for the privilege of beholding the completion <note>original: "consummation"</note> of the Great Work. He traveled through Spain, Belgium, and Austria, obtaining large sums under the pretense of preparing the tincture, not only from private individuals, but from the Emperor Leopold and the Palatine Elector. In different places he assumed different names. Now he was Count Gaëtano, now Count de Ruggiero; at other times he called himself Field Marshal to the Duke of Bavaria, Commandant of Munich, a Prussian major-general, and by other titles. In 1705 he appeared at Berlin, where he imposed on the King himself for a brief period, after which, unable to fulfill <note>original: "ratify"</note> his

Because of his deceptive transmutation dealings, he was convicted of treason and hanged. This occurred on August 29, 1709.

## DELISLE.

This practitioner, whose first name is not mentioned by his biographers, is included by Figuier among the agents or disciples of Lascaris, and much information concerning him can be found in the *Histoire de la Philosophie Hermétique* by his contemporary, Langlet du Fresnoy. He was a peasant of humble birth in Provence, and he became familiar with alchemical experiments by entering the service of a gentleman who was believed to possess the stone. This gentleman is thought to have received the secret from Lascaris. However, his operations fell under suspicion, and he was forced to leave France. He retired to Switzerland, accompanied by Delisle, who is said to have murdered him in the mountains and thus gained possession of a significant amount of the transmuting powder. Whatever the case may be, the servant re-entered France in disguise, and around the year 1708, he attracted general attention by changing lead and iron into silver and gold. He traveled through Languedoc, Dauphiné, and Provence. At Sisteron, he became involved with the wife of a man named Alnys, who eventually shared his fortunes for three years. His fame grew because of the apparent simplicity of his methods. He spread powder and oil over iron, thrust it into the fire, and brought it out as a bar of gold. He distributed nails, knives, and rings that were partially transmuted, and he was particularly successful in his experiments with common steel.

Cerisy, the prior of New Castel, was employed by the Bishop of Senez to collect evidence regarding the truth of these wonders. An old gentleman offered Delisle a refuge at his

Delisle gained fame across southern France for performing simple alchemical demonstrations, such as transforming iron and steel into gold.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the account of the alchemist Delisle, following his arrival at the castle of La Palud and the growing interest in his alleged transmutations.</meta>

<header>DELISLE. 217</header>

castle of La Palud, where the alchemist, surrounded by admirers, received daily visits from the curious. In Lenglet’s *History of Hermetic Philosophy*, there is a letter from the Bishop of Senez to the Minister of State and Comptroller-General of the Treasury in Paris. In this letter, the prelate—who was initially skeptical—confesses his inability to resist the evidence of actual transformation performed before him and several watchful witnesses, who took every precaution against deception. There is also the report of M. de Saint-Maurice, President of the Mint at Lyons, who testifies to the following facts: 

He was accompanied by Delisle onto the grounds of the Chateau de Saint Auban in May 1710, where he uncovered a basket that was buried in the ground. In the middle of this basket was an iron wire, at the end of which he saw a piece of linen with some object tied up inside it. He took possession of this parcel, carried it into the dining room of the chateau, and—at the direction of Delisle—he exposed its contents (a blackish earth weighing about half a pound) to the rays of the sun. After fifteen minutes, the earth was distilled in a retort of a portable furnace. When a yellow liquid was seen flowing into the receiver, Delisle recommended that the container should be removed before a viscous oil, which was then rising, could flow into it. Two drops of this yellow liquid, projected onto hot <term>quicksilver</term>, produced three ounces of gold through fusion, which were presented to the Master of the Mint. Afterward, three ounces of pistol bullets were melted and purified with alum and <term>saltpeter</term>. Delisle handed Saint-Maurice a small paper, asking him to throw in a pinch of the powder and two drops of the oil used in the first experiment. This done, the substance was covered with saltpeter, kept in fusion for fifteen minutes, and then poured out onto a piece of iron armor, which emerged as pure gold.

<summary>The Bishop of Senez and M. de Saint-Maurice provide eyewitness accounts and official reports validating Delisle’s alchemical transmutations at La Palud and Saint Auban. Saint-Maurice details a specific experiment where a "blackish earth" and distilled liquids were used to transform quicksilver and lead bullets into gold.</summary>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the alchemist Delisle, detailing the official recognition of his gold and silver transmutations and his subsequent imprisonment and death.</meta>

<header>LIVES OF ALCHEMYSTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.</header>

withstanding all tests. The conversion to silver was performed in the same manner with white powder, and the certificate which testifies to these occurrences was officially signed on December 14, 1760.

A portion of the gold produced in this manner by Delisle was subjected to refinement at Paris, where three medals were struck from it; one of them was deposited in the king’s cabinet. It bore the inscription *Aurum Arte Factum.*

Despite all his alchemical skill, Delisle was unable to read or write, and in temperament he was stubborn, rude, and fanatical. He was invited to Court, but he claimed that the climate where he lived was necessary for the success of his experiments, as his preparations were vegetable-based. The Bishop of Senez, suspecting him of unwillingness rather than inability, obtained a *lettre de cachet* after two years of constant excuses on the part of the alchemist, who was then arrested and taken on the road to Paris. During the journey, his guards—after attempting to extort his supposed riches—wounded him severely on the head. In this state, upon his arrival at the Bastille, he was forced to begin his alchemical operations, but after a short time he persistently refused to proceed. In the frenzy of his desperation, he repeatedly tore the bandages from his wound, and in the year following his imprisonment, he poisoned himself.

His illegitimate son, Alnys, by some means inherited a portion of the powder from his mother. He wandered through Italy and Germany performing transmutations. On one occasion he performed a <term>projection</term> before the Duke of Richlieu, then the French ambassador at Vienna, who assured the Abbé Langlet that he not only saw the operation performed, but performed it himself, twice on gold and forty times on silver.

<summary>The alchemist Delisle is imprisoned in the Bastille after dodging royal invitations, where he eventually commits suicide; his son Alnys later demonstrates alchemical transmutations for the Duke of Richlieu.</summary>

<header>JOHN HERMANN OBEREIT. 219</header>

Alnys gathered a significant collection of gold coins, both ancient and modern, while on a journey through Austria and Bohemia. Upon his return to Aix, he presented himself to the President of Provence, who requested that he return the next day. Alnys, suspecting a plan to arrest him, fled in the meantime. He was later imprisoned at Marseilles, from where he managed to escape to Brussels. It was here, in 1731, that he gave some philosophic mercury to M. Percell, the brother of Langlet de Fresnoy; the recipient fermented this mercury imperfectly, yet succeeded far enough to convert an ounce of silver into gold. The death of a certain M. Grefier occurred shortly after some operations with corrosive sublimate, through which Alnys had intended to instruct him in alchemy. This made it necessary for him to leave, and he was never heard from again.

### JOHN HERMANN OBEREIT.

This writer, who was as much a mystic as an alchemist, was born at Arbon in Switzerland in 1725, and died in 1798. He inherited from his father a taste for transcendental chemistry and the belief that metals could be developed to their full perfection, but that the primary instrument was the grace of God working within the soul of the alchemist. He worked unceasingly at physical processes, hoping to restore the lost fortune of his family, but the authorities closed his laboratory because it endangered public safety. He managed to demonstrate the harmless nature of his work and was welcomed into the home of a brother of the physiognomist Lavater. He informs us that he celebrated a mystical marriage with a seraphic and enlightened shepherdess named Theantis; the ceremony took place in a castle on the highest peak of a mountain surrounded by clouds. His bride

The bereaved husband, throughout the entire night of her death, mourned her in a mystical song. *La Connexion Originaire des Esprits et des Corps, d’apres les principes de Newton, Augsbourg, 1776* (The Original Connection of Spirits and Bodies, according to the principles of Newton), and *Les Promenades de Gamaliel, juif Philosophe* (The Walks of Gamaliel, Jewish Philosopher), were bequeathed by Obereit to a neglectful posterity.

## TRAVELS, ADVENTURES, AND IMPRISONMENTS OF JOSEPH BALSAMO.

The notorious Count Cagliostro appears, from an impartial review of his history and phenomenal exploits, to have been one of those characters not uncommonly met with in the checkered annals of occultism. Just like modern “mediums,” who betray the confidence of their believers by tainting the supernatural bread—of which the spiritual patent is their privilege and birthright—with the unrighteous greed of material conjuring tricks; and just like those magicians who are sometimes supposed to further confuse their audience through supplementary pacts with “spirits,” this high priest of transcendental trickery seems to have possessed, perhaps unconsciously, a certain share of occult gifts, which significantly aided his unparalleled swindling. He undoubtedly possessed mystical knowledge beyond that of the age in which he lived, and though it was still superficial, he had a genius for making the most of it.

Joseph Balsamo—regardless of what has been claimed to the contrary by himself* or his admirers—was the son of Peter

# TRAVELS, ETC., OF JOSEPH BALSAMO.

Balsamo and Felicia Bracconieri, both of humble origins. He was born at Palermo, in Sicily, on the 8th of June 1743. His parents are reliably described as honest tradespeople and good Catholics, who were careful in the education of their offspring and concerned for their spiritual welfare. Their shop drew much business in the populous neighborhood which divided the handsome *Rue del Cæsaro*. While his children were still young, Peter Balsamo died; left under the inadequate control of a widowed mother, Joseph showed, even in his earliest years, a selfish and lazy disposition, greatly neglecting the meager educational advantages which were afforded to him. According to other accounts, he was taken under the protection of his maternal uncle, who endeavored to instruct him in the principles of religion and to give him an education suitable to his age and prospects; but, even from his infancy, he showed himself consistently averse to a virtuous life. His uncle was a worthy citizen of Palermo, who foresaw, through the liveliness and insight of his nephew,

<margin>N.r.</margin>
> "...of the parents who gave birth to me. All my research on these points has provided me with nothing but vague and uncertain, though, in truth, exalted, notions. My earliest childhood was passed in the town of Medina, in Arabia, where I was brought up under the name of Acharat—a name which I afterward used during my Asian and African travels—and was lodged in the palace of the mufti. I distinctly recollect having four persons continually about me—a tutor, between fifty-five and sixty years of age, named Altotas, and three slaves, one of whom was white, while the others were black. My tutor always told me that I had been left an orphan at the age of three months, and that my parents were noble, and Christians as well, but he preserved the most absolute silence as to their name and as to the place where I was born, though certain chance words led me to suspect that I first saw the light at Malta. Altotas took pleasure in cultivating my natural taste for the sciences; he himself was proficient in all, from the most profound even to the most trivial. It was in botany and physics that I made the most progress. Like my instructor, I wore the dress of a Muslim, and outwardly we professed the Islamic law. The principles of the true religion were, however, engraved in our hearts. I was frequently visited by the mufti, who treated me with much kindness and had great respect for my instructor, through whom I early became proficient in most oriental languages.”

Consequently, at thirteen years old, he was enrolled in the Seminary of St. Roch in Palermo, where he demonstrated his independence and hatred for discipline by repeatedly running away. After being caught in the company of vagrants, he was handed over—with a poor reputation—to a certain father-general of the *Bon Fratelli* <note>The "Good Brothers," a religious order dedicated to nursing</note> who was traveling through Palermo. The father-general took responsibility for him and immediately took him to a Benedictine convent on the outskirts of Cartagirone. There the walls were high, and the "caged dove" was kept by a strict *frère tourier* <note>A lay brother who manages the monastery gate</note>. He was forced to wear the habit of a novice. When the father-general discovered his aptitude for natural history and especially his interest in herbs, he placed him under the instruction of the convent’s apothecary, from whom, as he later admitted, he learned the basic principles of chemistry and medicine. Figuier states that within a short time, he was able to handle drugs with remarkable skill; however, even then it was noted that he seemed eager to find those secrets that would aid him in a life of trickery.† True to his naturally difficult character, he repeatedly displayed his inherent bad behavior, bringing frequent punishment upon himself from his superiors.

One day, this reluctant novice—whose misbehavior was somewhat excusable due to the forced nature of his situation, but who often crossed all bounds—was assigned to read a section of a very inspiring martyrology <note>A collection of stories about the lives and deaths of martyrs</note> during dinner in the refectory. # TRAVELS, ETC., OF JOSEPH BALSAMO.

...says one writer with pious indignation, following an inspiration from Belial, he replaced the sacred text with a blasphemous version suggested by his own depraved imagination. He distorted the meaning and the events, and was so audacious that he substituted the names of the saints with those of the most notorious courtesans of the period. A severe penance was imposed on the insolent offender, but one night he found a way to evade the watchfulness of his guardians. He escaped from the convent, crossed the countryside, and after some days of cheerful roaming and wandering as a vagabond, he arrived at Palermo.

A basic knowledge of the principles of chemistry and medicine was essentially the total of the benefits he had gained from the discipline of life in the monastery. His uncle began to lose hope for him, but advice and protests were both wasted on the young rascal, who mocked them all; and while he spent a certain portion of his time cultivating a natural talent for drawing, he otherwise abandoned himself to unrestrained excess.

He associated with scoundrels and good-for-nothings; his drunkenness, gambling, and general debauchery led him into constant brawling. He was frequently in the hands of the police, whom he is said to have taken special pleasure in resisting, often using force to free the prisoners whom they had arrested.

He has also been accused of forging theater tickets and selling them with his typical boldness. One of his uncles persuaded him to return to his house for a time and was rewarded by the theft of a significant amount of money and some valuable items.

He became a middleman in the romantic affair of a female cousin and one of his friends. He carried love letters <note>billets-doux</note> back and forth between them and made the entire transaction personally profitable by extorting money from his friend, convincing him that the beautiful cousin had a fondness for gifts, including both

money and jewelry, and, of course, stealing the funds which were entrusted to him. More serious crimes were soon attributed to him. There was a certain corrupt Marquis Maurigi in Palermo who desired an inheritance that had been left to a religious institution, and knowing Balsamo, he shared his plans with him; a solution was soon provided. Joseph had a relative who was a notary, and by visiting his office, he found a way to forge a will—bearing every mark of authenticity—in favor of the Marquis. The Marquis successfully claimed the estate and undoubtedly rewarded his accomplice's skill and efforts generously. The forgery was discovered many years later, but both guilty parties were long gone. It was also rumored that Balsamo was involved in the assassination of a wealthy <term>canon</term>, but the matter is very uncertain. He was arrested many times on various charges but escaped justice, either due to a lack of direct evidence against him or because of his family's reputation and the efforts of respectable people in Palermo who cared about his family. It is hard to believe that at this time Balsamo was only fourteen years old. Naturally gifted with artistic talent, he soon began giving drawing lessons and seemed to be on the path to reform several times. His skill with weapons is also recognized, but being aware of his own talent, his street fights often ended in duels; his impulsiveness even led him to defend his companions <note>take up the gauntlet</note>, and he had no fear of danger.

The most infamous of his youthful escapades, and the one that forced him to begin his lifelong travels, was the adventure of the hidden treasure, which has been told in various ways.

### TRAVELS, ETC., OF JOSEPH BALSAMO.

...in magic, says M. Louis Figuier—and he was much attracted by the mystery which, even at this period, Figuier declares to have surrounded the life and escapades of Balsamo, who already posed as an initiate of the occult sciences. Joseph was now seventeen years old, with a handsome appearance and a proud posture; he spoke little but held his listeners spellbound by the magnetic fascination of his gaze. He had been seen summoning spirits; he was believed to converse with angels and to obtain an insight into the most interesting secrets through their help. He had, in fact, radically changed; the common rogue was developing into a <term>transcendental impostor</term>. Marano listened closely to the stories about him and was eager to see "the friend of the celestial spirits." Their first meeting took place at Balsamo’s lodgings; the goldsmith fell on his knees before him, and Balsamo, after receiving his tribute, raised him graciously from the ground and solemnly asked why he had come to him.

"Thanks to your daily communication with spirits, you will easily know that," answered Marano, "and you should have no difficulty in helping me recover the money I have wasted on fake alchemists, or even in getting me more."

"I can do this for you, provided you believe," said Balsamo calmly.

"Provided I believe!" cried the goldsmith. "I certainly believe."

An appointment was made for the following day in a meadow beyond the town, and the meeting ended without another word.

This version of the story is more romantic than likely, and we owe it to the vividness of a Frenchman’s imagination, which is never more brilliant than when used in

According to the more sober *Aventures de Cagliostro*, Marano had for some time been acquainted with the youthful charlatan, who sought him out one day at his own residence and said to him: "You are aware of my communications with the heavenly spirits; you are aware of the limitless power of the incantations to which I devote myself. Listen! In an olive field, at no great distance from Palermo, there is a buried treasure according to my certain knowledge, and by the help of a ceremonial evocation I can discover the precise spot where the seeker's spade should be driven in. The operation, however, requires some expensive preliminaries; sixty ounces of gold are absolutely needed. Will you place them at my disposal?"

Marano protested against the absurd extravagance of the demand, maintaining that the herbs and drugs used in alchemical experiments were exceedingly *moderate* in their price.

"It is well," said Balsamo, coldly. "The matter is soon settled; I shall enjoy the vast treasure alone. A blessing when shared is but half a blessing for those who participate in it."

On the next day, however, Marano sought out the enchanter, having been tormented by the gold fever the whole night.

"I have the sum you require," he said. "But I pray you to bargain a little with the spirits, and endeavor to lower their price."

"Do you take them for greedy speculators?" cried the magician, indignant. "The devil is no miser, though he lived for a long time in Judea. He is a magnificent lord, living generously in every country of the world. Treat him with respect, and he returns it a hundredfold. I shall find the sixty ounces of gold elsewhere, and can afford to do without your assistance."

# TRAVELS, ETC., OF JOSEPH BALSAMO.

"It is here," said Marano, quickly drawing a leather bag from his pocket, and the arrangements were soon made.

In the moonlight, they went to the olive field, where Balsamo had secretly made preparations for the upcoming evocation. The incantatory preliminaries were sufficiently prolonged, and Marano panted in terror under the influence of the magical spells, until it seemed to him that the very earth shivered beneath his feet and phantoms emerged from the ground. Marano fell flat on his face—an action apparently foreseen—for then and there the wretched goldsmith was beaten unmercifully with sticks by the "infernal spirits," who finally left him for dead, fleeing in the company of the enchanter and strengthened by the possession of the sixty ounces of gold. The next day, the goldsmith, fortunately discovered by muleteers, was carried disconsolately home and immediately denounced Balsamo to the law. The adventure became known everywhere, but the magician had already sailed for Messina.

These are the facts of the case, but the lying chronicle of Louis Figuier, alchemical critic and universal manufacturer of light scientific literature, offers us a far more ornate and attractive version. In that version, the adept and his miserable dupe go to an appointed place at six o’clock in the morning, with Balsamo in dignified silence motioning the goldsmith to follow him, proceeding with a preoccupied appearance along the road to the chapel of Saint Rosalia for the space of a full hour. They stopped at length in the middle of a wild meadow and in front of a grotto, before which Balsamo extended his hand and solemnly declared that a treasure was buried within it which he himself was forbidden to touch. It was guarded by devils of hell, though these devils might, however, be bound for a short period by the angels who commonly responded to his potent magical call.

“It only remains to be determined,” he remarked in conclusion, “whether you will scrupulously fulfill the conditions which must be imposed on you. At that price, the treasure may be yours.”

The credulous goldsmith impetuously implored him to name them.

“They cannot be learned from my lips,” said Balsamo haughtily. “On your knees, first of all!”

He himself had already assumed a posture of worship. Marano hastened to imitate him, and immediately a clear, harmonious voice from the heavenly heights pronounced the following words—words, says the Frenchman, more delicious to the ears of the covetous miser than all the symphonies of aerial choirs.

“Sixty ounces of pearls, sixty ounces of rubies, sixty ounces of diamonds, in a chest of engraved gold, weighing one hundred and twenty ounces. The infernal spirits who protect this treasure will place it in the hands of the worthy man whom our friend has brought, if he is fifty years of age, if he is no Christian—if—if—if—” and a series of conditions followed which Marano perfectly matched in his own stingy person, even to the last, which was formulated this way:—“And if he deposits at the entrance of the grotto, before setting foot inside, sixty ounces of gold to appease the guardians.”

“You have heard,” said Balsamo, who, already on his feet, began to retrace his steps, completely ignoring the utter amazement of his companion.

“Sixty ounces of gold!” the miser cried with a dismal groan, torn by the internal conflict of greed and desire; but Balsamo paid as little attention to the exclamation as he did to the groan, and returned to the town in silence.

When they were on the point of separating, Marano appeared to have made up his mind.

“Grant me one instant!” he cried in a piteous voice. 

“Sixty ounces of gold? Is that the irrevocable condition?” 

“Undoubtedly,” said Balsamo, carelessly. 

“Alas! Alas! And at what hour tomorrow?” 

“At six o’clock in the morning and, take note, at the same spot.” 

“I will be there.”

This was the parting speech of the goldsmith, and, as it were, the last gasp of his conquered avarice. On the morrow, punctual to the appointed time, they met as before: Balsamo with his habitual coolness, Marano with his gold. They arrived in due course at the grotto, where the angels, consulted as on the previous day, returned the same oracles. Balsamo assumed ignorance of what would take place. With a terrific struggle, Marano deposited his gold and prepared to cross the threshold. He took one step forward, then started back, inquiring if there were any danger in penetrating into the depths of the cavern. He was assured of safety if the gold had been faithfully weighed. He entered with more confidence, and again returned, these maneuvers being repeated several times under the eyes of the adept, whose expression indicated the most disinterested indifference. At length, Marano took courage and proceeded so far that a return was impossible, for three black, muscular devils started out from the shadows and barred his path, giving vent to the most alarming growls. They seized him, forced him to whirl round and round for a long time, and then—while the unhappy creature vainly invoked the assistance of Balsamo—they proceeded to beat him vigorously until he dropped, overwhelmed, to the ground. At that point, a clear voice bade him remain absolutely silent and motionless, for he would be instantaneously dispatched if he stirred either hand or foot. The wretched man did not

dare to disobey, but after a long fainting spell the complete stillness encouraged him to raise his head; he dragged himself as best he could to the mouth of the terrible grotto, looked around him, and found that the adept, the demons, and the gold had all vanished.

When Balsamo arrived at Messina, he was provided with a very handsome sum of money to support the expenses of his stay there, for the lion's share of the loot obtained from the goldsmith had, of course, fallen to himself. He stayed in one of the main inns near the port and had prepared himself for further adventures when he suddenly remembered that he had an old and wealthy aunt in the town whom he took the opportunity to visit, but only to discover that she had recently died, leaving the bulk of her fortune to various churches of Messina and distributing the rest to the poor. No doubt the dutiful nephew paid a fitting tribute of regret to the memory of this very devout relative, and being anxious to inherit at least something from a person of such great holiness, he determined to assume her family name, joined to a title of nobility. From that time forward, he commonly called himself the Count Alessandro Cagliostro. His sharp and calculating mind, says one of his biographers, understood the prestige that was attached to a title at a time when the privileges of birth still exercised an almost undisputed influence.

It was in the town of Messina that Balsamo first met the mysterious alchemist Altotas, whom in his fictional autobiography he represented as the Eastern tutor of his childhood. As he was walking one day near the jetty at the far end of the port, he encountered an individual dressed strangely and possessing a most remarkable face. According to other accounts, he was a Spaniard or a Greek. He wore a type of caftan and a silk bonnet, and the ends of his breeches were hidden in a pair of wide boots. In his left hand he held a parasol, and in his right the end of a cord, to which a graceful Albanian greyhound was attached.

Whether from curiosity or by a premonition, Cagliostro saluted this strange-looking man, who bowed slightly, but with a satisfied dignity.

> “You do not live in Messina, signor?” he said in Sicilian, but with a noticeable foreign accent.

Cagliostro replied that he was staying for a few days, and they began to talk about the beauty of the town and its favorable location. A kind of oriental imagery characterized the eloquence of the stranger, whose remarks were, furthermore, skillfully adorned with a few appropriate compliments. He avoided questions about his own identity but offered to reveal the past of Count Cagliostro and to disclose what was actually going through his mind at that moment. When Cagliostro hinted at sorcery, the Armenian smiled somewhat scornfully and spoke at length on the ignorance of a nation that confused science with witchcraft and prepared the stake for discoverers.

His listener, much interested, ventured to ask for the address of the illustrious stranger, who graciously invited him to call. They walked past the cathedral and stopped in a small square street shaded by sycamores, which had a charming fountain in the center.

> “Signor,” said the stranger, “there is the house where I live. I receive no one; but as you are a traveler, as you are young and courteous, and as, moreover, you are driven by a noble passion for the sciences, I permit you to visit me. On the next day, at the appointed time, he knocked at the door of the little house inhabited by the Armenian. It was opened at the fifth knock without any visible means, and closed as soon as the visitor had entered. Cagliostro cautiously advanced along a narrow passage, lit by a small iron lamp in a niche of the wall. At the end of the passage a spacious door sprang open, giving entrance into a ground-floor parlor which was lit by a four-branched candelabra holding wax candles; it was, in fact, a laboratory furnished with all the equipment used by practical alchemists. The Armenian, emerging from a neighboring side-room, greeted the visitor, inquired after the safety of the gold, learned the truth of his foresight and of the deserved fate of the criminal, but cut short Cagliostro’s expressed astonishment and admiration by declaring that the art of divination was simply the result of scientific combinations and close observations. He ended by asking his listener if he denied the infallible certainty of judicial astrology, but the self-styled count denied nothing except the superior power of virtue over self-interest, at which the Armenian asked to whom he was indebted for his training.

“The devil!” ejaculated Cagliostro.

“Therefore, with your permission, I intend to become your disciple.”

The Armenian extended his hand, and their departure was fixed for the next day.

This Altotas, or Althotes, we are assured by Figuier, was no imaginary character. The Roman Inquisition collected many proofs of his existence, without, however, ascertaining where it began or ended, for the mysterious personage vanished like a meteor. According to the Italian biography of Joseph Balsamo, Altotas was in possession of several Arabic manuscripts, and assumed great skill in chemistry. According to Figuier, he was a magician and doctor as well, though others represent him despising and rejecting the abused name of physician. As to his divinatory abilities, he had already given a signal proof of their

They embarked on board a Genoese vessel, sailed along the Archipelago, and landed at Alexandria, where they stayed for forty days. During that time, they performed several operations in chemistry, by which they are said to have produced a considerable sum of money, though whether by alchemical transmutation or by fraud is not entirely clear. Cagliostro’s respect for his master did not prevent him, with true Sicilian subtlety, from inquiring about his own background, until Altotas—weary of resorting to the same tactics of evasion—declared to him once and for all that he himself was in complete ignorance regarding his birth and parentage.

"This may surprise you," he said, "but science, which can enlighten us regarding others, is almost always unable to instruct us concerning ourselves."

He declared himself to be much older than he appeared, but said that he was in possession of certain secrets for the preservation of strength and health. He had discovered scientific methods for producing gold and precious stones, spoke ten or twelve languages fluently, and was acquainted with almost the entire circle of human sciences. "Nothing astonishes me," he said, "nothing grieves me, except the evils which I am powerless to prevent, and I trust to reach the end of my long existence in peace."

He confessed that his name of Altotas was self-chosen, yet it was truly his. His early years had been passed on the coast of Barbary, near Tunis, where he belonged to a Muslim privateer who was a rich and humane man. This master had purchased him from pirates, by whom he had been stolen from his family. At twelve years of age, he spoke Arabic like a native, read the Quran to his master, who was a true believer, studied botany under his direction, and learned the best methods for making sherbet and

In his will, he gave the young slave his liberty and bequeathed him a sum equivalent to six thousand *livres*, with which Altotas left Tunis to indulge his passion for traveling.

Cagliostro claimed that he had followed his instructor into Africa and the heart of Egypt, that he visited the pyramids, made the acquaintance of the priests of different temples, and penetrated into the *arcana* of their mysterious sanctuaries. Moreover, he declares that he visited all the principal kingdoms of Africa and Asia over the course of three years. These statements are identical in value to the romantic story of his education in the palace of the *mufti* at Medina. It is altogether doubtful whether he ever visited Arabia, which was in any case the extreme limit of his wanderings; he is subsequently discovered at Rhodes, still in the company of Altotas and pursuing, along with that mysterious being, his questionable chemical operations.

At Malta, they had letters of introduction to the Grand Master, Pinto, and stayed for some time to work in his laboratory, for the “supreme chief of Maltese chivalry” was infatuated with alchemical experiments and, after the fashion of that extravagant period, had a strong bias toward the marvelous. The record of the failure or success of these wandering adepts remains in the laboratory of the Grand Master; but from this moment Altotas—the chemist and alchemist, the phenomenal wise man and scientist—disappears completely. “Malta was his tomb, or perhaps the place of his divine transformation.” “There,” says the Count in his *Memoir*, “it was my misery to lose my best friend, the wisest, most enlightened of mortals, the vener-

...venerable Altotas. He clasped my hands shortly before his death. “My son,” he said, in a failing voice, “keep always before your eyes the fear of the Eternal and the love of your neighbor. You will soon learn the truth of all that I have taught you.”

With every mark of respect from the Grand Master, and accompanied by the Chevalier d’Aquino—of the illustrious house of Caramania and himself a Knight of Malta—Cagliostro traveled to Naples. There, he supported himself for some time with money that had been presented to him by Pinto, and perhaps by loans from his possibly wealthy companion, who, however, eventually left him to proceed to France. In Naples, Cagliostro met a Sicilian prince who was afflicted by the widespread gold fever. The prince was so enthralled with Cagliostro's grand-sounding theories that he invited him to his chateau near Palermo, where they might pursue their work together. It was unwise, but the pupil of the great Altotas could not resist the desire to revisit his native land. He stayed for a certain period with his companion, but while visiting Messina one day, he encountered an old acquaintance: a certain dissolute priest who had been his accomplice in the Marano affair. In fact, this priest had acted as one of the dark-clad fiends whose heavy clubs had tortured the unfortunate goldsmith. The adventurer warned Cagliostro not to enter Palermo, where the law was still highly offended by his youthful indiscretions. He persuaded Cagliostro to join forces with him, return to Naples, and there open a gambling house for the benefit—or rather, the fleecing—of the wealthy foreigners who visited Italy. This method of gold-making was very much to his listener's liking, and Cagliostro soon took his leave of the Sicilian prince; however, they were regarded with so much suspicion by the Neapolitan government that they retreated into the Papal States. <meta>This page continues the account of Cagliostro’s activities in Rome following his arrival from Naples, detailing his rise in Roman society and the conflicting reports of his conduct.</meta>

His companion had, however, received the tonsure, and he trembled for his safety on the consecrated ground which was the stronghold of the Holy Inquisition; so he hastened his departure to less orthodox places and does not figure further in the checkered history of his brother in trickery.

Cagliostro remained, and is said to have assumed several different characters, occasionally including the priestly habit. According to some accounts, he made himself remarkable for his extreme piety, visiting all the churches, fulfilling the duties of religion, and frequenting the palaces of cardinals. By means of some letters of recommendation which he had brought with him from Naples, he obtained access to several persons of distinction, among others to the Seneschal de Breteuil, at that time Ambassador from Malta to Rome, and who, hearing of his former connection with the Grand Master, received him with much warmth and procured him other honorable connections. One illustrious dupe ensured others, and we find him in a short time established in the Holy City, selling wonderful recipes and cures for all the diseases which afflict fallen humanity in Rome and the universe. Crowns and ducats flowed in upon him; he lived in some state and luxury, refraining, however, from scandalous enjoyments.

The Italian biography which represents the opinions, embodies the researches, and champions the cause of the Inquisition, draws, however, a different picture to those of Saint-Felix and Louis Figuier. "He employed himself at this period," says this doubtful—because indisputably biased—authority, "in making drawings on paper, the outlines of which were produced by means of a copper-plate engraving, and afterwards were filled up with India ink. These he sold as designs made by means of the pen alone. Having taken up his abode at the Sign of the Sun,

Whatever these statements are worth, there is no doubt hanging over the most important incident of his Roman career. It was in that place and at this period that he first beheld the young and beautiful Lorenza Feliciani; and having, in two days, fallen violently in love with her, he demanded her in marriage from her father. The father, fascinated by his birth, his aristocratic name, and opulent appearance, consented, along with the lady. The marriage took place, not without *éclat* <note>brilliant display or social distinction</note>, says one group of witnesses, and the pair resided in the house of the father-in-law. The Italian biography, minimizing to the utmost the success of Cagliostro, says that he received as a dowry a trifling fortune proportionate to their condition.

According to the testimony of all the biographers, inquisitorial or otherwise, Lorenza was not only young and beautiful, but "rich in every quality of the heart, being tender, devoted, honest, and modest." However, her husband conceived the diabolical design of advancing his fortunes at the expense of her honor, and in private conversation took the opportunity to mock her notions of virtue, which he sought to undermine. The first lesson which the young bride received from her husband, according to her own confession, was intended to instruct her in the means of attracting and gratifying the passions of the opposite sex. The most wanton coquetry and the most lustful arts were the principles with which he endeavored to inspire her. The mother of Lorenza, scandalized at his conduct, had such frequent altercations with her son-in-law that he resolved to move from her house, and in other quarters found it a simpler task to corrupt the mind and morals of his wife. Then, according to the Italian author, he presented her to two persons well qualified for the exercise of

His behavior causes a conflict with his mother-in-law, leading the couple to move out so he can continue his influence over his wife more easily.</summary>
