Cagliostro himself conducted her to the house intended for the lover's pleasure, left her alone in his company, and retired to another room.

The interview and the offers made to her were such as entirely corresponded to the wishes of the husband, but the wife on this occasion did not show a proper example of marital obedience; and upon sharing the whole affair confidentially with her husband, she received the most bitter reproaches and the most violent and dreadful threats. He also repeatedly assured her that adultery was no crime when it was committed by a woman to advance her interests, and not through affection for other men. He even added example to instruction by showing how little he himself respected the bonds of marital fidelity—that is, apparently, he sold himself to lustful older women, and on these occasions aroused his dormant passions by drinking a certain Egyptian wine, composed of aromatics which possessed the necessary qualities for fulfilling his intentions. His wife, finally listening to his instructions, was taken several times to the place where she had previously been so disobedient to his orders. She sometimes received, says the same witness, either clothes or trinkets, and sometimes a little money, as the reward for her compliance. One day her husband wrote a letter in the name of his wife, in which he begged for a loan of a few crowns; these were immediately sent. In return for them, an interview was promised for the next day, and the lady kept the appointment.

he corrupted his wife’s morals. Indeed, the only question is whether the transaction occurred on the sordid scale described by the Italian writer. Other authorities tell us that his success tempted “a beautiful Roman—Lorenza Feliciani—to share his rising fortunes. Unscrupulous, witty, and fascinating, Lorenza was an admirable partner for Cagliostro, who quickly made her an adept in all his pretended mysteries.” Whatever her natural virtues or failings were, it is highly improbable that she sold her uncommon attractions for such paltry and miserable advantages.

The house taken by Cagliostro became a resort for swindlers, two of whom—Ottavio Nicestro, who was eventually hanged, and a so-called Marquis d’Agriata, both Sicilians—became intimate associates of their host. He was frequently closeted with the latter for hours at a time. Their occupation is uncertain; but since Cagliostro’s wealth increased at an extraordinary rate, and since the Marquis was exceptionally skilled in producing counterfeit writing, they are supposed to have succeeded in forging numerous bills of exchange. It is, at any rate, certain that the letters patent by which the great charlatan was authorized to assume the uniform of a Prussian colonel—which he later did to his clear advantage—were the work of this skillful criminal. However, a quarrel arose among the three accomplices; Nicastro betrayed his partners, the Marquis fled from Rome, and Cagliostro and the unhappy Lorenza immediately followed his example.

Our three fugitives took the road to Venice and reached Bergamo, where they practiced several unspecified swindles until the government discovered their identities. The Marquis again managed to escape; the others, after a short imprisonment, were expelled from the town. Being stripped of all their resources, they undertook a pil—

...pilgrimage into Galicia, hoping to cross Spain through the charity of the clergy and monastic communities. They traveled through the territories of the King of Sardinia, through Genoa, and thus arrived at Antibes. From this moment, the life of Count Cagliostro was one of constant wandering for several years. According to his Italian biographer, as begging proved unprofitable, Lorenza was again forced by her husband to increase their resources by selling her charms. In this way, they arrived at Barcelona, where they stayed for six months; this same course of infamous prostitution—followed by Lorenza with the most obvious reluctance—contributed mainly to their support.

From Barcelona, they went to Madrid, where certain noble Spaniards also proved susceptible to the charms of Lorenza. From Madrid, they journeyed to Lisbon, and from there sailed to England, where Cagliostro is said to have adopted the profession of an ordinary quack, to have fallen into prison, and to have been released by his wife, through whom he still continued to trade, bartering her charms to every wealthy man who wished to become a purchaser; however, the frequency of her prostitution has probably been grossly exaggerated.

An *English Life of the Count Cagliostro*, dedicated in 1787 to the Countess and written in the interests of the charlatan, gives a singular account of his misfortunes in London. It shows that when he arrived there, he was in possession of silverware, jewels, and cash to the amount of three thousand pounds; that he hired apartments in Whitcomb Street, where he dedicated a large portion of his time to his favorite studies of chemistry and physics; and that all he suffered must be attributed entirely to the profuse generosity and charity of himself and his wife.

<meta>This page continues the account of Cagliostro and Lorenza's travels, transitioning from their arrival in England and their return to the continent, specifically their move to France and the beginning of Cagliostro's career as a self-proclaimed alchemist.</meta>

In 1772, Cagliostro and his wife crossed over to France, accompanied by a man named M. Duplaisir, who lodged with them at Paris and seems to have been intimate with Lorenza. But Cagliostro was insatiable, says St. Felix. He sold his honor at a high price, and the fortune of Duplaisir melted in the crucible of another's follies and extravagances. At length, in alarm, the victim took leave of his rapacious guests, not without strongly warning Lorenza to return to her parents, for he had learned to esteem the natural good qualities which she possessed. According to one account, she attempted to follow this advice, but others say that she sought refuge from incessant prostitution with Duplaisir himself. In either case, Cagliostro appealed to the authority of the king, and obtaining an order for her arrest, she was imprisoned in the penitentiary of Sainte Pélagie and was detained there several months, during which Cagliostro gave himself over to a life of compatible dissipation. The sale of a specific wash for beautifying the complexion appears to have provided him with a significant income during this period.

The imprisonment of Lorenza did not prevent a reconciliation with her husband immediately after her release, which occurred on December 21, 1772. On that date, having obtained some magnificent dresses from the costumers under false pretenses, Cagliostro appeared at a dancing-master's ball in a particularly brilliant costume.

It is from this period that our adventurer's success as an alchemist must be dated. Here he found means to form an acquaintance with two persons of distinction, who carried their love of chemistry to a ridiculous excess. He pretended to have discovered some miraculous secrets in the transcendent science, proclaimed himself publicly a guardian of the Hermetic Mystery, and posed as a supernatural personage in possession of the great <term>arcanum</term>

### LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

...of the philosopher's stone and of the glorious elixir of life. This was also the era of mesmerism <note>A precursor to hypnotism, named after Franz Mesmer, which was wildly popular in the late 18th century.</note>, a new science that Cagliostro decided to exploit. After a while, according to the Italian biography, the two people he was tricking began to doubt his honesty. Fearing arrest, he obtained a passport under a fake name and fled quickly to Brussels. He then traveled through Germany and Italy before returning once more to his home city of Palermo.*

In Palermo, he was quickly arrested by the relentless Marano. However, he had obtained a strong recommendation to a nobleman while in Naples, and this man's protection ensured his quick release. He then sailed with his wife for Malta. According to the Italian biographer, he officially supported himself there by selling a complexion cream <term>original: pomade</term>, though his more reliable source of income appears to have been his wife. <note>The text implies that Cagliostro may have been profiting from his wife's infidelity or prostitution.</note> Monsieur Saint-Félix, however, claims—and this seems most likely—that the Grand Master of the Knights of Malta received them with great honor. In either case, they soon moved to Naples, where Cagliostro publicly taught both chemistry and the Kabbalah <term>a form of Jewish mysticism</term> for three months. While in Naples, they were joined by Feliciani’s younger brother, a boy named Paolo, who was known for his extraordinary...

* "At a later time, when Cagliostro—emboldened by fame and wealth—returned to Paris in a grand carriage with many attendants, he vigorously denied his first stay in the capital and the shameful incident at the Sainte-Pélagie <note>A famous prison in Paris used for debtors and later for political prisoners.</note>. loveliness. Cagliostro, seeing that he might prove useful, persuaded him to share their fortunes. They embarked with a large entourage for Marseilles, and from there proceeded to Barcelona. The star of the great adventurer was now clearly rising, and from this time on he seems always to have traveled in grand style. He met, however, with no victims of importance in the Spanish peninsula until he reached its southern tip, where he swindled a fanatical alchemist out of a hundred thousand crowns under the pretense of a colossal achievement of the *magnum opus* <term>the "Great Work" of alchemy, referring to the creation of the philosopher's stone</term>. After this notable success, he immediately departed for England, while Paolo—with whom he had argued—returned to Rome, much to the sorrow of his sister.

The beginning of Cagliostro’s era of grandeur is dated from his second visit to London. It was then that he was initiated into Freemasonry and conceived his ambitious project of the mysterious Egyptian rite. Saint Félix attributes a secret objective to him from the very moment of his admission into the order. Cagliostro, he informs us, was determined to one day seat himself on the throne of the Grand Master of a rival and more powerful institution; henceforth, he appears to have lived in pursuit of this high aspiration and to have avoided—theoretically, at least—all petty scams.

He frequently visited the various London lodges, and a correspondence printed in English at Strasbourg during the year 1788 relates that, by pure chance, he picked up a curious manuscript at an obscure London bookstall. This manuscript appears to have belonged to a certain George Gaston, who is completely unknown. It dealt with Egyptian Masonry and was filled with magical and mystical ideas that excited the curiosity of its purchaser, fueled both his ambition and his imagination, and in a short time he developed his own system from its suggestive hints. The

The source of his inspiration, of course, remained concealed. He pretended to have received his Masonic tradition by succession from Enoch and Elias. Privately, however, he pursued his former dishonest dealings, and his stay in London was frequently disturbed by his squabbles with the police. Those who are interested in this part of the Cagliostro controversy would do well to refer to the English biography, dedicated to the countess, which contains much curious information.

When all his plans were finalized, he departed for the Hague, and from there proceeded to Venice, where some of his English creditors seem to have disturbed his peace and prompted him, as a consequence, to retreat through Germany into Holstein, where he is supposed to have visited the renowned Count de St Germain.

According to the *Mémoires Authentiques pour servir à l’Histoire du Comte de Cagliostro* <note>Authentic Memoirs Serving as a History of the Count de Cagliostro</note>, published in 1785, he requested an audience with this man of inscrutable mystery so that he might prostrate himself before the *dieu des croyants* <gloss>god of the believers</gloss>. With characteristic eccentricity, the Count de Saint-Germain appointed two in the morning as the hour for the interview. When that moment arrived, say the "Memoirs," Cagliostro and his wife, dressed in white garments and fastened at the waist with rose-colored sashes, presented themselves at the fortified temple of mystery, which was the home of the questionable deity whom they desired to adore. The drawbridge was lowered; a man six feet tall, dressed in a long gray robe, led them into a dimly lit chamber. There, some folding doors suddenly sprang open, and they saw a temple illuminated by a thousand wax candles, with the Count de Saint-Germain enthroned upon the altar; at his feet, two acolytes swung golden thuribles <gloss>incense burners</gloss>, which spread sweet and subtle perfumes. The divinity wore upon his breast a diamond pentagram of

A majestic statue, white and translucent, held up a vase on the steps of the altar inscribed with "Elixir of Immortality," while a vast mirror hung on the wall; before it, a living being, as majestic as the statue, walked back and forth. Above the mirror were these singular words: "Storehouse of Wandering Souls." The most solemn silence prevailed in this sacred retreat, but at length a voice, which seemed hardly a voice, pronounced these words: "Who are you? From where do you come? What do you want?" Then the Count and Countess Cagliostro prostrated themselves, and the Count answered after a long pause, "I come to invoke the God of the faithful, the Son of Nature, the father of truth. I come to demand of him one of the fourteen thousand seven hundred secrets which are treasured in his breast; I come to proclaim myself his slave, his apostle, his martyr."

The divinity did not respond, but after a long silence, the same voice asked: "What does the partner of your long wanderings intend?"

"To obey and to serve," answered Lorenza.

Simultaneously with her words, profound darkness replaced the glare of light, uproar followed tranquility, terror followed trust, and a sharp and menacing voice cried loudly: "Woe to those who cannot stand the tests!"

Husband and wife were immediately separated to undergo their respective trials, which they endured with exemplary fortitude, and which are detailed in the text of the memoirs. When the theatrical mummery was over, the two candidates were led back into the temple with the promise of admission to the divine mysteries. There, a man mysteriously draped in a long mantle cried out to them: "Know that the <term>arcanum</term> of our great art is the government of mankind, and that the one means to rule them is never to tell them the truth. ### LIVES OF ALCHEMYSTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

Do not regulate your actions according to the rules of common sense; instead, defy reason and courageously uphold every unbelievable absurdity. Remember that reproduction is the primary <note>original: palmary</note> active power in nature, politics, and society alike. It is a mania for mortals to desire to be immortal, to seek to know the future without understanding the present, and to strive to be spiritual while everything surrounding them is material.

After this speech <note>original: harangue</note>, the orator knelt devoutly <note>original: genuflected</note> before the deity of the temple and retired. At the same moment, a man of gigantic stature led the countess to the feet of the immortal Count de Saint-Germain <note>original spelling: Saint-German</note>, who spoke as follows:

"Chosen from my earliest youth for greatness, I dedicated myself to discovering the nature of true glory. Politics appeared to me as nothing but the science of deception. Military tactics seemed the art of assassination, and philosophy the ambitious foolishness of complete irrationality. Physics appeared as beautiful fantasies about Nature and the constant mistakes of people suddenly dropped into a country that is utterly unknown to them. Theology was the science of the misery that results from human pride, and history was the gloomy spectacle of unending betrayal and blundering. 

From this, I concluded that the statesman was a skilled liar, the hero an illustrious idiot, the philosopher an eccentric creature, the physician a pitiable and blind man, the theologian a fanatical teacher <note>original: pedagogue</note>, and the historian a mere dealer in words <note>original: word-monger</note>. Then I heard of the deity of this temple. I cast my cares upon him, along with my uncertainties <note>original: incertitudes</note> and aspirations. When he took possession of my soul, he allowed me to perceive all objects in a new light; I began to read the future <note>original: futurity</note>. This universe—previously so limited, so narrow, and so barren—was now expanded. I lived not only with those who exist now, but with those who lived in the past. He united me..."

I found it eminently delightful to know everything without studying anything, to use the treasures of the earth without having to beg favors from monarchs, and to rule the elements rather than men. Heaven made me generous; I have enough to satisfy my tastes; all that surrounds me is rich, loving, and predestined.”

When the service was finished, they changed back into their everyday clothes. A magnificent feast <note>original: repast</note> concluded the ceremony. During the course of the banquet, the two guests were informed that the <term>Elixir of Immortality</term> <note>A legendary substance believed to grant eternal life or youth.</note> was merely Tokay <note>A famous, expensive wine from the Tokaj region of Hungary.</note> colored green or red depending on the needs of the situation. Several essential rules <note>original: precepts</note> were commanded of them; among others, that they must hate, avoid, and slander <note>original: calumniate</note> men of intelligence, but flatter, encourage, and deceive <note>original: blind</note> fools. They were told to spread the news with great mystery that the Count de Saint-Germain was five hundred years old, and that they must make gold—but above all, make victims to swindle.

The truth of this strange episode is not confirmed by any serious biographer. If it occurred as narrated, it certainly served to strengthen Cagliostro in his ambitious plans. The change which had taken place in the adventurer since his second visit to England is well described by Figuier <note>Louis Figuier (1819–1894), a French writer who documented the history of alchemy and pseudoscience.</note>.

> “His language, his appearance <note>original: mien</note>, and his manners are all transformed. His conversation turns only on his travels in Egypt, to Mecca, and in other remote places; on the sciences into which he was initiated at the foot of the Pyramids; and on the secrets <note>original: arcana</note> of Nature which his own cleverness has discovered. At the same time, he talks little, more often wrapping himself in mysterious silence. When questioned with repeated pleas, he only condescends <note>original: deigns</note> at the most to draw his symbol—a serpent with an apple in its mouth and pierced by a dart, meaning that human wisdom should be...”

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Joseph Balsamo (Cagliostro) and his wife Lorenza, following their alleged initiation into the mysteries of the Count de Saint-Germain.</meta>

"...silent on the mysteries which it has unraveled. . . . Lorenza was transformed at the same time as her husband. Her ambitions and behavior became worthy of the new projects of Cagliostro. She aimed, like himself, at the glory of massive successes."

The initiates of the Count de Saint-Germain moved into Courland <note>A historical region located in modern-day Latvia.</note>, where they established Masonic lodges according to the sublime rite of Egyptian Freemasonry. The countess was an excellent preacher for captivating hearts and enchanting imaginations; her beauty fascinated a large number of the nobility of Courland. At Mittau <note>Now Jelgava, the former capital of the Duchy of Courland.</note>, Cagliostro attracted the attention of persons of high rank, who were led by his reputation to regard him as an extraordinary person. By means of his Freemasonry, he began to obtain an ascendancy over the minds of the nobles. Some of them, discontented with the reigning duke, are actually said to have offered him the sovereignty of the country, viewing him as a divine man and a messenger from above. The Italian biography represents him plotting with this end in view. “He claims,” say the documents of the Holy Inquisition, “that he had virtue enough to resist the temptation, and that he refused the offered gift out of the respect due to sovereigns. His wife has assured us that his refusal was caused by the reflection that his deceptions would soon be discovered.” He collected, however, a prodigious number of presents in gold, silver, and money, and traveled to St. Petersburg, provided with regular passports. But the prophet soon found that a sufficiently impressive reputation had not preceded him, and he, therefore, simply announced himself as a physician and chemist, soon attracting attention through his private life and air of mystery.

His adoption of the *role* of physician leads to a brief consideration of the miraculous cures which have been attributed to him. They are generally referred to a broad

application of the principles and methods of Mesmer, his contemporary. They were performed without passes, iron rods, or any of the cumbersome equipment of his rival in the healing art; he relied simply on the laying on of hands. Furthermore, he did not exploit his patients but instead distributed his wealth—which now appeared unlimited—among the poor, who flocked to him in great numbers as his reputation grew. The source of this wealth is not accurately known, but it is thought to have been derived from the Masonic initiates, for whom he served as an apostle and propagandist.

Many of the miraculous cures which Cagliostro performed in Germany became widely known, and in Russia he was soon surrounded by the curious. Lorenza played her own part admirably; she answered discreetly and naturally, making the most outrageous statements with apparent complete innocence. The physician-chemist, in addition to his healing powers, had a reputation as an alchemist and an expert in the arcane sciences. The supposed miraculous restoration to health of the infant child of an illustrious nobleman raised him to the height of fame, and his extravagant claims, strongly supported by the naive beauty of his wife, were beginning to be taken seriously. However, the combined result of an affair between Lorenza and Prince Potemkin <note>Spelled "Poternki" in the original text; refers to Prince Grigory Potemkin, the Prime Minister and favorite of Catherine the Great.</note>, favorite of the Empress Catherine, and the discovery that the nobleman's child had apparently been swapped, caused them to depart hastily with great riches toward the German border.

They stayed at Warsaw for a time, and there the Italian biographer tells us that Cagliostro used all his tricks to deceive a prince to whom he was introduced, and who was exceedingly anxious to obtain, with the help of the supposed magician, the permanent com-

...command of a devil. Cagliostro inflated his ego for a long time with the expectation of fulfilling this absurd ambition, and actually obtained gifts from him amounting to several thousand crowns. The prince finally realized that there was no hope of keeping one of the hellish spirits in his service and wished instead to win the earthly affections of the countess; however, in this too he was disappointed, as the lady positively refused to comply with his desires. Finding himself thwarted in both attempts, he gave up every feeling except revenge and frightened our adventurer and his wife so much with his threats that they were forced to return his gifts.

The truthfulness of this account is not, however, beyond suspicion; other biographers describe Cagliostro proceeding directly to Frankfurt and then to Strasbourg, where, more wealthy and successful than ever, he made a grand entry. The distinguished visitor—the Rosicrucian, the alchemist, the physician, the sublime count—had been expected since early morning by the citizens of the old town. The following extraordinary account in the *Dictionnaire des Sciences Occultes* has been given by an anonymous biographer:

> “On September 19, 1780, in a tavern just outside Strasbourg, surrounded by a group of humble drinkers who stared from the small window at the vast crowd gathered below them, the face of a bald and wrinkled man about eighty years of age, and evidently of southern origin, might have been noticed; this was the goldsmith Marano. Repeated failures and debts which he did not choose to pay had forced him to leave Palermo, and he had set himself up in his old trade at Strasbourg. Like the rest of the townspeople, he had come out to see the remarkable personage

...whose arrival was expected, and who caused a greater sensation than many a powerful monarch. He had come by way of Germany from Warsaw, where, according to popular rumor, he had amassed immense riches by the transmutation of base metals into gold; for he possessed the secret of the philosopher's stone and all the incalculable talents of an alchemist.”

“By my faith,” said a hatter, “I am indeed happy, since I am destined to behold this illustrious mortal—if, indeed, he is a mortal.”

“It is asserted,” added a druggist, “that he is a son of the Princess of Trebizond, and that he has, moreover, the fine eyes of his mother.”

“Also that he is a lineal descendant of Charles Martel,” said a town clerk.

“He dates back even further,” added a rope-maker, “for he took part in the wedding feast at Cana.”

“Then beyond doubt, he is the wandering Jew!” exclaimed Marano.

“Better yet, some credible persons assert that he was born before the Great Flood.”

“What audacity! Yet, suppose he is the devil.”

These notions, reproduced here with fidelity and adorned by the most extravagant commentaries, were actually in general circulation among the crowd at that period. Some regarded the mysterious Count Cagliostro as an inspired saint, a performer of miracles, and a phenomenal personage outside the order of Nature. The cures attributed to him were both innumerable and unexplainable. Others regarded him merely as a clever charlatan. Cagliostro himself boldly asserted that all his wonders were performed under the special favor and help of heaven. He added that the Supreme Being had deigned to grant him the beatific vision, and that it was his

While many view him as a divine miracle worker, others suspect he is a charlatan, though he maintains his powers come from God.</summary>

mission to convert unbelievers and restore Catholicism; but despite this exalted vocation, he told fortunes, taught the art of winning at lotteries, interpreted dreams, and held séances of transcendental phantasmagoria.

"But," argued the ropemaker with much excitement, "a man who speaks with angels is never the devil."

"Is he in communication with angels?" cried Marano, struck by the news. "In that case, I must see him at all costs. How old is he?"

"Bah!" said the druggist, "as if such a being could have an age! He looks about thirty-six."

"Oh!" muttered the goldsmith. "What if he were my rascal? My rascal should be thirty-seven by now."

As the elderly Sicilian pondered his miserable past, he was startled by a commotion of voices. The heavenly being had arrived, and he soon passed by on the road, surrounded by a large procession of couriers, footmen, valets, and others, all in magnificent uniforms. By his side, in the open carriage, sat Lorenza or Seraphina Feliciani, his wife, who supported her husband’s schemes with all her ability. Reasonable people regarded him as a wandering member and agent of the Masonic Templars, his wealth guaranteed by contributions from the order's various lodges.

A great shout went up when Count Cagliostro passed the inn. Marano recognized his man and, rushing out, managed to stop the carriage, shouting as he did so—"Joseph Balsamo! It is Joseph! Scoundrel, where are my sixty ounces of gold?"

Cagliostro barely deigned to glance at the furious goldsmith; but in the middle of the deep silence that the incident caused among the crowd, a voice—apparently from the clouds—spoke these words very clearly: "Remove this lunatic, who is possessed by hellish spirits!"

Some of the spectators fell on their knees, others seized the unfortunate goldsmith, and the brilliant cortege passed on.

Entering Strasbourg in triumph, Cagliostro paused in front of a large hall, where the equerries who had preceded him had already collected a considerable crowd of the sick. The famous <term>empiric</term> entered and cured them all, some simply by touch, others apparently by words or by a gift of money, and the rest by his universal <term>panacea</term>; but the historian who records these things asserts that the sick persons thus variously treated had been carefully selected, the physician preferring to treat the more serious cases at the patients' homes.

Cagliostro emerged from the hall amidst universal acclamations and was accompanied by the immense crowd to the doors of the magnificent lodging which had been prepared for his arrival. The elite of Strasbourg society was invited to a sumptuous meal, which was followed by a séance of transcendental magnetism, where he produced some extraordinary manifestations through the mediation of clairvoyant children of either sex, whom he called his "doves" or pupils. The unspotted virginity and innocence of these children were an indispensable condition of success. They were chosen by himself and received a mystical consecration at his hands. Then, over a crystal vessel filled with water, he pronounced the magical formulas for the evocation of angelic intelligences as they are written in the celestial rituals. Heavenly spirits became visible in the depths of the water and responded to questions, occasionally in an intelligible voice, but more often in characters which appeared on the surface of the water and were visible to the pupils alone, who interpreted them to the public.

Contemporary testimony establishes that these manifestations, as a whole, were genuine, and there is little doubt

...of the mesmeric abilities of Cagliostro, who had probably become acquainted in the East with the phenomena of *virginal lucidity*, especially in boys, and had supplemented the oriental methods with the discoveries of Puységur, which were at that time quite well known.

For three years Cagliostro remained at Strasbourg and was celebrated continually. Here he obtained complete influence over the mind of the famous cardinal-archbishop, the Prince de Rohan. His first priority, upon taking up residence in the town, was to prove his respect for the clergy through his generosity and zeal. He visited the sick in the hospitals, respectfully participated in the duties of the regular doctors, proposed his new remedies with caution, and did not condemn the old methods, but sought to unite new science with the science based on experience. He gained the reputation of a bold experimenter in chemistry, a wise physician, and a truly enlightened innovator. The inhabitants of the crowded neighborhoods regarded him as a man sent from God, performing miraculous cures and distributing riches from an inexhaustible source known only to him. Unheard-of cures were cited, as well as alchemical operations that surpassed even the supposed possibilities of the <term>transmutatory art</term>.

Anything that savored of the marvelous was an attraction for the cardinal-archbishop, and he longed to see Cagliostro. An anonymous writer states that he unsuccessfully sought an interview with him again and again; for the cardinal-prince of trickery sensed the character of the prince-cardinal even from a distance and wrapped himself in a reserve which, to the imagination of his dupe, was like a <term>lodestone</term> to a magnet. Others represent him, however, courting the favor of the great ecclesiastic's secretary, and so obtaining an introduction. At the first

The sum of twenty thousand francs was actually paid to the adept to accomplish this operation.

Doubtless during his stay at Strasbourg he propagated with zeal the mysteries of his <term>Egyptian Freemasonry</term>, and at length, laden with spoils, he went to Bordeaux, where he continued his healing in public, and then proceeded to Lyons, where for the space of three months he occupied himself with the foundation of a mother-lodge, and, according to the Italian biographer, here as elsewhere, in less creditable pursuits. At length he arrived at Paris, where, says the same authority, he soon became the object of general conversation, regard, and esteem. His curative powers were now but little exercised, for Paris abounded with <term>mesmerists</term> and healers, and the wonders of simple <term>magnetism</term> were stale and unprofitable in consequence. He now assumed the *role* of a practical magician, and astonished the city by the summoning of <term>phantoms</term>, which he caused to appear, at the wish of the inquirer, either in a mirror or in a vase of clear water. These phantoms equally represented dead and living beings, and as occasionally collusion appears to have been nearly impossible, and as the theory of coincidence is preposterous, there is reason to suppose that he produced results

All of Paris, at any rate, was left wondering at his enchantments and wonders, and it is seriously stated that Louis XVI was so infatuated with *the divine Cagliostro* that he declared anyone who injured him should be considered guilty of treason. At Versailles, and in the presence of several distinguished nobles, he is said to have caused appearances in mirrors, vases, etc., not merely of the specters of absent or deceased people, but living and moving beings of a ghostly nature, including many dead men and women selected by the astonished spectators.

The mystery which surrounded him abroad was deepened even when he received visitors at home. He lived in the Rue Saint Claude, in an isolated house surrounded by gardens and sheltered from the unwanted curiosity of neighbors. There he established his laboratory, which no one might enter. He received guests in a vast and lavish apartment on the first floor. Lorenza lived a retired life, only being visible at certain hours before a select company, and in a transparent and glamorous costume. The report of her beauty spread through the city; she was considered a model of perfection, and duels were fought on her account. Cagliostro was now no longer young, and Lorenza was in the prime of her beauty. He is said, for the first time, to have experienced the pangs of jealousy on account of a certain Chevalier d’Oisemont, with whom she had several secret meetings. Private frustrations did not, however, interfere with professional <term>thaumaturgy</term>, and the summoning of the famous dead was a common occurrence at certain magical suppers which became celebrated throughout all Paris. These were undoubtedly exaggerated by rumor, but as they all occurred within the questionable surroundings of his own house of mystery, they were in all probability fraudulent, for it must be distinctly remembered that in

...his true character was that of an unparalleled trickster, and that the genuine phenomena he occasionally produced were simply supplements to his charlatanry, rather than his deceptions being aids to normally genuine phenomena.

On one occasion, according to the *Mémoires authentiques pour servir à l'histoire du Comte de Cagliostro*, the distinguished <term>thaumaturgist</term> announced that at a private supper given to six guests, he would summon the spirits of any dead persons whom they named to him, and that the phantoms, appearing substantial, would seat themselves at the banquet. The meal took place with the knowledge and, it may be supposed, with the complicity of Lorenza. At midnight the guests were assembled; a round table, set for twelve, was laid with unheard-of luxury in a dining room where everything was in harmony with the approaching <term>Kabbalistic</term> ritual. The six guests, along with Cagliostro, took their seats, and thus the ominous number of thirteen was designed to be present at the table.

The supper was served, and the servants were dismissed with threats of immediate death if they dared to open the doors before they were summoned. Each guest named the deceased person whom he desired to see. Cagliostro took the names, placed them in the pocket of his gold-embroidered vest, and announced that with no further preparation than a simple invocation on his part, the summoned spirits would appear in flesh and blood; for, according to Egyptian dogma, there were in reality no dead. These guests from the other world, requested and awaited with trembling anxiety, were the Duke de Choiseul, Voltaire, d'Alembert, Diderot, the Abbé de Voisenon, and Montesquieu. Their names were pronounced slowly in a loud voice and with all the concentrated determination of the adept's will; and after a moment of intolerable doubt, the summoned guests appeared very unobtrusively and took their seats with the quiet courtesy which had characterized them in life.

The first question put to them, once the awe of their presence had somewhat worn off, was about their situation in the world beyond.

"There is no world beyond," replied d'Alembert. "Death is simply the end of the evils that have tortured us. No pleasure is experienced, but, on the other hand, there is no suffering. I have not met Mademoiselle Lespinasse, and I have not seen Lorignet. There is also a marked sincerity there. Some deceased persons who have recently joined us inform me that I am almost forgotten. I am, however, consoled. Men are unworthy of the trouble we take for them. I never loved them; now I despise them."

"What has become of your learning?" said M. de —— to Diderot.

"I was not as learned as people commonly supposed. My ready wit adapted everything I read, and in my writing I borrowed from every side. From this comes the disjointed character of my books, which will be forgotten in half a century. The Encyclopedia, for the merit of which I am honored, does not belong to me. The duty of an editor is simply to organize the selection of subjects. The man who showed the most talent in the whole work was the compiler of its index, yet no one has dreamed of recognizing his merits."

"I praised the project," said Voltaire, "for it seemed well-suited to advance my philosophical opinions. Speaking of philosophy, I am not at all certain that I was right. I have learned strange things since my death and have conversed with half a dozen Popes. Clement XIV and Benedict, above all, are men of infinite intelligence and good sense."

"What most annoys me," said the Duc de Choiseul, "is the absence of gender where we dwell. Whatever may be

"What is truly a pleasure to me," said the Abbé Voisenon, "is that among us one is perfectly cured of the folly of intelligence. You cannot imagine how I have been teased about my ridiculous little romances. I had almost confessed that I appreciated these trivialities at their true value, but whether the modesty of an academician is disbelieved, or whether such frivolity is out of character with my age and profession, I pay the penalty almost daily for the mistakes of my mortal existence."

Amid these marvels, Cagliostro proceeded with the dearest of all his projects, namely, the spread of his Egyptian Masonic rite,* into which ladies were subsequently admitted, a course of magic being opened for the purpose by Madame Cagliostro. The candidates admitted to this course were thirty-six in number, and all males were excluded. Thus Lorenza figured as the Grand Mistress of Egyptian Masonry, just as her husband was himself the grand and sublime Copt. The fair neophytes were required to contribute each of them the sum of one hundred louis to

<note>* "It was his ambition to establish a mother-lodge in Paris, to which the rest should be entirely subordinate. He proclaimed himself as the bearer of the mysteries of Isis and Anubis from the far East. Although he threatened common masonry with a radical reform, his innovations triumphed over all obstacles. He obtained numerous and distinguished followers, who on one occasion assembled in great force to hear Joseph Balsamo explain to them the doctrines of Egyptian Freemasonry. At this solemn convention he is said to have spoken with overpowering eloquence, and such was his remarkable success that his listeners departed in amazement and completely converted to his regenerated and purified masonry. None of them doubted that he was an initiate of the secrets of Nature, as preserved in the temple of Apis at the time when Cambyses struck that temperamental divinity. From this moment the initiations into the new masonry were numerous, although they were limited to the aristocracy of society. There are reasons to believe that the grandees who were deemed worthy of admission paid exceedingly high prices for the honor."—*Figuier, History of the Marvelous, vol. iv. pp. 23, 24.*</note>

A vast mansion was hired in the Rue Verte, Faubourg Saint Honoré, which at that period was a lonely part of the city. The building was surrounded with gardens and magnificent trees. The séance for initiation took place shortly before midnight on the 7th of August 1785.

On entering the first apartment, says Figuier, the ladies were obliged to undress and put on a white garment with a belt of various colors. They were divided into six groups, distinguished by the tint of their sashes. A large veil was also provided, and they were made to enter a temple lit from the roof and furnished with thirty-six armchairs covered with black satin. Lorenza, dressed in white, was seated on a type of throne supported by two tall figures, dressed in such a way that their sex could not be determined. The light was lowered by degrees until surrounding objects could scarcely be seen, when the Grand Mistress commanded the ladies to uncover their left legs as far as the thigh and to rest their right arms on a nearby pillar. Two young women then entered holding swords, and with silk ropes they bound all the ladies together by the arms and legs. Then, after a period of impressive silence, Lorenza delivered a speech—which is given at length, though on doubtful authority, by several biographers—and which fervidly preached the emancipation of women from the shameful bonds imposed on them by the masters of creation.

These bonds were symbolized by the silk ropes from which the beautiful initiates were released at the end of the long speech, when they were conducted into separate apartments, each opening onto the garden, where they had the most extraordinary experiences. Some were pursued by men who unmercifully harassed them with cruel solicita-

When they had breathed a little after their trials, the vaulted roof opened suddenly, and, on a vast sphere of gold, there descended a man, naked as Adam before the fall, holding a serpent in his hand, and having a burning star upon his head.

The Grand Mistress announced that this was the genius of Truth, the immortal, the divine Cagliostro, born without procreation from the bosom of our father Abraham, and the guardian of all that has been, is, or shall be known on the entire earth. He was there to initiate them into the secrets of which they had been dishonestly deprived. The Grand Copt then commanded them to cast off their profane clothing, for if they would receive truth, they must be as naked as truth itself. The sovereign priestess, setting the example, unbound her sash and permitted her drapery to fall to the ground, and the beautiful initiates, following her example, exposed themselves in all the nudity of their charms to the magnetic glances of the heavenly genius, who then began his revelations.

They had shown themselves worthy to know the truth; he undertook to instruct them by degrees in it. It was enough at the outset to inform them that the sublime goal of that Egyptian Freemasonry, which he had brought from the very heart of the Orient, was the happiness of mankind. Thereupon the Genius of Truth seated himself again upon the sphere of gold and was carried away through the roof. At the same time, the floor opened, the light flared up, and a table splendidly adorned and luxuriously spread rose up from the ground. The ladies were joined by their lovers *in person* <note>The original text uses the Latin phrase *in propria persona*.</note>; the supper was followed by dancing and various diversions until three o'clock in the morning.

About this time, Count Cagliostro was reluctantly forced to yield to the continual requests of the poor and resume his medical role. In a short time, he was raised to the height of celebrity by a miraculous cure of the Prince de Soubise, the brother of the Cardinal de Rohan, who was suffering from a severe attack of scarlet fever. From this moment, the portrait of the adept could be seen everywhere in Paris.

The count was far away from Paris at the time, and the adventuress promised to keep the secret on the condition that Lorenza should, in turn, do all in her power to establish her as an intimate friend in the house, with free entrance there, and should persuade Cagliostro to place his knowledge and skill at her disposal if she ever required it. This arrangement resulted in the complicity of Cagliostro in the extraordinary and scandalous affair of the Diamond Necklace. When the plot was exposed, Cagliostro was arrested along with the other alleged conspirators, including the principal victim, the Cardinal de Rohan. He was exonerated, and indeed not without honor, from the charge of which he was undoubtedly guilty; however, his wife had fled to Rome at the time of his arrest and had rejoined her family. He himself began to tremble at his own notoriety and grew anxious to leave France. He postponed his grand project concerning the metropolitan lodge of the Egyptian rite until a more favorable period.* A personage calling himself Thomas Ximenes, who claimed descent from the cardinal of that name, sought to revive his former Masonic enthusiasm; but the vision of the Bastille seemed to be constantly before his eyes, and neither this person nor the great dignitaries of the Parisian lodges could persuade him. In spite of his acquittal, he nursed a desire for vengeance against the Court of France, and more than once he confided to his private friends that he would make his voice heard once he had crossed the border. -> ### LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS. <-

His discouraged followers learned that he was on the road to England.

Once in London, he recovered his energy. He was received with great honor; many of his disciples from Lyons and Paris followed him there. The English masons invited him to the metropolitan lodge and gave him the highest position, that of Grand Orient. He was urged to organize a masonic lodge of the Egyptian Rite and consented with some sadness, for the memory of the brilliant Paris lodge—which he had been on the verge of founding—was constantly on his mind. He could not comfort himself over the failure of that beautiful and long-cherished plan, which had cost him so much study, effort, and preaching.

It was from this safe distance that Cagliostro addressed his famous *Letter to the People of France*, which was translated into many languages and circulated widely throughout Europe. It predicted the French Revolution, the demolition of the Bastille, and the rise of a great prince who would abolish the infamous *lettres de cachet* <note>original: "lettres de cachet"; these were private warrants signed by the French King that allowed for the imprisonment of individuals without a trial or the right of appeal</note>, summon the States-General <note>The legislative assembly of the different classes of French subjects</note>, and re-establish the true religion.

The publication was extreme in its language and revolutionary in its sentiments. Shortly after its release, his well-known quarrel with the *Courrier de l’Europe* <note>original: "Courrier de l’Europe"; a French-language newspaper published in London that played a major role in exposing Cagliostro's past</note> began, which resulted in the exposure of Cagliostro's true life story from beginning to end.

Fearing the rage of his countless victims and extreme legal actions from his creditors, he hurried to leave London. He landed in Holland, crossed Germany, and took refuge in Basel, where the traditional hospitality of the Swiss cantons somewhat reassured the exposed occultist. From the moment of this exposure, however, Cagliostro's downfall was incredibly rapid. Nevertheless, he was still followed by some of his initiates, who...

urged him to return to France, assuring him of the powerful protection of high-ranking masonic dignitaries. In his hesitation, he wrote to the Baron de Breteuil, the king's minister of the household, but, as it happened, a personal enemy of the Cardinal de Rohan. Considering Cagliostro as a *protégé* of the prince, he replied that if he had sufficient audacity to set foot within the borders of the kingdom, he would be arrested and transferred to a prison in Paris, there to await prosecution as a common swindler who would answer to royal justice for his criminal life.

From this moment Cagliostro saw that he was a permanent exile from France, and feeling not at all sure of his safety even in Switzerland, he left Basel for Aix, in Savoy. He was ordered to quit that town within forty-eight hours. At Roveredo, a territory of Austria, the same treatment awaited him. He migrated to Trent and announced himself as a practitioner of lawful medicine, but the prince-bishop who was sovereign of the country recognized the signs of the sorcerer beneath the doctor's somber dress, and in a short amount of time showed his hostility toward magical practices. The wandering hierophant <note>A high priest or person who interprets sacred mysteries</note> of Egyptian masonry, somewhat hard-pressed, traveled by post to Rome and reached the Eternal City after many hardships. Here, according to Saint-Félix and Figuier, he was rejoined by his wife; according to his Italian biographer, Lorenza had accompanied him in his wanderings and persuaded him to seek refuge in Rome, being weary to death of her miserable way of life. The former statement is, on the whole, the most probable, as it is difficult to suppose that she left Italy to rejoin Cagliostro at Passy, and she appears to have returned to him with marked reluctance. She endeavored to lead him back to religion, which had never been uprooted from her heart. He lived for some time with extraordinary circumspection...

The Holy Inquisition, which doubtless had scrutinized all his movements, is said to have been deceived for a time, and he was favorably received by several cardinals. He lived for a year in perfect liberty, occupied with the private study of medicine. During this time he endeavored to obtain loans from the initiates of his Egyptian rite who were scattered over France and Germany, but they did not arrive, and the sublime Copt, the illuminated proprietor of the philosophical stone and the medicine called metallic, came once more—to the eternal disgrace of Osiris, Isis, and Anubis—to the very verge of poverty.

His desperation prompted him to renew his relations with the masonic societies within the area of the Papal States. A penalty of death hung over the initiates of the superior grades, and their lodges were, in consequence, surrounded with great mystery and were convened in subterranean places. He was persuaded to found a lodge of Egyptian Freemasonry in Rome itself, from which moment Lorenza reasonably regarded him as lost. One of his own adepts betrayed him; he was arrested on the 27th of September 1789, by order of the Holy Office, and imprisoned in the Castle of St Angelo. An inventory of his papers was taken, and all his effects were sealed up. The legal proceedings against him were drawn up with the most precise inquisitorial care during the long period of eighteen months. When the trial began, he was defended by Count Gaetano Bernardini, advocate of the accused before the sacred and august tribunal; and to this regular pleader, the impartial and benign office, of its free grace and pleasure, generously added as counsel one Monsignor Louis Constantini, “whose knowledge and probity,” says an unbought and unbuyable witness (inquisitorially inspired),

"were generally recognized." They did not hide from him the seriousness of his position, and advised him to avoid basing his defense on a series of denials, promising to save him from the death penalty. Thus, he was persuaded to confess everything and was again reconciled to the church; and being almost fragrant with genuine holiness, on March 21, 1791, he was brought before the general assembly of those who purge souls by fire. On the 7th of the following April, he was brought before the Pope, when the lawyers pleaded with such eloquence that they retired in the agonies of breathless exhaustion. Cagliostro repeated his confession, and as a natural result of the unpaid eloquence and the bought confession, the penalty of death was pronounced.

When, however, the exhausted energies of the lawyers were somewhat restored, a recommendation of mercy was addressed to the Pope. The sentence was changed to life imprisonment, and the condemned man was sent to the Castle of St. Angelo. After an imprisonment of two years, he died—God knows how—still in the prime of life, at the age of fifty.

Lorenza, whose testimony had contributed significantly to her husband’s conviction, was sentenced to life in a penitentiary. Cagliostro’s papers were burned by the Holy Office, and the shadow of that institution keeps the secret of the exact date of its victim’s death to this day. It carefully spread the report that on one occasion he attempted to strangle a priest whom he had sent for under the pretense of making a confession, hoping to escape in his clothes; it then publicly stated that he had afterward strangled himself. # LIVES OF ALCHEMISTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

They were determined to release the entombed adept <note>Cagliostro, who was imprisoned by the Church</note>, but they were informed that Cagliostro was dead. “At this intelligence,” says Figuier <note>Louis Figuier (1819–1894), a French scientist and writer</note>, “they perceived plainly that the former *Parliament of France* was not to be compared with the Roman Inquisition; and without regretting the demolished Bastille, they could not but acknowledge that it disgorged its prey more easily than the Castle of Saint Angelo.”

The personal attractions of Cagliostro appear to have been exaggerated by some of his biographers. “His splendid stature and high bearing, increased by a dress of the most bizarre magnificence, and the extensive suite which invariably accompanied him in his wanderings, turned all eyes upon him and disposed the minds of the common people toward an almost idolatrous admiration.”

With this opinion of Figuier may be compared the counter-statement of the Italian biographer: “He was of a brown complexion, a bloated countenance, and a severe aspect; he was destitute of any of those graces so common in the world of gallantry, without knowledge and without abilities.” But the Italian biographer was a false witness, for Cagliostro was beyond all question and controversy a man of consummate ability, tact, and talent. The truth would appear to lie between these opposite extremes. “The Count de Cagliostro,” says the English biography published in 1787, “is below the middle stature, inclined to corpulency; his face is a round oval, his complexion and eyes dark, the latter uncommonly penetrating. In his address, we are not sensible of that indescribable grace which engages the affections before we consult the understanding. On the contrary, there is in his manner a self-importance which at first sight rather disgusts than allures, and obliges us to withhold our regards until, on a more intimate acquaintance, we yield it as a tribute to our reason.”

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Cagliostro's character, contrasting favorable biographical accounts of his eloquence with the negative testimony provided by his wife, Lorenza, to the Inquisition.</meta>

# TRAVELS, ETC., OF JOSEPH BALSAMO.

Though naturally studious and contemplative, his conversation is lively, filled with wise remarks and pleasant anecdotes; yet, with an understanding that is exceptionally clear and broad, he is constantly made a fool of by sycophants and flatterers.”

The persuasive and occasionally overpowering eloquence of Cagliostro is also emphasized by most of his biographers; however, according to the testimony of his wife—extracted under the terror of the Inquisition and cited in the Italian biography:—

> “His speech, instead of being eloquent, was composed in a style of the most tiresome confusion and was full of the most incoherent ideas. Before ascending the speaker's platform, he was always careful to prepare himself for his work with several bottles of wine, and he was so ignorant of the subject on which he was about to speak that he generally asked his wife for the text on which he was to preach to his disciples. If to these circumstances we add a Sicilian dialect, mixed with a jargon of French and Italian, we cannot hesitate for a single moment regarding the degree of credibility we should give to the claims made about the miraculous effects of his eloquence.”

But the Inquisition possessed documents that bore irrefutable testimony to the extraordinary influence Cagliostro exerted over the minds of his numerous followers, and it is absurd to suppose such influence could be held by a man who was ignorant, unpresentable, and poorly spoken. Moreover, the testimony of Lorenza—given under circumstances of, at the very least, the strongest moral intimidation—is completely worthless on all points whatsoever, and the biased views of our inquisitorial apologists are of no significant value.

I have given an almost disproportionate amount of space to the

Despite the veil of mystery that surrounded Altotas, there is no reason to suppose that he was an adept in anything but the <term>sophistication</term> <note>The adulteration or debasement of metals to make them appear more valuable.</note> of metals, and his skill in alchemical trickery was passed down to his pupil. That Balsamo was a powerful mesmerist, that he could easily induce clairvoyance in suitable subjects, that he had dabbled in Arabic occultism, and that he had the faculty of healing magnetically, are points which the evidence allows us to acknowledge. These genuine phenomena supported his massive impostures, being themselves supplemented by direct and prepared fraud wherever they were weak or lacking. Thus, his miraculous prophecies, descriptions of absent persons, revelations of private matters, etc., may be accounted for to some extent by the insatiable curiosity and diligence he used to obtain knowledge of the secrets of any families with which he communicated. Lorenza <note>The OCR transcription reads "Lorenzo," but the context and previous pages clarify this refers to his wife, Lorenza Feliciani.</note> declared under oath during her examination that many of the pupils had been prepared beforehand by her husband, but that some had been brought to him unaware, and that regarding them she could only suppose he had been assisted by the marvels of magical art.

His powers, whatever they were, were passed on to at least some of his Masonic initiates, as can be seen in a genuine letter addressed to him from Lyons, which describes in enthusiastic language the consecration in that town of the Egyptian lodge called Wisdom Triumphant. This letter fell into the hands of the Inquisition. ## CONCLUSION.

It has now been made clear beyond all reasonable doubt, by the certain and abundant evidence of the lives and labors of the alchemists, that they were in search of a physical process for the transmutation of the so-called baser metals into silver and gold. The methods and processes by which they endeavored to attain this *desired goal*, and the secrets which they are supposed to have discovered, are embodied in allegorical writings; their curious symbolism, in the hands of ingenious interpreters, is capable of several explanations, but the facts in their difficult and generally checkered careers are not allegorical, and are not capable of any mystical interpretations. Consequently, the attempt to enthrone them upon the loftiest pinnacles of achievement in the psychic world—however attractive and dazzling to a romantic imagination, and however spiritually suggestive—must be regretfully abandoned. Their less magnificent but substantial and permanent reputation is based on their physical discoveries and

<meta>This page continues the conclusion of the work, moving from the physical achievements of the alchemists to the philosophical and spiritual implications of their "Universal Development" theory.</meta>

on their persistent enunciation of a theory of Universal Development. True and far-sighted adepts clearly perceived that this theory applied as much to the triune nature of man as it did to those metals which, in their conception, also had a triune nature.

As stated in the Introduction to this work, I have little personal doubt—after a careful and unbiased evaluation of all the evidence—that the *Magnum Opus* has been performed, at least occasionally, in the past. Therefore, while the alchemists were laying the foundations of modern chemistry, they had already surpassed its highest results in the metallic kingdom. Now, the Hermetic doctrine of correspondences—which is, at any rate, entitled to the sincere respect of all esoteric thinkers—teaches us that the fact of their success with physical subjects is, by analogy, a substantial guarantee of the successful outcome of parallel methods when applied in the psychic world with man as the subject. But the revelations of mesmerism and the phenomena called spiritualism have discovered thaumaturgic <note>miracle-working</note> possibilities for humanity; these, in a completely independent manner, contribute to the verification of the alchemical hypothesis of development in its extension to the plane of intelligence. I believe these possibilities can be realized exclusively along the lines indicated in Hermetic parables. I am not prepared to explain how the alchemical theory of Universal Development came to be evolved in the scientific and psychological twilight of the Middle Ages, but the fact remains. Nor am I prepared to explain how and why the method of a discredited science—which is not commonly supposed to have achieved its goal—should not only be consistent within its own sphere but should also have a vast field of application outside of it; yet, again, the fact remains. I have brought a wide acquaintance with the history of modern supernaturalism to bear

# CONCLUSION.

I have engaged in the serious study of alchemy and have found that the old theories are illustrated by new facts, while these new facts coincide with the old theories. As all of this has occurred—in the words of the alchemists—"by a natural process, devoid of haste or violence," I trust that it is no illusory discovery. I hope that its future publication may give a new impulse to the study of the <term>Hermetic writings</term> <note>Works attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> among the <term>occultists</term> <note>Those who study hidden or supernatural arts and sciences.</note> of England and America.

<summary>The author concludes by asserting that modern evidence supports ancient alchemical theories and expresses hope that these findings will revitalize the study of Hermetic philosophy in the English-speaking world.</summary>

**The Ruin of the Alchemists**. 16mo. Paris, 1612. <note>original: La Ruine des Alchimistes</note>

**Amelung, Dr. Peter—A Noble Treatise, in which the invention, necessity, and utility of Alchemy are discussed**. 8vo. Leipzig, 1607. <note>original: Amelungii (D. Petri)—Tractatus Nobilis, in quo de Alchimiæ Inventione, necessitate et utilitate agitur</note>

**Angelique, Le Sieur d’—The True Philosophical Stone of Medicine**. 12mo. Paris, 1622. <note>original: La Vraye Pierre Philosophale de Médecine</note>
**The Silent Book of High Value, in which the whole of Hermetic Philosophy is depicted in hieroglyphic figures**. Folio. La Rochelle, 1677. <note>original: Altus Mutus Liber, in quo tota Philosophia Hermetica figuris Hieroglyphicis depingitur</note>
**Hermetic Apocalypses**. 4to. Gdansk, 1683. <note>original: Apocalypses Hermeticorum</note>
**Apology for the Great Work, or the Elixir of the Philosophers**. 12mo. Paris, 1657. <note>original: Apologie du Grand Œuvre, ou Elixir des Philosophes</note>
**Adventures of the Unknown Philosopher in the search for and discovery of the Philosopher's Stone, divided into four books; in the last of which the method of making it is spoken of so clearly, that it has never been discussed with such candor**. 12mo. Paris, 1646. (*Attributed to the celebrated Abbé Belin.*) <note>original: Avantures du Philosophe Inconnu... Abbé Bebris</note>

# ALPHABETICAL CATALOGUE.

**Manget (J. J.)**—*Bibliotheca Chemica Curiosa*. 2 volumes. Folio. It contains 133 alchemical tracts, of which 33 are reprinted from *Theatrum Chemicum*, namely: Arisleus, Artephius, Aristotle, Avicenna, Arnold, Altus (*Liber Mutus*), Angenelli, Albinus, Bacon, Becher, Blawenstein, Borrichius, Brachesky, Bono, Bernard, Basil, Bernaud, Balduinus, Cato, Clauder, Chortalasseus, Cuoffelius, Dornea, Dastin, Espagnet, Faber, Fanianus, Ficinus, Friben, Geber, Gerard, Guido, Hermes, Hogheland, Helvetius, Icon, Johnson, Kalid, Kircher, Kuigman, Libavius, Lewis, Lully, Massa Solis, Merlin, Morhoff, Morien, Malvisius, F. Mirandola, T. Norton, Orthelius, Paracelsus, Philalethes, Pantaleon, Ripley, Richard, Rupescissa, Sachs, Sendivogius, Stoleius, Todenfeld, Zadith, Zacharia. # ALPHABETICAL CATALOGUE

->**P**<-

*Palladium Spagiricum* <note>original: "Palladium Spagiricum"; a "Palladium" refers to a safeguard or essential treatise; "Spagyric" refers to the alchemical art of separating and recombining plant or mineral components</note>. Octavo. Paris, 1624.

Palmarius, Peter (Doctor of Medicine, Paris)—*The Philosopher's Stone* <note>original: "Lapis Philosophicus"</note>. Octavo. Paris, 1609.

Pamphilus, Lucidas—*A Brief and Pleasant Spectacle of the Alchemical-Medical Theater* <note>original: "Theatri Alchymistico-Medici breve et jucundum spectaculum"</note>. Octavo. 1681.

Pansa, Martin—*The Golden Booklet* <note>original: "Libellus Aureus"</note>. Octavo. Leipzig, 1615.

Pantaleon—*The Metallic Two-Leafed Book* <note>original: "Bifolium Metallicum"</note>. Octavo. Nuremberg, 1676.

Pantheus, J. A.—A Venetian Priest, *On the Art and Theory of Metallic Transmutation* <note>original: "de Arte et Theoria Transmutationis Metallicis"</note>. Octavo. Paris, 1550.

Paracelsus, Theophrastus—*Complete Works* <note>original: "Opera Omnia"</note>. 3 volumes. Folio. Geneva, 1662.
—— *A Compendium of his Life and a Catalogue*. Basel, 1568.
—— *The Love of Fire regarding Vexation* <note>original: "Pyrophilia Vexationem"; likely referring to the "vexations" or chemical trials of the alchemist</note>. Octavo. Basel, 1568.
—— *Seven Books on the Degrees of Great Philosophy* <note>original: "Septem Libri de Gradibus Philosophiæ Magnæ"</note>.
—— *On Tartar* <note>original: "De Tartaro"; referring to the stony deposits in the body or in wine casks, a central concept in Paracelsian medicine</note>. Octavo. Basel, 1570.
—— *Ten Books of the Archidoxes* <note>original: "Archidoxorum, lib. x."; the "Archidoxes" were Paracelsus's primary teachings on the "primary doctrines" of medicine and alchemy</note>. Octavo. Cologne, 1570.
—— *The Aurora* <note>original: "Aurora"; meaning "The Dawn"</note>. Octavo. Basel, 1577.
—— *Key of Philosophy*. Octavo. London, 1580.
—— *On the Mercuries of Metals* <note>original: "De Mercuriis Metallorum"</note>. Octavo. Cologne, 1582.
—— *Medical-Chemical-Surgical Works* <note>original: "Medico-Chimico Chirurgica"</note>. 12 tomes. Quarto. Frankfurt, 1603.
—— *Sympathy*. Octavo. London, 1656.
—— *Chemical Transmutation*. Octavo. London, 1657.
—— *Philosophy to the Athenians*. Duodecimo. London, 1657.
—— *Prescription of 114 Cures*. Quarto. 1559.
—— *Archidoxis of Arcana and Elixirs*. Octavo. London, 1663.
—— *Adept Philosophy* <note>original: "Philosophiæ Adeptæ"</note>. Octavo. Basel.

Parkhurst's *Sympathetic Mummy*. London, 1653. <note>Refers to "mummy" as a medicinal substance or the "mumia" of the body used in sympathetic magic</note>

Patricius, Franciscus—*The Philosophical Magic of Zoroaster* <note>original: "Magia Philos. Zoroastris"</note>. Octavo. Hamburg, 1593.

Partridge, John—*Treasury of Secrets*. Octavo. London, 1591.

Paysan—*The Little Peasant* <note>original: "Le Petit Paysan"</note>. Alchemical. (*German, rare.*) Octavo. Strasbourg, 1619.

Pelagius the Greek—*Commentary on Democritus of Abdera, regarding the Sacred Art, or concerning Mystic and Natural Matters* <note>original: "In Democritum Abderitam, de Arte Sacra, sive de Rebus Mysticis, et Naturalibus comment."</note>. Octavo. Cologne, 1574.

Pelletier—*The Alkahest of Helmont* <note>original: "L'Alcæst de Helmont"; the Alkahest was the hypothetical "universal solvent" sought by alchemists</note>. (*Highly regarded.*) Duodecimo. Rouen, 1704.

Penotus, Bernard—*Alchemist's Enchiridion* <note>An "Enchiridion" is a handbook or manual</note>. Duodecimo. London, 1692.
—— *On the Matter of the Philosopher's Stone* <note>original: "De Materia Lapidis Philosophorum"</note>. Octavo. Bern, 1608.

Persons, David—*Salamandra* <note>The salamander was a symbol for the fire-resistant qualities of the Philosopher's Stone</note>. Quarto. London, 1636.

Petreus, Heinrich—*Hermetic Nosology* <note>original: "Nosologia Hermetica"; "Nosology" is the branch of medicine that deals with the classification of diseases</note>. Quarto. Marburg, 1614.
