<meta>This page begins an appendix providing historical context and supplementary details regarding the lives and reputations of specific alchemists and occultists mentioned in the preceding text.</meta>

# APPENDIX.

## I.

The life of Denis Zachaire has been made the subject of an interesting and well-written novel—*A Professor of Alchemy*—by "Percy Ross," recently published by Mr. George Redway. The life of the great adept, after his accomplishment of the **Great Work** <note>original: *Magnum Opus*; the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone</note>, is detailed at some length, with M. Louis Figuier apparently being the authority for the basic facts of the case. The alchemist is described by the French writer as having traveled to Lausanne, where he fell in love with a young and beautiful lady. He allegedly took her from Switzerland into Germany and then abandoned himself completely to a life of wastefulness and folly, which ended tragically at Cologne in the year 1556. He was strangled in the middle of a drunken sleep by the cousin who had accompanied him on his travels, and who desired his wealth and his mistress. The murderer escaped with the lady, who appears to have been his accomplice. The sole authority for this story appears to be a poem by Mardoché de Delle, who served as a kind of poet laureate to the court of Rudolph II. It is quite likely a mere invention of the poet; there is nothing in the serious and restrained treatise written by Denis Zachaire during that period to support the account of his supposed extravagance.

## II.

The manuscript volume entitled "Egyptian Freemasonry" fell, along with the other papers of Cagliostro <note>Count Alessandro di Cagliostro (1743–1795), a famous Italian occultist and high-society magician</note>, into the hands of the Inquisition. It was solemnly condemned in their judgment as containing rites, propositions, a doctrine, and a system which opened a broad road to rebellion and were calculated to destroy the Christian religion. The book was characterized as superstitious, blasphemous, impious, and heretical. It was publicly burned by the hands of the executioner, along with the instruments belonging to the

<summary>This appendix discusses the fictionalized accounts of the alchemist Denis Zachaire's death and the Inquisition's condemnation and burning of Cagliostro's "Egyptian Freemasonry" manuscript.</summary>
<keywords>Denis Zachaire, Cagliostro, Egyptian Freemasonry, Inquisition, Magnum Opus, alchemy in literature, Rudolph II</keywords>

--- Page 312 ---
<meta>This page continues the appendix regarding Alessandro Cagliostro, specifically detailing the nature and rituals of his "Egyptian Masonry" as recorded in his biography published by the Apostolic Chamber.</meta>

# APPENDIX.

Some valuable details concerning it are, however, preserved in the Italian biography; they are reproduced from the original proceedings published at Rome by order of the Apostolic Chamber <note>The administrative council of the Holy See, which oversaw the legal proceedings against Cagliostro</note>.

“It may be necessary to enter into some details concerning Egyptian Masonry. We shall draw our facts from a book compiled by himself, and now in our possession, by which he admits he was always guided in the exercise of his functions, and from which those regulations and instructions were copied with which he enriched many mother lodges. In this treatise, which is written in French, he promises to lead his disciples to perfection by means of physical and moral regeneration, to confer perpetual youth and beauty on them, and restore them to that state of innocence which they were deprived of by means of original sin. He asserts that Egyptian Masonry was first spread by Enoch and Elias, but that since that time it has lost much of its purity and splendor. Common masonry, according to him, has degenerated into mere buffoonery, and women have of late been entirely excluded from its mysteries; but the time had now arrived when the Grand Copt <note>The title assumed by Cagliostro as the supreme leader of his Masonic rite</note> was about to restore the glory of masonry, and allow its benefits to be shared by both sexes.

“The statutes of the order then follow in sequence: the division of the members into three distinct classes, the various signs by which they might recognize each other, the officers who are to preside over and regulate the society, the scheduled times when the members are to assemble, the establishment of a tribunal for deciding all differences that may arise between the various lodges or the individual members of each, and the various ceremonies which ought to take place at the admission of the candidates. In every part of this book the pious reader is disgusted with the sacrilege, the profanity, the superstition, and the idolatry with which it abounds—the invocations in the name of God, the prostrations, the adoration paid to the Grand Master, the fumigations, the incense, the exorcisms, the emblems of the Divine Triad, of the moon, of the sun, of the compass, of the square, and a thousand other scandalous details, with which the world is at present well acquainted.

“The Grand Copt, or chief of the lodge, is compared to God the Father. He is invoked upon every occasion; he regulates all the actions of the members and all the ceremonies of the lodge,

<summary>This page outlines the foundational principles and structure of Cagliostro’s "Egyptian Masonry," including its claims of spiritual and physical regeneration and its inclusion of women. The text also describes the rituals and organization of the order as recorded by the Roman Inquisition, which viewed them as sacrilegious and superstitious.</summary>
<keywords>Egyptian Masonry, Grand Copt, Cagliostro, physical regeneration, original sin, Enoch, Elias, Masonic rituals, Apostolic Chamber, occultism</keywords>

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Alessandro Cagliostro’s "Egyptian Masonry," detailing the specific rituals, admission requirements for men and women, and the alleged occult origins of the order’s sacred words.</meta>

# APPENDIX.

and he is even supposed to communicate with angels and with the Divinity. During the performance of many of the rites, they are asked to repeat the *Veni* and the *Te Deum*—indeed, they are ordered to such an excess of impiety that, in reciting the psalm *Memento Domine David*, the name of the Grand Master is always to be substituted for that of the King of Israel.

> “People of all religions are admitted into the society of Egyptian Masonry—Jews, Calvinists, and Lutherans are to be received into it as well as Catholics—provided they believe in the existence of God and the immortality of the soul, and have been previously allowed to participate in the mysteries of common masonry. When men are admitted, they receive a pair of garters from the Grand Copt <note>Cagliostro's title as the leader of the rite</note> for their mistresses, as is usual in all lodges; and when women are received into the society, they are presented by the Grand Mistress with a cockade, which they are asked to give to the man to whom they are most attached.

> “We shall here recount the ceremonies used when admitting a woman.

> “Once the candidate has presented herself, the Grand Mistress (Madame Cagliostro generally presided in that capacity) breathes upon her face from the forehead to the chin and then says, ‘I breathe upon you specifically to inspire you with the virtues we possess, so that they may take root and flourish in your heart. I thus fortify your soul; I thus confirm you in the faith of your brothers and sisters, according to the engagements you have made with them. We now admit you as a daughter of the Egyptian lodge. We order that you be acknowledged in that capacity by all the brothers and sisters of the Egyptian lodges, and that you enjoy the same privileges with them as you do with us.’

> “The Grand Master addresses the male candidate in this way: ‘By virtue of the power I have received from the Grand Copt, the founder of our order, and by the particular grace of God, I hereby confer upon you the honor of being admitted into our lodge in the name of *Helios*, *Mene*, and *Tetragrammaton*.’

> “In a book said to have been printed in Paris in 1789, it is asserted that these last words were suggested to Cagliostro as sacred and cabalistic expressions by a self-proclaimed conjurer. This man claimed he was assisted by a spirit, and that this spirit was none other than the soul of a cabalistic Jew who, through the magical arts, had murdered his own father before the incarnation of Jesus Christ.

<summary>This page describes the initiation rituals of Egyptian Masonry, including the inclusion of various religious sects and the specific ceremonies for male and female candidates led by Cagliostro and his wife.</summary>

# APPENDIX.

> “Common masons have been accustomed to regard St. John as their patron and to celebrate the festival of that saint. Cagliostro also adopted him as his protector, and it is quite remarkable that he was imprisoned at Rome on the very festival of his patron. The reason for his veneration of this great prophet was, if we are to believe him, the great similarity between the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> and the rites of his institution.
> 
> “We must observe here that when any of his disciples were admitted into the highest class, the following execrable ceremony took place. A young boy or girl, in a state of virgin innocence and purity, was procured; they were called the pupil, and power was given to them over the seven spirits that surround the throne of the divinity and preside over the seven planets. Their names, according to Cagliostro’s book, are Anaël, Michaël, Raphaël, Gabriel, Uriel, Zobiachel, and Anachiel. The pupil is then used as an intermediate agent between the spiritual and physical worlds, and being clothed in a long white robe, adorned with a red ribbon and blue silk festoons, they are shut up in a little closet. From that place, the pupil gives responses to the Grand Master and tells whether the spirits and Moses have agreed to receive the candidates into the highest class of Egyptian masons. . . .
> 
> “In his instructions for obtaining the moral and physical regeneration which he had promised to his disciples, he is exceedingly careful to give a minute description of the operations to which they are to submit. Those who desire to experience moral regeneration are to retire from the world for the space of forty days and divide their time into certain proportions. Six hours are to be employed in reflection, three in prayer to the Deity, and nine in the holy operations of Egyptian Masonry, while the remaining period is to be dedicated to rest. At the end of thirty-three days, a visible communication is to take place between the patient and the seven primitive spirits, and on the morning of the fortieth day, his soul will be inspired with divine knowledge, and his body will be as pure as that of a new-born infant.
> 
> “To achieve a physical regeneration, the patient is to retire to the country in the month of May, and during forty days is to live according to the most strict and austere rules, eating very little—and then only laxative and healing herbs—and using no other drink than distilled water or rain that has fallen in the course of the month. On the seventeenth day, after being bled, certain white drops are to be taken, six at night and

> "...six in the morning, increasing them by two each day in progression. In three more days, a small quantity of blood is again to be let from the arm before sunrise, and the patient is to remain in bed until the operation is completed. A grain of the *panacea* is then to be taken; this panacea is the same as that from which God created man when He first made him immortal. When this is swallowed, the candidate loses his speech and his ability to reflect for three entire days, and he is subject to frequent convulsions, struggles, and bouts of sweating. Having recovered from this state—in which, however, he experiences no pain at all—on the thirty-sixth day, he takes the third and last grain of the panacea, which causes him to fall into a profound and tranquil sleep. It is then that he loses his hair, his skin, and his teeth. These are all reproduced in a few hours, and having become a new man, on the morning of the fortieth day he leaves his room, enjoying a complete rejuvenation, by which he is enabled to live 5,557 years, or until such time as he, of his own accord, may desire to go to the world of spirits.”

### CONCERNING THE LODGE OF FREEMASONS DISCOVERED AT ROME.

The final chapter of the Italian biography of Cagliostro, which appeared before the death of its subject, contains a curious and interesting account under the above title. The lodge was situated in the district of the city called the Holy Trinity of the Mountain. It was visited on the night of Cagliostro’s capture, but the members had evidently been forewarned; they had taken precautions for their personal safety and had removed the symbols of their craft and the greater part of their books and papers, which perhaps, says the writer, contained secrets of great importance. The Inquisition claims to have a true insight, notwithstanding, into the origin, establishment, and other particulars of this lodge, drawn in part from the depositions of “a multitude of well-informed persons.”

The founders were seven in number: five Frenchmen, an American, and a Pole, all of whom had been previously initiated into other lodges. It assumed the title of the Lodge of the Reunion of True Friends, and the first meeting took place on November 1, 1787. Converts were immediately made, and included candidates who had not been admitted into any other...

<summary>This page details the final stages of a forty-day occult "regeneration" ritual involving a panacea that supposedly grants extreme longevity, followed by an account of a Masonic lodge in Rome raided by the Inquisition.</summary>
<keywords>Cagliostro, panacea, physical regeneration, rejuvenation, Freemasonry, Rome, Inquisition, Holy Trinity of the Mountain, Lodge of the Reunion of True Friends</keywords>

--- Page 316 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Masonic lodge discovered in Rome, detailing its administrative connection to the Mother Lodge in Paris and describing its internal hierarchy and ritual spaces.</meta>

# APPENDIX.

society. Its numbers rapidly increased, and to establish it with all the necessary formalities, approval was obtained from the Mother Lodge in Paris, and a deputy was sent to reside in that city as its representative. Its letters were carried by special messengers. Mention is made in the register of archives kept under three locks, in which the statutes, mysteries, and symbols transmitted from Paris were preserved, along with all the most interesting speeches delivered within the lodge. However, it is claimed that the Egyptian lodge was, in this instance, devoid of special characteristics. The list of its officers was as follows:

1. The Venerable, or Grand Master.
2. The Superintendent, or Deputy Grand Master.
3. The Terrible.
4. The Master of the Ceremonies.
5. The Treasurer.
6. The Almoner.
7. The Secretary.
8. The Orator, or Export Broker.

The entire Lodge was composed of two chambers or halls. The first was called the Chamber of Reflections. A death's head was placed on a table, and above it were two inscriptions in French which held an arcane significance. The second apartment was called the Temple; it was decorated according to the various rites performed in it. On all occasions, it was provided with a throne on which the Venerable constantly sat. Various Masonic emblems adorned the walls—among them were the sun, moon, and planets. On the two sides of the throne, several magnificent pillars were placed, and opposite to these, the brotherhood was arranged in order. Each member wore his leather apron and a black ribbon in the form of a deacon's stole around his neck, while in his hands, which were covered with a pair of white gloves, he brandished a bare sword, a hammer, or a compass, according to the different formalities prescribed by the institution.

The Inquisition does not seem to have been familiar with the secret signs and passports.

## THE REAL HISTORY OF THE ROSICRUCIANS.

Founded on their own Manifestoes, and on Facts and Documents collected from the Writings of Initiated Brethren. By ARTHUR EDWARD WAITE.

> “We desire to speak of Mr. Waite's work with the greatest respect regarding its honesty, impartiality, and sound scholarship. Mr. Waite has given, for the first time, the documents with which Rosicrucianism has been connected *in extenso* <note>In full; at full length</note>.”—*Literary World*.

## MAGIC, WHITE AND BLACK; or, The Science of 

Finite and Infinite Life, containing Practical Hints for Students of Occultism. By FRANZ HARTMANN, M.D.

> “Dr. Hartmann's ‘Magic,’ as compared with ‘Light on the Path,’ is a bulky volume; and in its closely-printed pages students of occultism will find hints, ‘practical’ and otherwise, likely to be of great service to them in the pursuit of their studies and researches. It was not the author's object, in composing this book, to write merely a code of ethics, and thereby to increase the already existing enormous mountain of unread moral precepts, but to assist the student of occultism in studying the elements of which his own soul is composed, and to learn to know his own physical organism. I want to give an impulse to the study of a science which may be called the ‘anatomy and physiology of the soul,’ which investigates the elements of which the soul is composed, and the source from which man’s desires and emotions spring. Dr. Hartmann’s compendium is ‘an attempt to show the way man may become a cooperator with the Divine Power, whose product is Nature,’ and his pages, as described by himself, ‘constitute a book which may properly have the title of “Magic,” for if the readers succeed in practically following its teaching, they will be able to perform the greatest of all magical feats, the spiritual regeneration of Man.’ Dr. Hartmann’s book has also gone into a third edition, and has developed from an insignificant pamphlet, ‘written originally for the purpose of demonstrating to a few inexperienced inquirers that the study of the occult side of nature was not identical with the vile practices of sorcery,’ into a comprehensive volume, comprising, we are willing to believe, the entire philosophic system of occultism. There are abundant evidences that the science of theosophy has made vast strides in public estimation of late years, and that those desirous of experimenting in this particular and in many respects fascinating branch of ethics, have leaders whose teaching they can follow with satisfaction to themselves.”—*Saturday Review*.

->GEORGE REDWAY, YORK STREET, COVENT GARDEN.<-

# POSTHUMOUS HUMANITY; A Study of Phantoms.

->By ADOLPHE D’ASSIER, Member of the Bordeaux Academy of Sciences. Translated and Annotated by HENRY S. OLCOTT, President of the Theosophical Society. To which is added an Appendix showing the Popular Beliefs current in India respecting the Post-mortem Vicissitudes of the Human Entity.<-

> Truth says—"If you care for ghost stories, duly accredited, excellently told, and scientifically explained, you should read the translation by Colonel Olcott of M. Adolphe d’Assier’s ‘Posthumous Humanity,’ a study of phantoms. There is no dogmatism so dogged and offensive as that of the professed skeptic—of the scientific skeptic especially—who *ex vi termini* <note>by the very definition of the term</note> ought to keep the doors of his mind hospitably open; and it is refreshing, therefore, to find such scientists as Wallace, Crookes, and M. d’Assier, who is a Positivist, in the ranks of the Psychical Research host. For my own part, though I have attended the seance of a celebrated London medium, and there convinced myself beyond all doubt of his imposture, I no more think that the detection of a medium's fraud disposes of the whole question of ghosts, etc., than that the detection of an atheist priest disposes of the whole question of Christianity. Whatever view you take of this controversy, however, I can promise you that you will find the book interesting at least, if not convincing."

# LIGHT ON THE PATH.
### A Treatise written for the

->Personal Use of those who are ignorant of the Eastern Wisdom, and who desire to enter within its influence. Written down by M. C., Fellow of the Theosophical Society. New Edition, with Notes, by the Author.<-

> "So far as we can gather from the mystic language in which it is expressed, ‘Light on the Path’ is intended to guide the footsteps of those who have discarded the forms of religion while retaining the moral principle to its fullest extent. It is in harmony with much that was said by Socrates and Plato, although the author does not use the terminology of those philosophers, but rather the language of Buddhism, easily understood by esoteric Buddhists, but difficult to grasp by those outside the circle. ‘Light on the Path’ may, we think, be said to be the only attempt in this language and in this century to put practical occultism into words; and it may be added, by way of further explanation, that the character of Gautama Buddha, as shown in Sir Edwin Arnold’s ‘Light of Asia,’ is the perfect type of the being who has reached the threshold of Divinity by this road. That it has reached a third edition speaks favorably for this *multum in parvo* <note>much in little; a great deal in a small space</note> of the science of occultism; and ‘M. C.’ may be expected to gather fresh laurels in the future."—*Saturday Review.*

---

->GEORGE REDWAY, YORK STREET, COVENT GARDEN.<-

## A PROFESSOR OF ALCHEMY (Denis Zachaire).

> "Sure of a special circle of readers with congenial tastes."—*Graphic.*
> "This is a story of love—of deep, undying, refining love—not without suggestions of Faust. The figure of Berengaria, his wife, is a noble and touching one, and her purity and sweetness stand out in beautiful relief from the gloom of the alchemist's laboratory and the horrors of the terrible Inquisition into whose hands she falls. The romance of the crucible, however, is not all permeated by sulfurous vapors and tinged with tartarean <note>pertaining to the deepest part of Hades; hellish</note> smoke. There is often a highly dramatic element."—*Glasgow Herald.*

->*Demy 8vo <note>A book size, approximately 5.5 by 8.75 inches</note>, beveled cloth, gilt, price 10s. 6d.*<-

## THE MYSTERIES OF MAGIC: A Digest of the Writings of Eliphas Lévi.

->With Biographical and Critical Essay, by ARTHUR EDWARD WAITE.<-

> *The Morning Post* says:—"Of the many remarkable men who have gained notoriety by their proficiency, real or imaginary, in the Black Arts, probably none presents a more strange and irreconcilable character than the French magician, Alphonse Louis Constant. Better known under the Jewish pseudonym of Eliphas Lévi Zahed, this enthusiastic student of forbidden art made some stir in France, and even in London, and was frequently consulted by those who were inclined to place some credit in his reputation as a magician. His works on magic are those of an undoubted genius, and reveal a philosophy beautiful in conception even if totally opposed to common-sense principles. There is so great a fund of learning and of attractive reasoning in these writings that Mr. Arthur Waite has published a digest of them for the benefit of English readers. This gentleman has not attempted a literal translation in every case, but has arranged a volume which, while reproducing with sufficient accuracy a great portion of the more interesting works, affords an excellent idea of the scope of the entire literary remains of an enthusiast for whom he entertains a profound admiration.
> "With regard to the contents of the present volume, there is nothing in it very suggestive of sulfur. No apprehension need be felt if the book be left about the house that the adventurous members of the family circle will commence incantations in mystic robes with the aid of Abracadabra, the Pentagram, and incense. In fact, Eliphas over and over again opposes amateur attempts at magical practices. The reader may, however, with profit peruse carefully the learned dissertations written by Mr. Constant upon the Hermetic art treated as a religion, a philosophy, and a natural science. As a religion, Eliphas holds it to be that of the ancient Magi and the initiates of all ages; as a philosophy, its principles are traced in the Alexandrian school and in the theories of Pythagoras; as a science, he indicates the methods to be ascertained from Paracelsus, Nicholas Flamel, and Raymond Lully. In view of the remarkable exhibitions of mesmeric influence and thought-reading which have recently been given, it is not improbable that the thoughtful reader may find a clue in the writings of this cultured and amiable magician to the secret of many of the manifestations of witchcraft that formerly struck wonder and terror into the hearts of simple people eager to behold and ready to believe in supernatural powers."

->GEORGE REDWAY, YORK STREET, COVENT GARDEN.<-

# FORTUNE-TELLING CARDS.—THE TAROT;
### Its Occult Meaning, Use in Fortune-Telling, and Method of Play, etc.
## By S. L. MACGREGOR MATHERS.

> "The designs of the twenty-one trump cards are extremely unique; to give some idea of the manner in which Mr. Mathers uses them in fortune-telling, it is necessary to mention them in detail, along with the general meaning he assigns to each of them. The aspiring fortune-teller may then draw their own specific conclusions, and they will find considerable freedom for framing them according to their own preferences. It should also be mentioned that each of the cards, when reversed, conveys a meaning opposite to its primary significance. No. 1 is the Juggler or Bateleur, also called Pagad; this latter name is cited by Count de Gebelin as proof of the Tarot's Oriental origin, as it is derived from PAG, meaning chief or master, and GAD, meaning fortune. The Juggler symbolizes Will. 2. The High Priestess, or female Pope, represents Science, Wisdom, or Knowledge. 3. The Empress is the symbol of Action or Initiative. 4. The Emperor represents Realization or Development. 5. The Hierophant or Pope is the symbol of Mercy and Beneficence. 6. The Lovers signify wise choices and trials overcome. 7. The Chariot represents Triumph, or victory over obstacles. 8. Themis or Justice symbolizes balance and justice. 9. The Hermit denotes prudence. 10. The Wheel of Fortune represents luck, whether good or bad. 11. Fortitude symbolizes power or might. 12. The Hanged Man—a man suspended head-downward by one leg—means devotion and self-sacrifice. 13. Death signifies transformation or change. 14. Temperance typifies combination. 15. The Devil is the image of fate or fatality. 16. The Lightning-struck Tower, also called the House of God (Maison-Dieu), shows ruin and disruption. 17. The Star is the emblem of hope. 18. The Moon symbolizes twilight, deception, and error. 19. The Sun signifies earthly happiness. 20. The Last Judgment means renewal or the resolution of a matter. 21. The Universe represents completion and reward. 0. The Foolish Man signifies atonement or wavering. Separate meanings, along with their respective opposites, are also assigned to each of the other cards in the pack, so that when they have been dealt and arranged in any of the combinations recommended by the author for divination, the seeker only needs to use this small volume as a dictionary to read their fate."—*Saturday Review*.

->GEORGE REDWAY, YORK STREET, COVENT GARDEN.<-

### A. E. WAITE
<note>Arthur Edward Waite (1857–1942) was a prolific British scholarly author on occult and esoteric matters.</note>
</insert>

<term>Alchemy</term>: An ancient branch of natural philosophy and an early form of chemistry, focused on the transmutation of matter, particularly the turning of base metals into gold and the discovery of a universal cure for disease.
<term>Mysticism</term>: The belief that union with or absorption into the Deity or the absolute, or the spiritual apprehension of knowledge inaccessible to the intellect, may be attained through contemplation and self-surrender.
<term>Alchemists</term>: Practitioners of alchemy who sought the Philosopher's Stone and the Elixir of Life.
<term>Arthur Edward Waite</term>: A prominent member of the Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn and a key figure in the study of Western Hermeticism.

## FORTUNE-TELLING CARDS.—THE TAROT: Its
## Occult Significance, Use in Fortune-Telling, and Method of Play, etc.
### By S. L. MACGREGOR MATHERS.

> "The designs of the twenty-one trump cards are extremely singular. In order to give some idea of the manner in which Mr. Mathers uses them in fortune-telling, it is necessary to mention them in detail, together with the general meaning which he attaches to each of them. The aspiring fortune-teller <note>original: "cartomancer"</note> may then draw his own particular conclusions, and he will find considerable room for interpreting them according to his own preferences. It should further be mentioned that each of the cards, when reversed, conveys a meaning opposite to its primary significance. 
>
> 1. **The Juggler** <note>original: "Bateleur"</note>, also called **Pagad**; the latter name is cited by Count de Gebelin as proof of the Oriental origin of Tarots, being derived from *PAG* (chief or master) and *GAD* (fortune). The Juggler symbolizes Will. 
> 2. **The High Priestess**, or female Pope, represents Science, Wisdom, or Knowledge. 
> 3. **The Empress** is the symbol of Action or Initiative. 
> 4. **The Emperor** represents Realization or Development. 
> 5. **The Hierophant** or Pope is the symbol of Mercy and Beneficence. 
> 6. **The Lovers** signify Wise Disposition and Trials surmounted. 
> 7. **The Chariot** represents Triumph and Victory over Obstacles. 
> 8. **Themis** or **Justice** symbolizes Equilibrium and Justice. 
> 9. **The Hermit** denotes Prudence. 
> 10. **The Wheel of Fortune** represents Fortune, whether good or bad. 
> 11. **Fortitude** symbolizes Power or Might. 
> 12. **The Hanged Man**—a man suspended head-downwards by one leg—means Devotion and Self-Sacrifice. 
> 13. **Death** signifies Transformation or Change. 
> 14. **Temperance** typifies Combination. 
> 15. **The Devil** is the image of Fate or Fatality. 
> 16. **The Lightning-struck Tower**, also called the House of God <note>original: "Maison-Dieu"</note>, shows Ruin and Disruption. 
> 17. **The Star** is the emblem of Hope. 
> 18. **The Moon** symbolizes Twilight, Deception, and Error. 
> 19. **The Sun** signifies Earthly Happiness. 
> 20. **The Last Judgment** means Renewal and the Determination of a matter. 
> 21. **The Universe** represents Completion and Reward. 
> 0. **The Foolish Man** signifies Expiation or Wavering. 
>
> Separate meanings, along with their respective opposites, are also attached to each of the other cards in the pack. Thus, when they have been dealt out and arranged in any of the combinations recommended by the author for the purposes of divination, the inquirer has only to use this little volume as a dictionary in order to read his fate."—*Saturday Review*.

<meta>This is the title page and introductory text for S. L. MacGregor Mathers' 1888 treatise on the Tarot.</meta>

<header>These cards are usually known by the name of Tarots. It is divided into</header>

# FORTUNE-TELLING CARDS — THE TAROT;

## Its Occult Significance, Use in Fortune-Telling, and
## Method of Play,
## BY S. L. MACGREGOR MATHERS.
## Author of "The Key of Solomon the King," <note>A famous 14th or 15th-century grimoire (book of magic) attributed to King Solomon, which Mathers translated and edited.</note> etc.

The pack of cards known by the name of the Tarot is composed of seventy-eight cards, which are divided into two groups: the first consisting of twenty-two cards, which are called the Major Arcana <note>original: "Arcana" — meaning "mysteries" or "secrets."</note>; and the second consisting of fifty-six cards, which are called the Minor Arcana. The Major Arcana are also called the trumps, and they are numbered from one to twenty-one, with the twenty-second card being the Zero, or the Fool. The Minor Arcana are divided into four suits, which are called Scepters <note>modern: Wands</note>, Cups, Swords, and Pentacles <note>modern: Coins or Disks</note>. Each suit contains ten pip cards <note>term: "pip cards" — the numbered cards from Ace to ten.</note>, from one to ten, and four court cards, which are called the King, the Queen, the Knight, and the Page.

---

->LONDON: GEORGE REDWAY, YORK STREET, COVENT GARDEN.<-
->1888.<-

<meta>Handwritten letter "R" at the bottom right corner of the folio.</meta>

<summary>This title page introduces S. L. MacGregor Mathers' influential 1888 work on the Tarot, defining the structure of the 78-card deck into Major and Minor Arcana. It establishes the basic terminology for the suits and court cards used in occult divination.</summary>
